The golden light of a Virginia afternoon draped the rolling hills of the estate in warmth as young Harry Warrington rode up the long drive to his family’s home. The journey had been long, the sea unforgiving, and England—though grand—had not been kind. Now, the sight of the familiar red-brick house, its white columns standing tall against the blue sky, filled him with a relief so profound it near choked him.

The servants rushed out before his horse had fully halted, their faces alight with recognition and joy. "Master Harry! Master Harry's home!" The cry spread like wildfire, and soon the porch was crowded with well-wishers, old and young. Even the hounds, who had drowsed in the shade, sprang up barking, tails wagging furiously as if they, too, remembered him.

His brother George was not among them.

Harry dismounted, his boots hitting the dust of the drive with a thud that felt heavier than it ought. He had written ahead—had George not received the letter? Or had he chosen not to meet him? The thought stung, though he brushed it aside. There would be time enough for reunions and explanations. For now, he allowed himself to be swept inside, where the scent of beeswax and aged wood welcomed him like an old friend.

His mother stood in the hall, her back straight, her expression unreadable. For a heartbeat, neither moved. Then her arms opened, and Harry stepped into them, the years of absence dissolving in that embrace.

"You’ve grown thin," she murmured, pulling back to study him. "England did not feed you well."

Harry laughed, though it came out rougher than he intended. "It fed me enough. But no dish there tastes like home."

She smiled then, a rare softening. "We’ll see to that."

But as the household buzzed around him, Harry’s gaze drifted to the staircase, to the closed door of the study where his brother often sequestered himself. The war had changed them both—perhaps in ways neither could yet name.

And so Harry Warrington returned, not as the boy who had left, but as a man carrying the weight of battles fought and debts unpaid. Home, he would soon learn, was not quite as he remembered it.

On the library wall of a celebrated American writer hang two crossed swords—relics of the War of Independence. One was drawn in loyal service to the Crown, the other wielded by a steadfast republican. Their owner, a man of letters, has earned honor in both lands, his genius welcomed wherever it roams.

This tale recalls those swords. It follows two brothers, sons of Virginia, who found themselves on opposing sides in the Revolution, yet reunited as kin when the guns fell silent. Their portraits still gaze from the wainscoted walls of the Warrington estate in England, their letters preserved by a mother’s devotion.

They called them *The Virginians*—these brothers who crossed the Atlantic, who walked the edges of the Old World now fading into memory. Their lives brushed against figures of history, their voices lingering in ink-stained pages: letters scribbled by campfires, tear-blotted notes from grand ballrooms, even one pierced by a bullet, its words smeared with blood.

Their mother, Madam Esmond, kept every scrap. From childish scrawls to the last farewell, she treasured them until her dying hour. She never returned to Europe after her youth, save once in the reign of George II. When war burned their Virginia home, she fled to Richmond, clinging to her pride and her name—never Warrington, always Esmond.

The letters are fragments, hints of lives lived boldly. The rest must be imagined: the laughter in crowded parlors, the hushed words before battle, the weight of a brother’s loss.

---

On a bright summer morning in 1756, the *Young Rachel* sailed up the Avon, returning from her annual voyage to the Potomac. At Bristol’s bustling wharf, her owner, Mr. Trail, greeted Captain Franks with a merchant’s sharp eye.

“Only one passenger?” Trail frowned. “The *Duke William* brought fourteen last week!”

“Aye, and this one paid nothing,” Franks chuckled, nodding to a young man in mourning.

The stranger—tall, dark-haired, barely nineteen—stepped ashore, his servant trailing with trunks. At the mention of his name, Trail’s demeanor shifted instantly.

“Warrington? *The* Madam Esmond Warrington’s son?” He bowed deeply. “Your family’s name is honored here, sir! Come, you must dine with me—”

But the young Virginian declined. His heart was set on a different destination.

---

Harry Warrington had dreamed of this journey. He and his brother George had pored over maps, tracing the path from Bristol to Bath, to Salisbury, to Winchester—finally to *home*, the Hampshire estate of their ancestors.

Now, alone, Harry followed that route. The English countryside unfurled before him, lush and golden. Post-chaises rattled past laden wagons; farmers tipped hats; village greens buzzed with life. It was all as his grandfather had described—yet richer, brighter, *real*.

At last, his carriage rolled into Castlewood Green. The inn bore the family crest—three castles on a swinging sign. Heart pounding, Harry walked toward the manor, its grey stone rising against the woods.

An old man sat by the gate, a dog at his feet.

“You’ve had a fine ride, Master Harry,” the man said, as if expecting him.

Harry stared. “You must be Lockwood.”

The old retainer’s eyes clouded, then cleared. “Aye, I served your grandfather. But you—you’re the *Colonel’s* grandson? From Virginia?”

“I am,” Harry said softly.

Lockwood’s granddaughter, Molly, gaped. “Lord, sir! You’re as white as any Englishman!”

Harry smiled. “May I see the house? Even if the family is away?”

Molly led him inside. Though he had never walked these halls, each step felt familiar—a homecoming long deferred.

---

Harry Warrington stood before the ancestral doors of Castlewood, his heart pounding with a mix of anticipation and unease. The great house loomed before him, its stones steeped in family history—his history. Yet when he rang the bell, no fanfare greeted him, no eager steward rushed to embrace a long-lost son of the line. Instead, a disinterested servant peered out, barely masking his impatience as Harry announced himself.

*"The family is away,"* the man muttered, already turning back to his interrupted card game. Only after persistent pleading was Harry permitted a fleeting glimpse of the portrait gallery—his grandfather’s stern visage in periwig and breastplate, his grandmother’s youthful beauty immortalized in paint. But the housekeeper shooed him out like an intruder, muttering of preparations for the Earl’s imminent arrival.

Disheartened, Harry left his name scrawled on a scrap of paper—*Henry Esmond Warrington, of Virginia*—and retreated to the village inn, where the landlord barely glanced up at his title. The contrast stung. In Virginia, any stranger bearing the Warrington name would have been welcomed with open arms. Here, he was just another face, another mouth to feed.

As evening fell, Harry lingered by the bridge, watching the rooks circle the turrets of Castlewood. Lost in thought, he barely registered the thunder of hooves until two riders nearly trampled him. A curse, a flash of powdered hair, and they were gone—his cousins, though he did not know it yet. The grand coach that followed, emblazoned with the Esmond crest, rolled past without pause. Behind its windows, pale faces peered out, indifferent.

That night, as Harry brooded in his inn chamber, the door burst open. A flushed, liquor-scented figure staggered in—*William Esmond*, his cousin, slurring apologies and insults in equal measure.

*"Beg pardon—thought you were a damned bailiff!"* William hiccuped, swaying. *"Aunt Bernstein insists you come. Won’t take no for an answer—never does."*

Harry’s pride flared. *"I won’t be summoned like a servant."*

William only laughed, calling for more punch before the inn’s staff dragged him out, still bellowing challenges.

---

The family debated Harry’s fate over wine and silver.

*"Must we really invite him?"* Lady Castlewood sighed. *"He’s a stranger, and from the colonies at that."*

*"But he’s family,"* protested Lady Maria, though her interest was less in kinship than in novelty.

Only the Baroness Bernstein, sharp-eyed and imperious, cut through the pretense. *"You shame yourselves,"* she snapped. *"A grandson of Henry Esmond, turned away like a beggar?"*

Yet beneath her outrage lay amusement. She knew their greed—how they fawned over her wealth, how even the rebellious Lord Castlewood weighed his words carefully when her purse was in question.

---

At first light, she strode through dew-laden gardens, her mind made up. When Harry arrived, summoned by her note, she studied him—his proud bearing, his grandfather’s fire in his eyes.

*"You’ll stay here,"* she declared, looping her arm through his. *"This is your home, whether they like it or not."*

Harry hesitated. *"After yesterday—"*

*"Yesterday is done,"* she interrupted. *"Today, you take your place."*

And with that, the game began anew.

At the close of Queen Anne’s reign, Colonel Henry Esmond—once a loyal officer of her army—found himself entangled in ill-fated schemes to restore the Stuart line to the British throne. The nation, however, had chosen differently, and those who opposed the new Hanoverian rule were compelled to seek refuge abroad. Though Colonel Esmond regretted his part in the affair, the Crown proved merciful, and his friends soon secured his pardon.

Yet England held little charm for him now. The political strife had wearied him, and private sorrows weighed heavy upon his heart. So it was that he turned his gaze westward, to Virginia, where a vast estate—granted to his ancestors by King Charles I—awaited him. There he settled, raising his daughter and later her twin sons beneath the wide Virginian sky. His wife, the widow of his ill-fated kinsman, Viscount Castlewood (slain in a duel at the close of King William’s reign), had died years before, leaving him to the quiet companionship of his books and his thoughts.

He named his new home *Castlewood*, after the family seat in Hampshire, and in doing so, carried more than just a name across the Atlantic. The customs, the loyalties, even the prejudices of the old country took root in Virginian soil. Here, English kings were toasted with fervor, the Church of England held sway, and the gentry prided themselves on their pure bloodlines, looking down upon the Dutch traders of New York or the stern Puritans of New England. It was a land of contradictions—staunchly royalist, yet destined to lead the rebellion against the very Crown it revered.

Life in Virginia unfolded in a manner almost feudal. The great estates were self-sufficient kingdoms, worked by scores of enslaved laborers and indentured servants. The rivers teemed with fish, the forests with game, and the fields yielded tobacco, the lifeblood of the colony’s wealth. Ships laden with the fragrant leaves sailed for London, returning with fine English goods—silks, china, and the latest volumes for Colonel Esmond’s ever-growing library.

Hospitality was boundless. No traveler was turned away from a gentleman’s door, and visits between neighbors were conducted with the ceremoniousness of old-world courts. As for slavery—that question, which would one day tear the nation asunder, was then scarcely a whisper. To own slaves was as natural as owning horses, and the whip, in the eyes of men like Madam Esmond, was as necessary for one as the bridle was for the other.

Colonel Esmond himself was a man of quiet melancholy. Some sorrow, never spoken of, had shadowed his later years. He was kind, even indulgent, to those around him—especially the women of his household, whom he seldom contradicted—but his spirit had long since withdrawn from the world’s tumult. When his daughter assumed control of the estate, he relinquished it without protest. Books and solitude contented him. Company he entertained with dry wit, but their departures did not grieve him.

“My dear,” he once remarked to his daughter, “I shall not be sorry to go myself, and you—though the most devoted of daughters—will find consolation soon enough. At my age, romance is absurd. You, however, may indulge in it, if you choose.”

This was said with a faint smile, for the lady in question—Madam Esmond, as she would later be known—was the least romantic of creatures. Proud of her lineage, she filled her father’s later years with schemes to elevate the family’s standing. When the old wooden house no longer suited her ambitions, she persuaded him to build a grander mansion, fit for the descendants of Norman lords and, as she firmly believed, English royalty.

Her pride in her ancestry was boundless. The Colonel, amused, humored her, even painting two ludicrous heraldic trees tracing the Esmonds back to Charlemagne on one side and Queen Boadicea on the other—the latter depicted in a scandalously “ancient” costume that would have shocked any true antiquarian.

Upon the Colonel’s death, Madam Esmond ruled Castlewood with an iron hand. She brooked no slight to her dignity, quarreling with governors and neighbors alike over precedence. The colonial aristocracy, in her eyes, were upstarts compared to the true-born Esmonds. Her sons, George and Harry, were raised to revere the Crown—little dreaming that one day they would take up arms against it.

The twins, though identical in face, could not have been more different in temper. George, the elder by minutes, was bookish and gentle, while Harry was all fire and mischief—a born soldier, drilling the plantation’s enslaved children with martial fervor. Their grandfather had doted on George, whose quiet humor matched his own, while Harry’s boisterousness won him fewer confidences.

Yet it was George who surprised them all. When his enslaved playmate was sentenced to a whipping, the boy erupted in furious defiance, vowing to free the child when he came of age—a outburst that left his mother aghast and his grandfather quietly thoughtful.

“You let mamma rule you in everything, grandpapa,” George accused tearfully.

The old Colonel only laughed. “So I do. I am an old man, my dear, and I find it easier to sit than to stand.”

Thus life passed at Castlewood—a world of books and hunting, of feudal pride and coming strife. And when Colonel Esmond was laid to rest, it was with a funeral befitting a lord, though he himself would have scoffed at the pomp. The monument, paid for by his English stepson, bore the Esmond arms and a flattering epitaph—one of the few lies in which he had no part.

The estate of Castlewood was a place of plenty—its halls filled with the laughter of dutiful servants, its lands yielding abundance, its name respected throughout Virginia. Madam Esmond Warrington presided over it all with the authority of a sovereign, her rule unquestioned by neighbors, her children obedient to her will. Yet, for all her blessings—her wealth, her station, her devout piety—there was a thorn in her contentment.

She had been a dutiful daughter, obedient to her parents’ wishes, even in marriage. But obedience had not tempered her spirit. She was a woman who demanded submission, and woe to those who resisted. Her father, the old Colonel, had watched her imperious nature with amusement, but now that he was gone, there was none left to check her.

The English Warringtons, her late husband’s family, found little favor in her eyes. She had married beneath her, she often reminded herself—a mere younger son of a Norfolk baronet. The Warringtons, in turn, thought little of this proud colonial widow who spoke of them with such disdain. Once, formal letters had passed between them, but when word reached England of her sharp tongue, the correspondence ceased.

Nor did her sons escape her dominion. George, her eldest, inherited his grandfather’s thoughtful nature, and this often set him at odds with her. When the matter of his inheritance arose—six thousand pounds left in trust, which she believed should be hers to command—George sided with the lawyers against her. She could not forgive him for it.

"You would see your brother left with nothing!" she cried. "While you hoard your fortune, must Harry beg for scraps?"

George, wounded, could only reply: "I have no power over the money, mother. Would that I did, if only to give you peace."

But peace was not in her nature. She took to saving fiercely for Harry, cutting luxuries, halting improvements, even regretting the grand carriage she had imported from England.

"Three hundred and eighty guineas!" she lamented. "Oh, Harry, if only I had that now for you!"

Harry, ever cheerful, laughed. "You’ll provide for me while you live, and George will after. I’ll not starve."

She turned a cold eye upon George. "Not unless he learns charity—which I pray for daily."

At this, George’s composure broke. "Would that you could bless me as well, mother!" he exclaimed, tears choking his voice.

Harry flung an arm around him. "Pay no mind, George. I know your heart."

But the wound festered.

---

It was in this uneasy household that Harry first met the lady who would reveal a long-buried truth.

Seated beneath a portrait of a radiant young woman—painted by Kneller in Queen Anne’s day—the stranger studied Harry with keen interest.

"Are you my cousin too?" Harry asked, sensing a kindness in her that his other relatives lacked.

She smiled faintly, gesturing to the painting above her. "Do you not recognize that face?"

Harry shook his head.

"It was mine once," she said softly. "I was Beatrix Esmond."

A silence fell.

"Your mother never spoke of me?"

"Never."

The lady’s lips tightened. "Then let this be your first lesson in family secrets, child. Your mother is my half-sister—and she has erased me from her history."

The tale Harry Warrington shared with his newfound kinswoman painted a picture of life at Castlewood—one that Madam Bernstein, with her sharp wit and worldly wisdom, surely dissected with private amusement. To a woman of her refined tastes, the rustic existence of her American sister seemed dreary indeed. Yet Rachel Warrington, content in her dominion, knew no greater ambition than the management of her estate. She was a woman of ceaseless industry, her days filled with the minutiae of plantation life: overseeing the kitchens, directing the spinning maids, riding out to inspect the fields, and doctoring ailing slaves with her formidable collection of remedies. Her neighbors, a mix of threadbare gentry and hospitable squires, lived in much the same manner—though few matched her thrift.

At her side stood Mrs. Mountain, the widow of an old regimental companion, who had crossed the Atlantic to serve as companion and confidante. Their friendship was a tempestuous one, marked by quarrels and swift reconciliations, yet bound by mutual loyalty. Mrs. Mountain brought laughter to the household, played cards with her mistress, and fended off the advances of eager suitors with a cheerful refusal. She had seen enough of marriage, she declared, and preferred the security of Castlewood to any uncertain future.

Madam Warrington, for her part, enjoyed the admiration of her neighbors, accepting their flattery with regal complacency. She was a woman who relished deference, and more than one hopeful gentleman found himself redirected toward Mrs. Mountain when his attentions grew too pointed. The widow’s pride in her lineage was unshakable; she would entertain no notion of remarriage, though she took pleasure in the game of courtship—much like the great Elizabeth herself.

Her sons, however, chafed under her whims. George, sharp-tongued and perceptive, saw through the sycophants who clustered around his mother, while Harry, ever the sportsman, joined their hunts and revels without a second thought. Their education, first under an indifferent tutor and then under the zealous Mr. Ward—a protégé of the fiery preacher Whitfield—became a battleground. George, disdainful of Ward’s ignorance and pomp, provoked him at every turn, while Harry, though more amiable, bristled at the man’s heavy-handed piety.

Matters came to a head when George’s defiance pushed his mother to reclaim her authority by force. Persuaded by Ward, she resolved to restore discipline with the rod—a common enough practice, but one that George, proud and resentful, could not abide. In a moment of cold fury, he shattered a cherished heirloom, his grandfather’s cup, declaring, *"You will never mend that again."* The act was a challenge, a defiance that could not be undone.

When Ward attempted to carry out the punishment, Harry intervened with a violence none had anticipated. Seizing a blade from the wall, he struck the tutor, bloodying his head and vowing, *"If one lifts a hand to me or my brother, I’ll have his life."*

The household stood divided—the mother aghast, the tutor wounded, and the sons united in rebellion. The echoes of that day’s strife would linger, for bonds had been strained, and the old order of Castlewood had been shaken to its core.

After the bitter departure of their chaplain, Madam Esmond and her elder son, George, reached an uneasy truce. Though neither spoke of their quarrel, the weight of it pressed upon the boy’s mind so heavily that he fell into a fever, his restless nights haunted by delirious cries of *“Broken! Broken! It can never be mended!”*—words that sent a chill through his mother’s heart as she watched over him.

Her remedies proved useless. Pride swallowed, she rode through the snow to the door of little Dr. Dempster, the very man she had once dismissed. *“I shall bury my resentment, madam,”* he said, *“as you have buried your pride. Let us hope it is not too late to save my young pupil.”* With that, he gathered his instruments and returned to Castlewood. For days, death hovered near, and Harry stood on the brink of inheritance—until the fever broke. Weak but alive, George recovered under Dempster’s care, though the physician urged a change of air, even a voyage to England. Yet old grudges barred that path. Instead, the brothers, with Dempster as tutor and a pair of servants, journeyed north: to New York, up the Hudson’s broad currents to Albany, and beyond into the French territories, where letters of introduction won them welcome among the colonial gentry.

Harry, ever the adventurer, took to the wilderness—hunting bears, trading furs, and bedding down beside campfires with the Indians. George, his health still delicate, found his place in drawing rooms. His French, already polished, became flawless; his manners charmed; his violin sang with the latest airs from Paris. He even fought a duel—a swift, graceful affair that left the Chevalier de la Jabotiere with a pierced shoulder and a newfound respect. *“A mother is blessed to have such a son,”* wrote Madame de Mouchy to Madam Esmond. (One wonders what the pious Mr. Whitfield might have thought of the widow’s pride in her son’s *prowess*.)

Ten months later, the brothers returned, taller, altered. George, once slight, now matched Harry in height. With powdered wigs, they were near indistinguishable; without them, George’s raven-black hair contrasted sharply with Harry’s sunlit curls. Yet beneath the surface, George’s temperament remained unchanged—proud, sensitive, quick to resent. He had left Virginia nursing a grudge against a certain gentleman, and he returned with it intact.

That gentleman was Mr. Washington. Though barely of age, Washington carried himself with the gravity of a man twice his years. Trusted by neighbors, respected for his judgment, he seemed to George a figure of unbearable virtue—especially when Madam Esmond held him up as a model. *“Ah, Harry! You waste your days on cockfights, while the Major guards our borders! George, is Latin worthier than service?”*

Mrs. Mountain, the widow’s sharp-eyed companion, fed George’s suspicions with whispers. *“Mark my words—he wants more than her counsel. A rich widow, a young officer—why, it’s plain as day!”* George, flushing, ordered her to silence. Yet the evidence gnawed at him: Washington’s constant presence, his own room at Castlewood, his name ever on his mother’s lips.

Then came news that shook George’s jealousy into something darker. Washington, commissioned to warn the French from encroaching on Virginia’s lands, had returned with defiance—and a militia regiment was mustering. *“An Esmond serves only under the king’s commission,”* Madam declared, spurning the thought of her son enlisting under Washington or begging favor from Governor Dinwiddie.

War loomed. French troops, outnumbering the British, seized a half-built fort in Pennsylvania, christening it Fort Duquesne. When Washington’s small force clashed with them, the first shots of the war were fired—though the skirmish ended in retreat. Humiliated, his regiment disbanded, Washington returned to Castlewood.

*“I was angry when we parted,”* he said to George, offering his hand. *“I thought you scorned me. I boasted too much of what we’d do.”*

*“You did your duty,”* George replied, the old envy fading. *“No one questions that.”*

But Madam Esmond, ever sharp, could not resist a barb. *“Kind of us to receive you? Colonel, I had not thought misfortune made men less welcome here.”* Her curtsey was flawless—and George, watching, felt the old bitterness rise anew.

The world had widened for the Virginians. Yet some wounds, it seemed, would not heal.

Sympathy is seldom so deserved as when granted to youth, bravery, and misfortune. Madam Esmond, even had she borne a dozen sons, could scarcely have helped doting upon her young soldier. Mr. Washington’s chamber had become a shrine in her eyes, his virtues the daily sermon preached to her boys. Harry’s reckless love of pleasure and George’s bookish indolence were forever measured—and found wanting—against the Major’s sterling character.

Yet George’s admiration did not flourish under such comparisons. A sullen demon took root within him, whispering doubts, breeding resentment. His jests grew so sharp that his mother, bewildered, could only stare at his brooding humor, uncertain whether to scold or pity him.

Meanwhile, the quarrel between France and England swelled beyond provincial skirmishes into open war. French reinforcements crowded Canada; English troops poured into Virginia. Letters from Quebec fluttered across the sea, their ink laced with foreboding.

*"Alas, my dear friend!"* wrote Madame de Mouchy to George. *"Must you forsake a mother’s embrace for the arms of Bellona? I tremble to wish victory for our lilies when you stand beneath the Leopard’s banner. Yet enmity has no place in my heart—only sorrow for the storm that divides us."*

The Leopard’s banner, indeed, unfurled boldly. Two royal regiments mustered in America, while Commodore Keppel’s fleet, bearing General Braddock and his staff, anchored in Hampton Roads. The Duke’s chosen commander arrived with fanfare, summoning colonial governors to council, demanding men and supplies for the campaign ahead.

The stir reached even Castlewood’s quiet halls. Harry, aflame with martial zeal, galloped to Alexandria to gape at the red-coated ranks, their drums throbbing like a pulse in his ears. He dined with officers, drank to the King’s health, and dreamed of glory. Madam Esmond, ever proud, deemed these English gentlemen a tonic against Virginia’s coarse squires and sycophants. She little guessed the raucous songs that echoed past midnight, or the unseemly scenes as officers staggered to their quarters.

The troops, swaggering through the countryside, soon wore out their welcome. Farmers cursed their looting; Indians, insulted by their arrogance, melted back into the forests—allies lost when most needed. Braddock raged against the colonials’ stinginess and his own men’s indiscipline. Yet when the General’s invitation came to Castlewood, Madam Esmond saw only honor.

His father had served with hers under Marlborough. Such ties demanded courtesy. The twins, splendid in London finery, presented themselves at his levee—only to be dismissed as *"young Squire Gawkeys"* by a bored commander.

*"Who are these popinjays?"* Braddock grumbled.

*"Sons of the Princess Pocahontas,"* sneered Lieutenant-Governor Dinwiddie, who bore the widow no love.

Dinwiddie’s malicious portrait—a pompous shrew with absurd pretensions—left the General chuckling. But Mr. Franklin, shrewder, noted her wealth: cattle, horses, stores vital for the campaign. A change of tone followed. An aide-de-camp soon bowed before the twins, bearing Braddock’s belated compliments and a dinner invitation.

Flushed with triumph, the boys returned. Their mother, quill in hand, penned a gracious reply. Yet beneath the civilities, tension simmered. War loomed; one son must answer its call. Harry ached to go, polishing swords, drilling slaves, his every thought bent on battle. George, though quieter, pored over military treatises, his fencing foil gleaming anew.

When Braddock’s offer came—a place in his family for a young Virginian—Harry’s face lit like a torch. *"I would give my soul to go!"*

George’s voice cut coldly. *"One of us must stay. Our mother cannot be left alone."*

Harry’s fists clenched, but habit bowed him to his brother’s will. The decision hung unspoken between them, a sword’s point at their throats. Not until they stood before Madam Esmond did the matter break.

*"I knew this hour must come,"* she whispered, eyes darting between them.

*"The youngest should go!"* Harry burst out.

*"The heir’s duty is first,"* George countered.

Harry reddened. *"But you’re still weak from fever! And Washington favors you—"*

*"Honor decides, not favor."* George’s hand gripped his brother’s shoulder. *"Were our births reversed, you’d claim the same. Our grandfather’s shade would demand it."*

Pride and anguish warred in their mother’s face. *"My father would say his grandsons are gentlemen,"* she murmured, and fled before her tears could shame her.

Preparations began. George’s servant, Sady, boasted of battles to come; Harry’s Gumbo whimpered—though all knew the boy feared his own shadow. Only Mrs. Mountain raged against George’s choice.

*"Folly!"* she stormed. *"You rush to your ruin!"*

*"Duty calls,"* he said stiffly.

*"Duty? Ha!"* Her laugh was bitter. *"When you return, you’ll find a new master here. Mark me—Washington means to wed your mother!"*

George recoiled. *"Lies!"*

*"Proof!"* She thrust a paper at him—Washington’s handwriting, snatched from his chamber.

*"You spied on a guest?"* George’s voice was deadly.

*"Chance! Mere chance!"* she squawked, as he flung the letter into the fire.

But the words had seared his mind. At dinner, he drank recklessly, his laughter too loud, his eyes darting to the door. Washington did not come that night—but the poison had been sown.

Jealousy, honor, and dread now marched alongside the drums of war.

The halls of Castlewood blazed with unwonted splendor on the day of Madam Esmond’s grand reception for General Braddock. The widow herself, usually so reserved in her attire, had arrayed herself in silks that whispered with every step, her pale cheeks touched with a color not entirely owed to rouge.

Colonel Washington, newly appointed aide-de-camp to the General, was the first to arrive, his boots dusty from the ride. He dismounted with the ease of a man born to the saddle, and for a time, he and his hostess paced the gallery in quiet conversation. The air between them hummed with unspoken things—old understandings, perhaps, or new tensions.

"You will watch over my son?" Madam Esmond asked at last, her voice softer than usual.

"With my life," Washington replied, bowing.

She colored faintly, her curtsy as precise as ever. "A mother could ask no more."

Then George appeared, powdered and polished, his bow as deep as it was cold. Beneath the veneer of courtesy, his eyes burned with a fury he made no effort to conceal.

"Ah, Colonel!" George said, his voice honeyed with mock admiration. "How fortunate we are to have you among us—so gallant, so *honorable*."

Washington’s jaw tightened, but he said nothing.

The evening unfolded with brittle gaiety. George played the harpsichord with exaggerated flourish, his fingers dancing over the keys as if to drown out the silence thickening around him. His jests grew sharper, his smiles more cutting.

"You must forgive my brother," Harry murmured to Washington at one point. "He has a fever today—not of the body, but of the mind."

Washington’s gaze flickered toward Madam Esmond, who stood stiffly by the hearth. "A common ailment," he said quietly. "And one that passes, with time."

But George had overheard. "Oh, does it?" he called, abandoning the harpsichord. "How reassuring. Tell me, Colonel—do you prescribe bleeding for such maladies? Or is it better to let the poison fester?"

The room fell silent. Even Harry, ever loyal, looked stricken.

Then the sound of carriage wheels announced the General’s arrival, and the moment shattered. Washington withdrew to compose himself, leaving George to stare after him, his hands clenched at his sides.

Later, when the guests had dispersed and the candles guttered low, Harry found his brother in the library, a crumpled letter in his grip.

"What is it?" Harry asked.

George’s laugh was hollow. "Only a draft of our future, dear brother. It seems we are to have a new father." He flung the paper into the fire. "Welcome to Castlewood, indeed."

His Excellency the Commander-in-Chief made his journey to Madam Esmond’s estate in a manner befitting the highest dignitary of His Majesty’s American dominions. A guard of dragoons led the procession from Williamsburg, the air thick with cheers—most notably from the enslaved folk, whose voices rose in dutiful clamor. The General himself traveled in his grand carriage, emblazoned with insignias, while Captain Talmadge, his Master of the Horse, rode alongside. Within the coach sat Major Danvers, the aide-de-camp, and the unlikeliest of companions—Mr. Franklin of Philadelphia, a man whose sharp wit and curious learning had already earned the begrudging respect of the Governor’s retinue.

“Remarkable,” mused His Excellency, tipping his hat with a flourish, “how a man of such humble beginnings has acquired such polish. One might almost mistake you for a gentleman, Mr. Franklin.”

The postmaster bowed. “Fortune has occasionally placed me in company where refinement was unavoidable. I merely observed and imitated.”

“Education, now—” The General waved a hand dismissively. “I’ve known officers who couldn’t spell ‘bull’ with one ‘b’ or two. The Duke of Marlborough himself was no scholar, yet he thrashed the French soundly enough.”

“So I have heard,” said Franklin mildly.

“And my own Duke—ah, I see that twinkle in your eye, sir! No scholar either, but a soldier through and through. Saved the Crown for His Majesty, God bless him.”

Franklin doffed his hat as powdered wig dust scattered. “A finer horseman I never saw.”

“A finer master, a finer friend! You’ll drink his health tonight, Franklin, mark my words.”

The carriage jolted violently over the uneven road, prompting fresh oaths from the General. Just then, a rider approached—a tall young man on a sturdy gelding, flanked by two liveried enslaved boys.

“Ah! Mr. Washington,” said the aide-de-camp.

“Fine figure on a horse,” the General remarked. “Wouldn’t mind him as a corporal. Knows the country, eh, Franklin?”

“Intimately,” Franklin agreed.

Washington saluted. “Your Excellency honors us.”

“No honor, sir—duty! And we’ll see you in proper regimentals soon enough, mark me.”

As the lumbering coach rolled toward Castlewood, a ragtag parade formed in its wake—Lieutenant-Governor Dinwiddie with his chaplain in tow, the schoolmaster in his grandest wig, the local justice and his wife on muleback, all too deferential to overtake the great man. Danvers and Franklin exchanged amused glances before urging the stragglers onward.

The feast that awaited them was worthy of a London alderman. Terrapin stew, gumbo thick with spices, shad fresh from the river—each dish proclaimed the skill of Madam Esmond’s cooks, trained under her exacting eye. She herself presided with effortless grace, carving joints with slender wrists bared beneath lace cuffs, pressing delicacies upon her guests.

“The jowl of this salmon is for Your Excellency—I insist! Mr. Franklin, must you refuse our wine? Justice Laws, another slice of woodcock pie?”

The judge bowed. “My wife often laments she lacks your hand with pastry, madam.”

Franklin’s gaze flicked to her hands. “A hand many would admire.”

She ignored the implication, though her son George frowned.

As the meal wore on, the talk turned to war—roads, rivers, supply trains. Washington traced imaginary battle lines with bread crumbs until a servant swept them away with the dishes. When the table cleared, Madam Esmond rose, glass in hand.

“Gentlemen—His Majesty.”

The toast was drunk with fervor. Then, with a curtsy, she withdrew—only to weep in the drawing-room moments later.

“Forgive me,” she murmured to Mrs. Laws. “But he takes my son. Who knows if he’ll return?”

The ladies murmured comforts, but she straightened. “No. I’ll not burden him with tears. An Esmond does his duty.”

Meanwhile, the wine flowed freely. Broadbent, the disreputable parson, was deep in his cups; the General’s face glowed crimson. Young George, usually abstemious, matched them drink for drink, his jabs at Washington growing sharper.

“You’ll join the ranks at last, Colonel,” he sneered. “Or will you survey the battlefield before committing?”

Washington stood abruptly. “Excuse me.”

Outside, pacing the veranda, he met Madam Esmond.

“The gentlemen are still at their sport?” she asked lightly.

“If drink wins battles, we shall conquer Canada by dawn,” he muttered.

She laid a hand on his arm. “And my sons?”

“Harry is steady. George—” He hesitated.

Her grip tightened. “What of George?”

But the answer was drowned by raucous laughter from within. The night wore on, glasses refilled, toasts called—while Washington, stiff and silent, watched the moon rise over the Virginia woods.

The guests of Castlewood had retired to their chambers, and the two young Warrington brothers found themselves alone in their room, their hearts aflame with indignation. The notion of their mother wedding a man scarcely older than themselves—a colonial surveyor, no less!—was unbearable. The very thought of Castlewood passing into the hands of a stepfather at three-and-twenty struck them as a monstrous betrayal.

Harry, impulsive as ever, was ready to storm their mother’s chamber at once, to denounce the match and swear they would leave her forever if she persisted. But George, ever the strategist, had a different plan.

“Mother cannot marry a man who has fought one of us,” George declared, his pale face flushed with the heat of the evening’s wine. “If we challenge him, if we face him on the field—wound or be wounded—the matter is settled. She would not dare wed our killer.”

Harry, ever the devoted follower, marveled at his brother’s cunning. “You’re right, George! She could never marry a man who’d spilled our blood. Should I send my boy with a challenge at once?”

George, recalling his own duel in Quebec, shook his head with lofty condescension. “We cannot insult a guest beneath our own roof. But once we ride beyond these gates, then we shall speak our minds plainly.”

Harry seized his brother’s hand. “By heaven, we will!”

A shadow crossed Harry’s face then, and he stammered, “George… if it comes to pistols, let it be my turn.”

George waved him off. “The head of the house must lead. If I fall, you may avenge me. Besides,” his voice hardened, “it is I who hate him most. Was it not he who counseled Mother to have that brute Ward lay hands on me?”

Harry, ever the peacemaker, murmured, “Forgive and forget, brother.”

“Forget?” George’s eyes burned. “A wrong does not vanish with time. I will not suffer this man’s arrogance—not then, not now.”

With that, the brothers retired, George sternly commanding Harry to maintain civility toward all guests until their departure.

***

Morning brought the aftermath of the night’s revelry. The English officers stumbled to breakfast, their heads heavy, while Colonel Washington remained composed, refusing the morning’s restorative draught. The British officers jested that he needed proper schooling in their ways, but the Virginian only smiled coolly.

Madam Esmond, though occupied with her duties, noted George’s scrupulous politeness toward Washington—a veneer that masked the storm beneath. Before General Braddock departed, he formally offered George a place in his staff, and the arrangements were swiftly made. The lady of Castlewood, though anxious, bore it with fortitude.

As the great coach rolled away, the boys lingered on the steps. Washington stood with their mother once more, and they heard his solemn vow: *“With my life. You may depend on me.”*

Then came the farewells. Washington clasped Harry’s hand warmly. To George, he said, “No adieu—we meet in three days at the camp.”

But George’s smile was cold. “Stay a moment longer, Colonel. Harry and I will ride with you a little way.”

Mounting their horses, the three set off, their grooms trailing behind. George, unnaturally cheerful, kept the conversation light, while Harry rode in troubled silence. Washington, disarmed by the apparent reconciliation, spoke warmly of the coming campaign, confident in British victory.

At Benson’s tavern, they paused. Halkett’s officers were already there, deep in their cups. George, with deliberate provocation, called for a round of toasts.

“The King!” he proclaimed, fixing Washington with a challenge.

The Colonel stiffened but drank.

“The Duke and the Army!” cried Captain Grace, slurring.

Again, Washington drank, though his face darkened.

“You don’t seem to relish the toast, Colonel,” George taunted.

“I’ve had enough,” Washington growled.

Grace, drunk and belligerent, took offense. “A British officer never refuses a toast!”

Washington’s temper snapped. “You are drunk, sir!”

The room erupted. George, seizing his moment, feigned outrage. “You insult my guest, Colonel!”

Washington, his patience spent, turned on George. “Months of your insolence—were it not for my regard for your mother—”

“What would you do?” George sneered.

“I’d take a cane to you, you impudent pup!”

The words hung in the air like a pistol shot.

Harry, pale but resolute, stepped forward. “That is an insult to us both. You will apologize or give satisfaction.”

Washington, aghast at his own outburst, tried to retreat. “My words were hasty—”

But George was relentless. “You shirk again, Colonel. You have insulted me publicly. I demand reparation.”

Washington, his face a mask of sorrow, bowed. “So be it.”

Grace, swaying, tried to intervene, but the matter was set. Pistols, George insisted—not swords, where Washington’s skill was superior.

As they parted to make preparations, Washington’s voice was heavy. “God forgive you, George. God pardon you, Harry.”

But George’s heart was stone. “This began long ago, Colonel. You thought me a slave then. You shall not think so now.”

Harry, torn but loyal, stood firm beside his brother.

And so the stage was set—not for war against the French, but for a duel that would alter their fates forever.

The Virginian colonel waited in the dim-lit tavern chamber, his thoughts heavy as he prepared for the impending duel. Across the hall, his young adversary, George Warrington, dictated a letter to his brother Harry—a grand, solemn farewell to their mother, laced with bitter irony.

“*As probably will be the case,*” George declared, his voice cold.

Harry’s quill faltered. “Don’t say that, Georgy!”

“But it is the truth,” George replied. “Colonel Washington is a marksman. And I? Well, I can hold my own. One of us may fall—likely both.” His tone sharpened as he added, “*I scarcely suppose you will carry out the intentions you have at present in view.*”

Harry’s eyes stung. He smudged the page, ink and tears mingling. “I can’t write properly—not now!”

With an impatient sigh, George took the quill himself. His words grew loftier, laced with Latin flourishes and cutting wit. He willed freedom to his servant Sady, his books to his tutor, his silver fruit-knife to little Fanny Mountain. And to Harry—a lock of his hair, a keepsake of a brother’s love. He sealed it with their grandfather’s crest.

Then came the watch—their grandfather’s gold timepiece. George pressed it into Harry’s hand. “It’s yours now.”

Harry flung his arms around him. “If he fights you, I’ll fight him too! If he kills you, by God, I’ll—” His voice broke into curses, raw and desperate.

Meanwhile, Colonel Washington had written his own letters—one to his mother, one to his brother, one marked *M.C.*, and one to General Braddock. And, as the tavern landlord informed the boys with a grim smile, one for Madam Esmond herself.

George’s face darkened. Another oath tore from his lips—one that would surely trouble the celestial scribes.

Outside, the world moved on, indifferent. The landlord, in his old militia coat, announced that the Colonel waited in the garden, pacing like a man already haunted. The English officers, roused from their drink, watched with bleary interest.

Then—chaos. A thunder of hooves, wild shouts. Sady, George’s servant, came galloping back, pistols in hand, whooping like a madman. Behind him, riding hard, came Mr. Dempster and—*Mrs. Mountain?*

She stumbled from her horse, breathless, and seized George in a frantic embrace. “It was a mistake! All my fault!”

George stiffened. “What mistake?”

“That paper—the one about the Colonel marrying a widow with two children—I thought it meant your mother! But no, it’s *Mrs. Custis*! He told Madam so himself today!”

Silence.

Harry exhaled. “Thank God.”

George’s jaw tightened. “Sady disobeyed me. I told him to keep silent.”

“Thank Heaven he didn’t!” Harry cried.

Then Harry was running—across the garden, to where Washington stood. Words poured out, clumsy and earnest. And the Colonel, after a moment’s stillness, pulled him into an embrace. “Thank God,” he murmured.

When George approached, the greeting was stiff, formal.

“I wronged you, Colonel,” George admitted. “I apologize—not for the error, but for my conduct since.”

Washington’s reply was curt. “The fault was not yours alone. Had my private papers remained private, much pain might have been spared.” He turned to Harry, clasping his shoulder. “Be less quick to think ill of friends, George. We meet again at camp—save your fire for the enemy.”

With a final bow to the English officers, he mounted his horse and rode away—leaving behind a silence thick with shame and relief.

The parting between the brothers had come and gone. George had taken his place among Mr. Braddock’s retinue, while Harry returned to the quiet halls of Castlewood, where duty now bound him. Yet his heart remained with the army, and the familiar comforts of home brought him no joy. He would not confess the depth of his disappointment, but the absence of his brother weighed upon him like a leaden cloak.

Harry walked past George’s empty chamber with averted eyes. He sat in his brother’s place at the head of the table, raising the silver tankard to his lips with a sigh. Each day, Madam Warrington lifted her voice in the loyal toast to the King, unwavering. On Sundays, when Harry read the service and prayed for travelers upon land and sea, she echoed the words with solemn emphasis. She spoke of George often—cheerfully, as though his return were assured. She entered his vacant room without flinching, saw that his books and linen were kept in order, and addressed the servants with quiet authority, speaking of tasks to be done "when Mr. George comes home."

Mrs. Mountain’s eyes welled at the mention of his name, and Harry’s face darkened with dread, but their mother remained composed. Her hands faltered more than usual at cards, and the servants found her awake at all hours, as if sleep had forsaken her. She had urged Mr. Dempster to resume his residence at Castlewood, and her manner, once so imperious, had softened into something gentler. She spoke often of her father’s campaigns, how he had emerged with little harm, and she clung to the hope that her son would do the same.

Letters from George arrived with welcome frequency, and as the army marched, he sent rough journals for Harry to read aloud in the evenings. The family gathered in the fading light—Madam Esmond erect at her tea-table, little Fanny bent over her needlework, Mr. Dempster and Mrs. Mountain at their cards, the servants hovering in silence—while Harry’s voice filled the room with his brother’s words.

*"The provinces betray His Majesty shamefully,"* George wrote. *"Virginia is remiss, Maryland worse, but Pennsylvania is the most perfidious of all. We beg for troops, promise to sustain them, then cheat the very men who come to defend us. No wonder the General curses and the men grow sullen. The delays have been endless. Had we moved sooner, the French would not now be strengthening their forts.

Colonel Washington, though no friend of mine, is brave and able. The General values his counsel, for he alone has faced the Indians in war-paint. I confess he was right to fire upon Jumonville.

As for the quarrel at Benson’s Tavern—let it rest. Captain Waring wished to revive it, but wiser heads intervened. The affair began in drunken folly, and the 44th’s officers were made to swallow their pride. Washington played the diplomat, and the matter ended over a dull dinner. He is too faultless—that is why I cannot like him. If he marries that widow, Heaven help her."*

“I marvel at the taste of some men—and the boldness of some women,” remarked Madam Esmond, setting down her cup. “That a widow should forget herself so far as to marry again!”

“Monstrous,” murmured Mrs. Mountain, with a peculiar glance.

Dempster studied his punch. Harry stifled a laugh—or some other emotion—but his mother bade him continue.

*"Discipline in camp is strict,"* George wrote. *"The men are flogged for drunkenness, and the drummers are ever at work. It sickened me to see the lash bite into a man’s back and hear his cries.

The officers, however, are less restrained. The Indians quit us in disgust after the young fools drank with their women—but I say no more of that."*

“Spare us the details,” said Madam Esmond. Harry skipped ahead.

*"Each Sunday, Divine Service is held at the colors. The General does his utmost to prevent plunder, offering his own money to secure provisions. He is a strange man—swears like a trooper yet never misses prayers. He idolizes the Duke, and speaks of him constantly.

Colonel Washington has been ill with fever. Perhaps his widow should nurse him. When one of us is ailing, we forget our quarrels. Yet I cannot forgive him entirely. We should have settled matters with powder and ball that day at the tavern. Still, though I do not love him as you do, I respect him as a soldier and an honest man—and I’ll not begrudge him for refusing to be our stepfather."*

“A stepfather!” Madam Esmond’s voice was sharp. “Does the boy think the Marquis of Esmond’s daughter could find no better match than a provincial surveyor? Skip such folly, Harry.”

*"The march is a grand sight,"* Harry read on. *"The redcoats winding through the forest, ever wary of ambush. Our scouts have taken scalps, for the French and their savages show no mercy. We found a scalped child, still alive, in a ruined homestead—his parents butchered. The General now offers five pounds per Indian scalp.

Our camp is orderly, with the baggage at its heart and sentries posted thick. I march on foot now, my horses pressed into service. Poor Roxana bears packs instead of me, but she knows my step and whinnies when I pass."*

The letter closed with tender duty to his mother and love to Harry.

But as July wore on, unease crept over Virginia. Rumors flew—whispered by servants, debated in taverns. The army had met disaster. An ambush. Slaughter. The General dead, his scalp taken.

When the news reached Castlewood, Mrs. Mountain let out a cry. Little Fanny clung to her mother. Madam Esmond sank to her knees, hands raised to Heaven. “Lord God Almighty, defend my boy!”

Harry and Dempster armed themselves and rode north, following the army’s trail. Each mile brought darker tidings. A thousand men lost. Officers cut down. The General slain.

And George?

Harry’s heart clenched like a fist. Memories of their boyhood—vows made in youthful earnestness—rose like ghosts. Wounded men pitied his grief; women softened at his stricken face. Even the stern tutor wept—not only for the pupil lost, but for the brother who rode through the gathering dark, desperate for word of the dead.

As Harry Warrington pressed deeper into Pennsylvania, the grim echoes of the British defeat grew louder. The vaunted regiments, seasoned in European wars, had crumbled before unseen foes—wilderness sharpshooters and a handful of French regulars. Tales of the disaster spread like wildfire: the river crossing, the slow advance through tangled woods, the ambush that erupted from the very trees themselves. The commanding general had fought valiantly—four horses shot beneath him, his body riddled with wounds—but bravery could not stem the slaughter.

Harry’s heart clenched with each new account. Survivors spoke of the fallen, of officers cut down in the chaos. Among them, perhaps, his brother. One name surfaced again and again: *George Washington*. The young Virginian aide had been at the general’s side, yet emerged unscathed while others perished.

At Dunbar’s camp, Harry found Washington himself—pale, fever-wracked, but alive. The man’s face darkened at Harry’s desperate questions. He had searched the field after the battle, burying the mutilated dead. One body, clad in George’s uniform, had been laid to rest. But Harry refused to believe. "If you loved him, you’d have found him!" he cried, raw with grief.

Against all advice, Harry retraced the army’s path. The battlefield was a graveyard of carrion and crows. He gagged at the stench, the sightless eyes of the fallen. No trace of George remained—only the silent accusation of the woods.

By the time he staggered back to camp, fever had him in its grip. Delirium blurred days into nights. When he woke, weak but lucid, Washington and the steadfast Dempster helped him home to Castlewood.

The estate was a house of shadows. His mother, Madam Esmond, greeted him with trembling hands and hollow eyes. She clung to a spectral hope: George was not dead. A prisoner, perhaps. A vision of her father had assured her. But her welcome for Washington was ice. "You promised to guard my son," she whispered, her voice a blade. "Yet here you stand—whole—while *he* is lost."

Harry watched, torn. Washington, stiff with regret, accepted her scorn. He had done his duty—fought, searched, mourned. But duty rang hollow to a mother’s grief.

Castlewood festered with unspoken accusations. Madam Esmond’s faith in George’s survival became obsession. She spurned Washington, forbade his name. Harry, caught between loyalty and love, met the colonel in secret, their friendship now a guilty thing.

The house itself seemed to sicken. Harry’s strength waned; the doctors urged a sea voyage. His mother, brittle with unresolved anger, let him go. As his ship sailed from the James River, he donned mourning—not for a brother confirmed dead, but for one vanished into the wilderness’s embrace.

Behind him, Castlewood stood empty of joy. Madam Esmond moved to Richmond, filling her days with cards and sermons, her heart with ghosts. The great estate, once vibrant with family strife and laughter, echoed only with absence.

And Harry? He sailed toward Europe, leaving behind a home that could no longer hold him.

When the young Virginian recounted his adventures to his aunt, Madame Bernstein, her stern heart softened in spite of itself. Though hardened by years of solitude and disappointment, she found herself strangely moved by the boy’s earnest simplicity. His voice, his manner—so unlike the polished artifice of the society she knew—stirred something long buried within her.

She listened as he spoke of his mother’s fierce protectiveness, of his own youthful jealousy toward Colonel Washington, of the vast forests and rivers of his homeland. And though she scoffed inwardly at sentiment, she could not deny the warmth that spread through her as he spoke.

*"If only I had had a son like him,"* she thought bitterly. *"But no—my life was not meant for such tenderness."*

Yet, for now, she allowed herself the indulgence.

Meanwhile, the Castlewood family received their colonial cousin with varying degrees of enthusiasm. Lady Castlewood, ever the gracious hostess, masked her reservations behind honeyed words.

*"How charming he is!"* she exclaimed to Madame Bernstein. *"So refreshingly unspoiled! One wonders how such manners could flourish in the wilderness."*

*"His mother was a woman of rare breeding,"* Madame Bernstein replied coolly. *"And his stepfather, Colonel Esmond, was a gentleman of the old school."*

*"Ah, but breeding alone does not make a man,"* Lady Castlewood sighed, casting a meaningful glance toward her daughters. *"One must have heart as well. And I confess, I live only for my children."* She clasped her hands dramatically, her bracelets tinkling. *"Should anything befall them, I should perish!"*

The young ladies, Fanny and Maria, were less guarded in their interest. Fanny, in particular, seemed eager to instruct Harry in the fashionable dances of the day, much to her brother William’s visible irritation.

*"Infernal savage!"* William muttered under his breath. *"Does he mean to turn Fan’s head?"*

Lady Castlewood, ever watchful, was quick to intervene.

*"Fanny, my dear,"* she purred, *"you must not monopolize our cousin. Surely Maria would like a turn as well?"*

Maria, seated at the harpsichord, played on with forced cheerfulness, while Harry—awkward but eager—attempted the steps under Fanny’s guidance.

*"I fear I am a poor student,"* Harry admitted with a sheepish grin.

*"Nonsense!"* Fanny laughed. *"You will learn quickly enough."*

But later, in private, Lady Castlewood’s tone sharpened.

*"Have you lost your senses?"* she hissed at her daughter. *"Encouraging that provincial boy? Do you imagine he is a suitable match for an earl’s daughter?"*

*"He is our cousin!"* Fanny protested.

*"Cousin or not, he is nobody!"* Lady Castlewood snapped. *"A backwoods upstart with no prospects. If you persist in this folly, I shall take you back to Kensington at once!"*

Fanny’s defiance wilted under her mother’s glare.

Meanwhile, Harry remained blissfully unaware of the undercurrents swirling around him. He rode with William, played cards with Lord Castlewood, and basked in the warmth of his newfound family.

For now, at least, he was welcome.

But in the grand halls of Castlewood, where ambition and deception walked hand in hand, his presence was already stirring whispers—and not all of them kind.

The morning after Harry’s arrival at Castlewood dawned bright and tranquil, the air crisp with the promise of autumn. Sunday had come, and with it, the quiet rituals of worship. The chapel adjoining the castle served as the village church, its ancient stones steeped in the prayers of generations. A private door led from the house to the family pew, where the Esmonds took their places with solemn propriety, while the villagers filled the humble benches below.

Above them, faded banners hung like forgotten sentinels, their colors dulled by time. Harry’s imagination kindled at the sight—these very standards might have been borne by loyal retainers in the Civil Wars, men who fought for king and creed. Behind the altar rail stood the effigy of their common ancestor, Sir Francis Esmond, forever kneeling in stone, his marble hands clasped in eternal devotion.

Madame de Bernstein, the dowager widow of a bishop, observed the service with stately reverence, her demeanor as composed as the sculptured knight before her. The young clergyman, a handsome man with a voice rich and resonant, read the Scriptures with such animation that the words seemed to leap from the page. The music swelled as one of the Castlewood daughters played the organ, though the harmony was nearly undone when a burst of laughter erupted from the servants’ pew. The cause? Harry’s manservant, Gumbo, whose exuberant voice soared above the congregation, his untrained but magnificent singing drowning out even the clerk. The parson stifled a grin behind his handkerchief, while the London footmen gaped in astonishment.

The sermon that followed was anything but dull. The clergyman, with the flair of a seasoned storyteller, painted a vivid scene: the execution of a horse-thief he had witnessed at the recent Assizes. He spoke not with the fire-and-brimstone fervor Harry knew from his mother’s favored preachers, but as a man of the world, warning his flock against vice with a narrative both gripping and grim. He conjured the thief’s downfall—from honest farmer to drunkard, gambler, and finally, gallows’ prey. The law, he declared, was ever watchful, its reach long and its grip unrelenting.

Harry, rapt, scarcely noticed the passage of time. The ladies of Castlewood listened with polite interest, though the village folk shuffled out afterward as unmoved as if they had heard a weather report.

As the congregation dispersed, Lord Castlewood remarked dryly, *"Sampson has thundered today. The Philistines are well smitten."*

*"Magnificent,"* Harry agreed, still under the spell of the sermon.

*"Bah! That’s his Assize speech,"* scoffed Will. *"He trots it out whenever he’s been to Winchester."*

The organ’s final notes faded into the crisp air. Only Sir Francis Esmond’s stone figure remained, frozen in prayer. The parson emerged from the vestry, nodding amiably to the lingering gentry.

*"Come, tell us of the dogs,"* Will demanded, and with a chuckle, the clergyman obliged.

In the gallery connecting chapel to house, introductions were made. Mr. Sampson bowed deeply to Harry, offering pious hopes that the young Virginian might learn virtue from his English kin. Then, with a swift pivot, the talk turned to horses—specifically, the odds at Huntingdon’s races.

*"Five to four on Brilliant,"* the parson declared.

*"Jason’s the better horse,"* countered Will.

*"Do you wager in Virginia, Mr. Warrington?"* the chaplain inquired.

*"On occasion,"* Harry admitted, grinning.

*"Then I’ll take Brilliant against the field—for ponies!"* Will challenged.

*"Three to one on Jason,"* the parson added.

Harry hesitated. *"I don’t bet on horses I don’t know."*

Will sneered. *"Best write home and ask your mother first."*

Harry’s jaw tightened. *"I manage my own affairs, cousin. Done and done."*

The bets were struck, though Harry later questioned his haste. *"A Virginian doesn’t back down,"* he told himself, even as his purse felt lighter at the thought.

Dinner was a lively affair, the parson regaling the table with London gossip—who had fled with whom, the latest scandal at court, the most outrageous wagers of the town. The ladies laughed at jests that would have scandalized a later age, their cheeks unblushing. Harry, wide-eyed, absorbed it all.

Cards followed, the clatter of ivory counters mingling with the evening birdsong. Madame de Bernstein, sharp as ever, dominated at piquet. The chaplain matched her glass for glass, returning from a swift evening prayer to drain another bottle.

Night fell unnoticed. The moon rose, the stars blinked awake, and still they played on, until supper was announced.

Then came the postboy’s horn, heralding news. Lord Castlewood’s paper held a revelation: *Jason* had won at Huntingdon.

Will swore. The parson groaned. Harry, flush with triumph, waved off his winnings. *"It was in jest, cousin."*

*"Do you think I can’t pay?"* Will snapped, drowning his irritation in wine.

Harry sighed. For all its grandeur, Castlewood was proving a place of sharp edges. Sunday had ended—not in prayerful quiet, but in the rattle of dice and the clink of glasses. And as the candles guttered low, he wondered if this was the England he had dreamed of, or something far more complicated.

The young Virginian, having relieved his cousin and the chaplain of their coin, felt obliged to offer them the chance of reclaiming their losses—though his mother and other strict moralists might have frowned upon his conduct. Cards had become a dangerous habit. Beyond the genteel whist and quadrille with the ladies after dinner, there were nightly gatherings where the stakes grew alarmingly high, drawing in every gentleman of the house, my lord included. Since their Sunday conversation, his lordship had warmed considerably toward his colonial kinsman, wagering with him amiably over backgammon and piquet. Mr. William and the devout chaplain, meanwhile, harbored a secret taste for hazard—though they indulged discreetly, well out of sight of the ladies, who had extracted numerous promises from Cousin Will that he would neither lead Harry astray nor fall into mischief himself. Will swore he would never gamble—never!—yet no sooner did the household retire than he would slip away with dice and rum to Harry’s chambers, where the three would play until dawn.

When Harry had painted his glowing picture of the Virginia estate to Lord Castlewood, he had not meant to deceive. The lad was honest to a fault. But his upbringing had exposed him to all manner of rough company—horse-traders, tavern idlers, gamblers, and sportsmen, who thrived in the colony. A landed gentry, propped up by enslaved labor, found little diversion beyond the hunt, the card table, or the punch bowl. Hospitality was boundless; every man’s house stood open to his neighbor, and idle planters rode from one ramshackle mansion to another, finding the same rough plenty and raucous entertainment. A Virginian squire might sport a barefoot valet and a patched saddle, but his stables never lacked corn, nor his table claret and venison. Harry had slept on countless straw mattresses and caroused through endless nights, draining cracked bowls of punch until the hounds were loosed at dawn. His sober elder brother, he admitted ruefully, had been of a different temperament—proof enough that Nature molds men as she pleases: some for books and tea, others for Burgundy and hard riding.

Harry’s inclinations were plain to his English kin, none of whom mourned his lack of Puritan restraint. A century prior, manners were looser, tongues freer, and morals far less delicate than in our present refined age. (Shall we not congratulate ourselves on such progress, dear reader?)

Thus, though wary of his cousins’ prying, Harry held his cards close. In games of chance or skill, he proved more than a match for his new companions. Even at billiards—a pastime beloved by his grandfather, who had imported one of Virginia’s few tables—he soon rivaled Will and the chaplain. After their first wager, Harry grew cautious with Will, who conceded grudgingly that the colonial was his equal in most contests, and his better in many. Yet for all his prowess against the parson and Will (who naturally bested the ladies), why did Harry so often lose to one opponent in particular?

His courtesy to petticoats was instinctive—a tradition not yet extinguished in his homeland. Every woman at Castlewood adored him. The stern housekeeper softened; the cook beamed; the maids, French and English alike, simpered in his wake; even the lodge-keeper’s pretty daughter brightened at his greeting. Madame de Bernstein noted it all, watching the boy’s character with silent scrutiny.

Lady Maria Esmond’s age was a mystery. Peerage records being scarce, none could say whether Will’s claim of thirty-five was slander or mere exaggeration. Maria was his half-sister, born of the late Lord Castlewood’s first wife, a German lady wed during Queen Anne’s wars. When she assured Harry she was but seven-and-twenty, he protested gallantly: *"Your ladyship might be mother to all the cupids! You scarce look twenty!"*

In truth, Maria’s age shifted with her audience. A fair beauty with roses in her cheeks, golden tresses tumbling over her shoulders, and shapely arms that gleamed as she stretched across the billiard table, she was a vision—especially when a fleeting glimpse of ankle, clocked stocking, or red-heeled slipper sent Harry into raptures. Foolish boy! Had he ever noticed how his mother’s foot was far more slender? But sons seldom study maternal ankles with such fervor.

Alone, Maria treated Harry with marked kindness. In company, she mocked his provincial accent (which he labored to correct) and dismissed him as a simpleton. Yet her aunt remarked approvingly that they would soon polish him into a proper gentleman.

Lord Castlewood’s manner grew ever more familiar. With harvest pending, sport was limited to the occasional cockfight or bull-baiting. Harry and Will toured neighboring estates—the Henleys, the Crawleys, the Redmaynes—where heavy coaches brought country society for days of rustic revelry. More would have come, but for Madame de Bernstein’s disdain. "We are livelier without her," the younger set confessed. "She ruled our father, and now commands here. Even my brother fears her."

*"You will return for our merrier seasons, Harry?"* Maria entreated, her blue eyes soft. *"This is your home."* Her hand lingered in his. How mistaken he had been to deem his English kin cold! The Earl was kindness itself; dear Maria, angelic. He rued his hasty letter to Virginia, lamenting his chilly reception. Now he wrote anew, extolling their affection—and misspelling *angel* as *Angle*, to his tutor’s dry amusement.

*"Can the boy be in love with his 'Angle'?"* laughed Mountain in Virginia.

*"Maria? Forty if she’s a day!"* scoffed Madam Esmond. *"A gawky, carrot-haired creature with feet like bellows."*

Ah, where lies truth? Is beauty real, or do love’s eyes conjure it? Let us pray Cupid anoints our sight, that we may ever find the beloved flawless!

Nor was Harry the only favorite. His valet, Gumbo, dazzled the household with his talents—fishing, forging, hair-dressing, cooking, and fiddling—though his lies were as prodigious as his skills. He spun tales of Virginia’s splendors: vast gardens, endless slaves, and Madam Esmond’s ships and gold. The servants half-believed him; the family, wholly. Soon, Harry found himself credited with fortunes he never possessed, his modest habits taken as proof of hidden wealth.

Even Madame de Bernstein, privy to her sister’s true means, indulged the fiction, amused by the sudden deference paid her nephew. The Countess rued her early coldness; Lady Fanny’s arm bore bruises from maternal pinches meant to curb her friendliness. Only Maria’s constant presence thwarted their designs.

*"If he’s so rich, our aunt will leave him everything,"* grumbled my lord.

*"He wears his wealth lightly,"* Maria observed.

*"Rich men often do,"* sighed my lord. *"Were I wealthy, I’d hoard like a miser. Poverty forces my extravagance."*

Maria pored over *The History of Virginia*, dreaming of its fertile lands. Might she suit its climate? Would Harry’s mother approve? When pressed about his estate, Harry laughed off Gumbo’s exaggerations, but his modesty only deepened the illusion.

As weeks passed, Madame de Bernstein wearied of Castlewood. The smoky chimneys, the draughty halls—even the cook she had praised now displeased her. Tunbridge’s waters beckoned.

*"I go for my health,"* she declared. *"Alone. No simpering nieces, no prattling companions."*

*"Might I escort you?"* Will offered.

But Harry, flushing crimson, stammered a refusal—prior engagements, promised shooting.

*"You refuse?"* the Baroness snapped, her eyes blazing.

Maria bent over her needlework, silent. The air thickened with unspoken tensions.

---

*Thus the stage is set: fortunes imagined, hearts entangled, and the Baroness’s patience worn thin. What follows, only the next turn of the card may reveal.*

The suddenness of young Harry Warrington’s defiance left Madame de Bernstein momentarily stunned, her fury flashing like lightning as she swept from the room. Her parting glare at Lady Fanny and her mother spoke volumes, while Lady Maria, bent over her tambour-frame, scarcely lifted her eyes—her needle darting in and out with practiced indifference.

“So, despite everything, *you* have—” Lady Castlewood’s sharp gaze seemed to accuse.

“Have what?” Fanny’s wide, innocent eyes blinked back. But innocence was a performance, and Fanny’s was less convincing than Maria’s. Had she been guilty, she would have rehearsed her role better.

Lady Castlewood’s lips thinned. She could not claw her daughter’s eyes out here—not in the open. Such discipline required privacy, a smiling arm around the waist before the gouging began.

“You’ve no desire to trail after the old lady to Tunbridge Wells, then?” she inquired of Harry, her tone honeyed.

Lord Castlewood intervened smoothly. “No blame to him. A man ought to mix duty with pleasure. Let him learn the workings of an English estate before he idles at pump-rooms. My brother has shown him the kennels, the stables, the games—and he plays them as well as his tutors. Come harvest, we’ll show him shooting; in winter, hunting. Though our Virginia kin and we have been estranged, blood is blood. Before he returns to his mother, let him see how an English gentleman lives.”

The frankness of his speech left the others wary. What game was he playing? Castlewood’s schemes often unfolded months later, his meanings opaque until the final move.

“What’s his aim now?” muttered William as they retreated to the corridor.

“By God, I see it!”

“What?”

“He’ll lure the boy to the tables and win back the Virginia lands!”

“But the lad hasn’t the estate to stake,” protested his mother.

William scoffed. “If my brother hasn’t a scheme, I’ll eat my hat.”

“He can’t mean Maria—she’s old enough to be his mother.”

Fanny sighed. “With her freckles and sandy hair? Impossible.”

Mamma’s eyes narrowed. “Oh? Had *you* designs on the Iroquois?”

“I know my station,” Fanny retorted. “Unlike some, I’d not marry where I didn’t love.”

A bitter laugh from William. “Our parents scarcely loved, yet they wed. Remember their battles, Fan? And Esmond playing peacemaker.”

“Spare me those memories,” Lady Castlewood snapped.

Fanny’s voice turned plaintive. “What good is a coronet without coin? Tradesmen’s widows dress finer than we do!”

William smirked. “Glad I dine at Court, then.”

“Happiness needs no title,” Fanny insisted.

“Even in America? With war-whoops and scalping knives?”

“I’d skip church,” Fanny muttered.

William roared. “You’d go with *any* suitor—just like Maria!”

Madame Bernstein’s voice cut through. “What merry war is this?”

William gleefully recounted the spat—Fanny’s supposed infatuation, their mother’s veto. Fanny protested, cheeks aflame.

Bernstein’s brow furrowed. “If not for Fanny, why does he stay? You’ve shown him no beauty but frights and nursery-maids.”

William grinned. “Tastes differ. The kitchen’s fair game.”

“Not for *this* boy.” Her mind raced. Maria—*Maria?* Impossible. Yet—

She recalled Harry’s absences, Maria’s coinciding walks. Garden whispers, lingering touches. A sudden suspicion took root.

“Impossible?” William scoffed. “Lady Suffolk wed young Berkeley at sixty. And didn’t Harry Estridge pine for *you* at twenty?”

The jab struck home—not with anger, but amusement. “Age doesn’t quench folly,” she mused.

“But Maria?” William persisted. “Unless—” He laughed. “By Jove, we’ve flushed the fox!”

How far had it gone? Threats would only harden Harry’s resolve. Opposition fed obsession—she knew that well.

“Leave him to it,” William suggested. “An earl’s daughter for a planter’s son isn’t ill-matched.”

Outrage erupted. Bernstein silenced them. “Remember what you owe his family! His grandfather surrendered title, lands—*this very castle*—to yours. And you’d repay him by palming off a withered spinster?”

Lady Castlewood bristled. “Our name is honor enough for any Virginian.”

Bernstein’s laugh was sharp. “Fanny belongs at Court, not in a wilderness. Let him take a wife fit for a plantation.”

The others demurred, but the Baroness saw through them. She knew the game now—Maria’s desperation, Harry’s infatuation. How deep did it run?

As she withdrew, her mind turned over the puzzle. Women’s secrets were buried deep—some from modesty, some from cunning. And Maria, the family joke, had outplayed them all.

*Let her try,* Bernstein thought. *But not at Harry’s expense.*

As Lady Castlewood and her children withdrew through one door of the saloon, Lord Castlewood made his exit by another, leaving Harry Warrington standing beneath the ancestral portrait above the fireplace, his face flushed with a boy’s fervor. The demure eyes that had been fixed upon a tambour frame now lifted, regarding him with a playful scrutiny.

"Why did you refuse to accompany our aunt, cousin?" Lady Maria inquired, her needle pausing mid-stitch.

"Because you bade me stay," Harry replied, his earnestness undimmed by her teasing tone.

"Bade you stay? La, child! Must every idle word a lady speaks be taken as law? If all Virginian gentlemen are so obedient, your country must be a paradise for our sex!"

"You said—" Harry’s voice trembled, "that night on the terrace—"

"Ah, that sweet evening!" she sighed, feigning innocence.

"—when you gave me this rose," he burst out, producing a wilted bloom from his waistcoat, "that if I left, I would forget you. But I swear by heaven, I never could!"

"All men forget," she murmured, casting her eyes down.

"Not in Virginia," Harry declared, his passion undeterred. "I would sooner lose a limb than deny my aunt—yet I could not bear to leave you. If you bade me leap from yonder window, I would do it. If you asked for my blood, I would spill it gladly."

"La! Must you crush my hand so, you foolish boy?" she protested, though she did not pull away.

Harry, heedless of decorum, pressed on. "Ever since that night, I have scarcely slept. I long to prove myself—to fight giants, to conquer kingdoms—if only for your sake."

She laughed, a practiced, musical sound. "Men always speak so when they are near. And when they are gone? They forget."

"Not I," he vowed. "I have tired of games, of books—of everything but you. Before I knew you, I longed for glory in battle. Now, there is only one thing I desire."

"You silly creature! Do you not realize I am nearly old enough to—"

"I know your age," he interrupted. "I’ve seen the family Bible. I know the dates, the names. Nothing changes what I feel."

At this, her composure faltered. "Who showed you?" she demanded, her voice sharp. "Was it my mother-in-law, that grasping harpy? Or my aunt, the moralist who—"

"It was no one," Harry interjected, startled by her vehemence.

She checked herself, smoothing her skirts. "Well, no matter. I only wish you to think well of me, Harry."

His hand found hers, and he pressed it fervently. "Angel," he breathed, lost in the depths of her gaze—those practiced, calculating eyes that mirrored back his own devotion.

Had he been wiser, he might have seen the artifice beneath the charm. But youth is ever blind. To Harry, she was divinity; to her, he was but another conquest, a fleeting amusement in the long, dull game of her life.

---

Later, in the solitude of his chamber, Harry penned a letter home—a carefully edited account of his days at Castlewood, omitting certain tender interludes.

*"The Baroness de Bernstein has shown me every kindness,"* he wrote, *"and I pray my mother will reconcile with her. Lady Maria is beloved by all, especially the poor, whom she visits often..."*
