He paused, recalling the rose, now crumbling in his pocket. How had it come to him? The details scarcely mattered. Such stories were as old as time—a glance, a whispered word, a trinket given and cherished.

The ink dried on the page. Some truths were best left unwritten.

The Baroness de Bernstein’s mood had shifted like the turn of a card by the next meal. Gone was the morning’s frost; in its place bloomed an amiable warmth that embraced the entire table—Harry Warrington included. She praised the cook’s fricassee, declared the Castlewood eels incomparable, and dismissed the notion of opening another bottle for an "old woman" like herself. When the fresh wine did arrive, she raised her glass to Madam Esmond Warrington of Virginia, then teased Harry about securing his mother’s blessing for an English bride.

Her chatter flowed like the wine—stories of her mother’s beauty, her happiness with Colonel Esmond despite the years between them. The table listened, each with private thoughts. Lady Castlewood and her children were silent. Harry, enraptured, hung on every word. Maria, ever dutiful, blinked in surprise. Lord Castlewood, though his face was a mask of contentment, watched his aunt with the wary patience of a man awaiting the reveal of a card trick.

The Baroness, with practiced charm, turned the conversation to her own childhood mischief. *"I was a horrid little creature,"* she confessed, laughing. *"If I’d been cross in the morning, I’d coax my parents by supper. I remember doing it right here—oh, centuries ago!—and we had collared eels, just like tonight."* She clasped her hands, a penitent girl of seventy. *"So you see, I’m only repeating history. I scolded my sister, snapped at her children, even boxed poor Harry’s ears for refusing Tunbridge Wells!"*

*"But I’ll go now, gladly!"* Harry interjected, too eager.

The Baroness beamed. *"There, you see? All forgiven. Maria, my dear, I owe you the deepest apology."*

Maria, ever the diplomat, murmured polite demurrals, but the Baroness pressed on. *"No, no, I was cruel. You offered to accompany me, and I refused you rudely. But now I beg—nay, command—you to come!"*

Lord Castlewood sipped his wine, his face unreadable. *"Lady Maria is of age to decide,"* he said smoothly.

*"Then it’s settled!"* the Baroness declared. *"Tunbridge shall be delightful. And Harry, you’ll escort us, of course? Protect us from highwaymen?"*

Harry’s face burned. *"It would be my honor,"* he managed, though his heart was a tumult. Two days riding beside her carriage! Two days of stolen glances, of her gloved hand resting on his as he helped her in and out. A week in Tunbridge—a week near her, even if not with her. It was a reprieve, a glimpse of heaven before the axe fell.

The cards were dealt again that night—not of love, but of luck. Will and the chaplain, desperate to recoup losses, pushed for higher stakes. Harry played with absent-minded precision, his thoughts across the courtyard where Maria’s lamp still glowed. He won, not from cunning, but because fortune favors the lovestruck. Will cursed; the chaplain groaned over his lost books and livestock. Harry barely noticed.

*"You’ll stay a day or two at the Wells,"* the Baroness had said. *"Then back to Castlewood and partridges."*

A week. A single, fleeting week. For now, that was enough.

As the clock struck midnight, Harry stood at his window, staring at the casement across the way. Behind it, his divinity slept. The stars above seemed to pulse in time with his heartbeat. Let Will rage over lost horses. Let the chaplain lament his pawned gown. None of it mattered.

For in that room, under that flickering lamp, lay the only treasure he cared to win.

The good Bishop of Cambray, in his celebrated romance, painted the sorrows of Calypso when Ulysses departed from her shores, but history is silent on the grief of Calypso’s maid upon parting with Odysseus’s own manservant. No doubt, while their betters wept in grand saloons, the domestics below exchanged tearful farewells in the kitchens. When the final bell tolled, and the ship’s mate roared, “All ashore!” both mistress and maid must have trod the same trembling plank, hearts throbbing, eyes streaming, waving their handkerchiefs—one of lace, the other of humbler linen—toward the retreating vessel. The crowd on the quay and the sailors in the prow might cheer, but the truth remained: if Calypso could not be consoled, neither could her maid. Grief is no respecter of rank; tears taste just as salt in a palace as in a scullery.

And so it was not only Harry Warrington who left Castlewood a lovesick swain, but his servant Gumbo as well, whose charms had bewitched the entire female household. His wit, his dancing, his skill in cookery and music, had endeared him to every maid from the still-room to the laundry. The men might grumble, but the women adored him. In those days, England had not yet learned to scorn dark skin; black servants were met with tolerance, even a rough kindness, especially from women, who have ever been tender toward those the world deems lesser. Gumbo, like many a man before and since, spun tales of royal lineage—descended, he claimed, from Ashantee kings. (How many such exiled monarchs must there have been, judging by their descendants scattered across the globe?)

On the morning of Madame de Bernstein’s departure, Castlewood’s servants crowded the halls, some to glimpse the Baroness’s retinue, others to bid farewell to Gumbo, and all to secure parting tokens from their wealthy guests. The Baroness, returning to the home of her youth, dispensed her bounty liberally, and Harry—having lived at free cost under the Earl’s roof and won both horses and promises of money from his cousin Will and the luckless chaplain—felt honor-bound to match her generosity. “My mother would expect no less,” he thought, scattering gold as though he were as rich as Gumbo boasted. From the stately major-domo to the lowly bootblack, none were forgotten. Love had softened him toward every soul in the household; even the grim housekeeper and the rheumy old porter received their due.

For when a man loves one woman in a family, he becomes inexplicably fond of them all—indulging the maids, flattering the butler, humoring the aunts, even feigning interest in the children’s tedious performances. So it was with Harry. In the brief, radiant time since his love had been confessed and returned, he had played the gallant to all. Cousin Will might rage at his losses, but Harry, though smitten, was no fool. A Virginian knew a good horse when he saw one, and the brown hunter that had nearly pitched him into the pond upon his arrival was now his own. He had won it fairly, along with a tidy sum and a stack of Mr. Esmond’s promissory notes.

That night, after sighing at the stars outside Maria’s window, Harry retired to dream of her—and of his new horse. How sweet it would be to ride with her clinging to him, her arms about his waist! Morning came too soon, and with it, betrayal. The chaplain’s boy arrived, red-eyed, leading not the prized brown hunter but a broken-kneed bay nag.

“Mr. William rode Prince William to Salisbury Races,” the groom explained, grinning. “His last words were, ‘Saddle Cato for Mr. Warrington—he owns him now.’”

Harry’s curses were cut short by Lord Castlewood’s laughter. “Will has outplayed you, cousin.”

“Outcheated, my lord!” Harry seethed.

But time pressed. The carriages were ready, the Baroness ensconced within, and Maria, radiant in her riding habit, awaited him. With a final glare at the groom, Harry mounted the sorry beast, Gumbo trotting behind on the chaplain’s black mare.

The villagers cheered as he rode out, their hats waving. They liked the young Virginian, with his open hand and honest face. Tales of his grandparents still lingered here—how Colonel Esmond might have been Earl, had he wished it. The present lord was a hard master, and Will a scoundrel, but Harry? Harry was a prince from a land of gold.

He spurred forward, the bay’s clumsy gait no match for his eagerness to rejoin Maria’s carriage. When he drew alongside, her finger pressed to her lips—Madame Bernstein dozed within. For hours, they whispered, their words soft as the rustling hedgerows. At the inn, over dinner, their hands brushed, a glass toppled, and Maria’s gasp woke no one. Harry’s cuff had reached for hers, and she did not pull away.

By dusk, the cavalcade rolled into Farnham, where the innkeeper bowed low. “The Baroness Bernstein! The rich young American!” the crowd murmured. Harry barely heard them. His world was the press of Maria’s fingers, the promise in her eyes.

And so the descent began—not into Avernus, perhaps, but into a far sweeter abyss.

Fear not, gentle reader, for the fate of young Mr. Harry Warrington. Though his ill-fated mount—that wretched, broken-kneed beast of Mr. Will’s—sent him sprawling upon the road, rest assured our hero is not so easily dispatched from this tale. We have already mourned one Virginian; must we now bury the other before he has scarcely tasted manhood? No indeed. Heroes are not so hastily swept from the stage unless the plot demands it, and ours has only just begun to unfold.

Harry, though bruised and shaken, is very much alive. He has been carried into a welcoming household, where the bell was rung by servants of Madame de Bernstein, and there he now lies, tended with care. To prove his survival beyond doubt, here follows a letter from the lady who has taken him in—a letter addressed to none other than his mother, Mrs. Esmond Warrington, of Castlewood, Virginia.

---

If memory serves you kindly, you may recall a girl named Molly Benson, your schoolmate at Kensington some three-and-twenty years ago. We quarreled often—usually, I confess, through my own fault—but I remember still the bright, spirited Rachel Esmond, the “Princess Pocahontas” whom so many of us admired.

Time has softened old grievances, Rachel. I still treasure the gold *etui* your father gave me after our performance of *Julius Caesar* on speech-day. Only yesterday, I dreamed we were girls again, reciting lessons before stern Miss Hardwood, with you, as ever, surpassing me. How vividly those days return! The walks in Kensington Gardens, the scent of lavender in our chests of drawers (yours always neater than mine), the whispered stories in the dormitory—do you remember?

But I digress. The true purpose of my writing is to set your mind at ease regarding your son, Mr. Henry Warrington. He is here, under my roof, and though he suffered a fall from his horse, he is in no mortal danger. My husband, Colonel Lambert (a skilled hand in such matters, thanks to his army service), has set his dislocated shoulder and bled him, and the doctor pronounces him well on the mend.

Indeed, I almost wish his recovery were slower, for his presence has been a delight. Last evening, as we sat at tea, the gate-bell clanged urgently—a rare sound in our quiet home. Our servants rushed out and returned bearing a young gentleman, pale and unconscious, who had tumbled from his saddle upon the road. My husband, ever the Good Samaritan, brought him inside, where we have since tended him.

Your cousin, Lady Maria Esmond, displayed the tenderest concern for him, though the Baroness de Bernstein, being of more advanced years, was eager to continue her journey to Tunbridge Wells once assured of his safety. My husband, with a physician’s confidence, declared he would sooner trust his own treatment than that of any local doctor, and so the ladies departed, leaving Harry in our care.

He is a most charming young man, Rachel. Handsome, with a noble bearing—my own boys, fine as they are, cannot compare. When he spoke of his late brother, my heart ached for you. A mother’s grief is a sorrow no time truly mends.

Fear not; he shall soon be strong enough to travel. My husband will escort him as far as Westerham, where an old comrade resides. And since this letter will not be posted until he is fully recovered, you may trust he is in safe hands.

*P.S.* How strange fate is! That your son should stumble upon our very gate—that I, of all people, should be the one to nurse him! I often think of our youthful quarrels now with a smile. Time teaches us forgiveness, does it not?

---

Harry Warrington possessed one of those faces which inspire instant trust—bright-eyed, ruddy-cheeked, with a look so frank it disarmed even the wary. Yet let it not be said he was a mere innocent. Youth may wear the guise of artlessness, but beneath it often lurks a shrewdness keener than age’s seasoned cunning.

As he lay abed in the Lamberts’ finest chamber, Mrs. Mary Lambert gazed upon him with maternal admiration. "What a fine figure he has!" she murmured to her husband. "Our Jack might have been as handsome, had not the smallpox marked him, and Charles—well, Charles takes after you, my dear."

The Colonel chuckled. "You need not say it aloud, Mrs. Lambert. I know well enough you measure every young man against our own boys."

She flushed. "And if I do? A mother cannot help but wonder which of her daughters might suit him."

"Ah! So I have guessed it. Already you are scheming to marry him to Theo or Hester—or even little Lucy, though she is still at school."

"You know me too well," she sighed.

"Because you wear your thoughts plain as print, my love," said the Colonel, kissing her hand.

They stole from the room, leaving Harry to his rest, and descended to the hall where their daughters awaited their French lesson. The Colonel, ever the jester, chose a scene from Molière’s *Tartuffe*, playing Orgon with exaggerated solemnity while Theo blushed and giggled over her lines.

The girls adored their father’s theatrics, and their mother beamed at the sight. There is no purer joy than that of a united family—no treasure greater than the love of faithful hearts.

That night, as Harry drifted between sleep and fevered dreams, he fancied he heard the Evening Hymn, sung as if by his brother George’s voice. The illusion comforted him, and he slept deeply, wrapped in the peace of a household where kindness reigned.

Harry Warrington awoke to the gentle light of a summer morning, the sun streaming through the window, and the kind faces of Colonel Lambert and his wife smiling at his bedside. Hunger gnawed at him, sharp and insistent, and the doctor, with a nod of approval, permitted him to eat—a bowl of chicken and rice prepared, as Mrs. Lambert proudly informed him, by one of her daughters.

One of her daughters? A fleeting image surfaced in Harry’s mind—two young women, their cheeks flushed, dark curls framing their faces, hovering near his bed before vanishing soon after he had regained his senses. Then, unbidden, came the memory of another woman—lovely, yes, but older, undeniably older, with—oh, horror! He winced, a pang of remorse striking him as he recalled the artifice beneath her charms. How long had it been since he had learned that those pearls were paste, those golden tresses mere trinkets? A lifetime ago, when he was still a boy, an innocent fool. Now he was a man—no, an old man, battered by experience. The fever, the bloodletting, the hours of fasting, the draught that had plunged him into deep, dreamless sleep—all had left him hollowed out, as though years had passed in a single night.

“What troubles you, my dear?” asked Mrs. Lambert as he flinched.

“Nothing, madam,” Harry lied smoothly. “A twinge in my shoulder. I must thank you both for your kindness.”

“We are old friends, Mr. Warrington,” she replied warmly. “My husband served with your father, and I was a girlhood companion of your mother’s. You were no stranger to us, even before your aunt and cousin told us who you were.”

“Are they here?” Harry asked, his voice betraying a flicker of unease.

“They must have stayed at Tunbridge Wells last night. A rider was sent from Reigate yesterday to inquire after you.”

“Ah,” Harry murmured, glancing at his bandaged arm.

“I’ve made a fine recovery of you,” declared the doctor. “Now Mrs. Lambert and the cook shall take charge.”

“Nay, Theo prepared the chicken herself,” corrected Mrs. Lambert.

“Theo?” Harry repeated, curiosity stirring.

“Our eldest,” the Colonel explained, grinning. “She has mended your coat, cut roses for your room, and, if I’m not mistaken, sang you to sleep last night.”

“I thought I dreamed the hymn,” Harry admitted.

“No dream. Though your man attempted to join in from the kitchen, with rather more enthusiasm than melody.”

Harry laughed, but the mention of home—of his grandfather, who had loved that hymn, of his brother, now gone—tightened his throat. He swallowed the sudden ache.

Colonel Lambert, observing the shift in his guest’s mood, steered the conversation elsewhere. “You’ll find no Tories under this roof, Mr. Warrington. Our ancestors fought for Cromwell, and though we’ve softened into good Churchmen, we’ve no love for Jacobites.”

Harry’s chin lifted. “The Esmonds were loyal to the rightful king. Had we been home twenty years ago, my brother and I would have staked our heads for James.”

“Best keep them on your shoulders,” Lambert said dryly. “Temple Bar’s spikes are no pleasant sight.”

Harry’s jaw set. “I’d salute them still, even with the court looking on.”

Lambert’s expression turned thoughtful. “Then you’d best guard such sentiments. Your aunt, the Baroness, has no patience for romantic treason. She’s maneuvering her nephews into the king’s favor, and I doubt she’d welcome a rebel in her household.”

Harry flushed. At Castlewood, such talk had flowed freely. Here, it seemed, even walls had ears.

Soon after, he rose with Gumbo’s assistance—not without threatening the servant another thrashing if he continued to gossip. (Gumbo swore solemn obedience, though Harry suspected the vow would last only until the next audience in the kitchen.)

Dressed in borrowed linen—his own finery lost to his aunt’s departing carriage—Harry was led through Lambert’s home: a sturdy brick house flanked by heraldic beasts, its halls lined with pikes and muskets from wars long past. Portraits of grim-faced Roundheads glowered from the walls.

“Our ancestors,” Lambert said, noting Harry’s frown. “We were rebels once. Now we’re peaceful enough—though my son means to take orders, and my womenfolk are staunch for the Church. But come, you must meet them properly.”

He ushered Harry into the parlor, where the Lambert daughters stood to greet him. Miss Hetty, bright-eyed and impudent, dipped a cheeky curtsey. Miss Theo—the broth-maker, the rose-gatherer, the singer of hymns—blushed furiously as her father introduced her.

Harry, no connoisseur of beauty, still noted the warmth of her smile, the frank kindness in her eyes. She was no ethereal creature, but sturdy and lively, her arms sun-kissed, her laughter easy.

“And which great author do you favor, Mr. Warrington?” Lambert asked, mischief glinting in his gaze.

Harry hesitated. “I fear my book-learning is poor, sir. My brother was the scholar. I know horses better than Homer.”

Lambert clapped him on the back. “Honesty’s worth more than a library. But tell me—what do you know of Prussia’s cavalry?”

Harry’s face lit with eager interest. Campaigns, kings, the clash of armies—here was a language he understood.

And yet, as he spoke, he caught Theo’s glance, swift and unreadable, before she looked away.

Mrs. Lambert’s fleeting daydream had dissolved like morning mist. Both she and Theo, though neither spoke of it, knew their imagined hero was merely flesh and blood—no demigod, just a young man like any other. Yet in the quiet understanding between mother and daughter, there was no bitterness, only a gentle resignation.

“Never fret, my love,” Mrs. Lambert murmured, pressing a kiss to Theo’s cheek. “If he is but ordinary, then he is not meant for you. Heaven has better in store—a truer man, a nobler heart. Why, I was scarcely older than you when your father first saw me at the Bury Assembly, and I knew even then I’d have no other. If I was blessed with such a husband, why not you, my darling, who deserves a king if she should fancy one?”

In truth, Mrs. Lambert—ever the romantic—might have nursed a secret hope that some grander suitor would yet appear. The Prince of Wales himself, perhaps, riding past their gate, taking a spill from his horse, being tenderly nursed back to health, and promptly falling in love with Theo. Such were the fancies spun from the pages of her beloved novels.

Colonel Lambert and Hetty, however, stood firmly on the side of laughter. Hetty, sharp-tongued and merry, teased without mercy.

“Another Persian prince for Theo, is it?” she’d say, fluttering her lashes. “Or perhaps the King of Prussia? How many wives do they allow in Saxony, Papa?” She left *The Persian Tales* conspicuously on the parlour table, smirked at mentions of “Theo’s gentleman,” and declared she’d sooner marry Harry’s servant—the “black prince”—than endure such absurdity.

Harry, blissfully unaware, remained untouched by their jests. The arrows of wit flew past him, harmless as summer gnats. He was too occupied with his own thoughts, too unversed in the subtle arts of flirtation, to grasp the undercurrents swirling around him.

Yet for all Hetty’s mockery, the household was not so tranquil as it seemed. Beneath the surface, hearts stirred. Theo, who had felt the first faint flutter of admiration when Harry arrived—pale, wounded, noble—now schooled herself to stillness. *Not yet,* she thought. *This is not love, only a passing fancy.*

Meanwhile, three days into his stay, Harry walked with the family in Lord Wrotham’s park, where swans glided across the water.

“Mamma’s swans are like these, Papa,” Hetty announced, eyes dancing. “Only shorter-necked. And presently roasting in our kitchen.”

“Don’t be absurd,” Theo chided.

Harry, missing the allegory entirely, remarked cheerfully that he’d shot three swans back in Virginia.

He was, in truth, an innocent interloper—a stone dropped into still waters, unaware of the ripples he caused. The Lamberts, kind and lively, were unlike any family he had known. At home, his world had been one of fox-hunting planters and decorous ladies; here, conversation sparkled with wit, books lay open on every table, and laughter came easily.

Colonel Lambert, with his quiet wisdom and tales of campaigns, was a man Harry admired at once. The girls—Theo gentle, Hetty bright as a blade—were companions of a sort he’d never had. And Mrs. Lambert, warm and motherly, fussed over him as if he were another son.

No, he was not in love. But when the time came to leave, he clasped their hands with real affection, promising to return.

“He is kind,” Theo said softly, watching him ride away beside her father.

“Not so stupid after all,” Hetty conceded.

Mrs. Lambert sighed. “It feels like sending one of our own off to school.”

And as the horses disappeared down the Westerham road, Theo added, quieter still, “Just like it.”

Hetty, ever practical, was glad their father rode with him. “Better than leaving him to those Castlewood folk. That Madame Bernstein—I’d wager she’d float if they tried her in the pond, like old Mother Hely.”

“Hush!”

But Hetty was not so easily hushed. “The other one—the aunt with the fair hair—seemed fond of him. Pity she had to go.”

Mrs. Lambert, with a faint blush, admitted she had sent a bottle of her cordial water and a cake for Harry’s journey.

“I can’t help it,” she said. “I feel as if he were one of my own.”

And perhaps, in some small way, he was.

Mrs. Lambert and her daughters stood at the gate of Oakhurst, their handkerchiefs fluttering like pale blossoms in the breeze as they waved farewell to the departing riders. The gentlemen trotted leisurely down the lane, their figures growing smaller and fainter until the bend of the road, where a cluster of limes stood sentinel, swallowed them from view. That very spot had witnessed many such leave-takings—husbands riding off to war, sons setting out for school—each vanishing behind those same trees, returning only when time and fate permitted.

The quiet rhythm of their lives, the hours spent in waiting and remembrance, had nurtured in these women a tenderness as deep as it was enduring. With the men gone, the house seemed to echo with absence. There was the empty chair at the table, the whip hanging idly in the hall, the fishing rod left propped in the corner—each a silent testament to the one who had just departed. At dinner, the cherry tart sat half-eaten, a bittersweet reminder of the boy who had forced himself to swallow a few bites despite his sorrow. The evening prayers were spoken without his voice in response. Night fell, and the mother lay awake, listening to the unfamiliar stillness of a house missing one of its own.

Beyond those rustling limes lay the world—a world from which the ladies of Oakhurst were, for the moment, shielded. Mrs. Lambert often grew pensive when she walked that way in the absence of her men. She spoke once of carving their names into the silvered bark, entwined with lovers’ knots, as was the gentle custom of the time. Miss Theo, whose talent for verse was as refined as her manners, composed a poem about the trees, which her proud mother promptly sent to a fashionable magazine.

“Now we are out of sight,” said Colonel Lambert, lifting his hat as they passed the limes. “My wife never fails to watch until we round this corner. I hope, Mr. Warrington, that you will return to see Oakhurst again—perhaps when my boys are home, and the hunting is better.”

“I have been happier here than I can say,” replied Harry. “You have made me feel as though I were leaving old friends behind.”

“The friend whose house will shelter us tonight has a son who is an old friend of our family,” said the Colonel. “My wife, who fancies herself a matchmaker, once hoped to pair him with one of my girls—until the young man chose to fall in love elsewhere.”

“Ah!” sighed Harry.

“Other hearts have been lost before his,” remarked the Colonel lightly.

“I beg your pardon—the gentleman’s name was—?”

“James Wolfe,” said the Colonel, smiling. “A young man still, though he has seen more of war than most men twice his age. He is the youngest lieutenant-colonel in the army—unless you count the scores of noblemen who take precedence by birth rather than merit.”

“Naturally,” said Harry, with the automatic deference of a colonial raised to respect rank.

“Yet he has commanded men old enough to be his father, and none resent it, for he has earned his place. He is loved by his regiment, fluent in languages, and as much a scholar as a soldier.”

Harry sighed again. “I feel the lack of my own education keenly. Had my brother lived, he would have represented our name far better. George was a scholar, a musician—he could hold his own among the learned men of our colony. I fear I am but a poor substitute.”

“Knowing your ignorance is the beginning of wisdom,” said the Colonel kindly. “At home, I thought myself a fine fellow, no doubt flattered by those around me. Now I see more clearly—though perhaps I am only boasting again.” He chuckled. “Our Virginia gentry know more of horses and hounds than books. I wish I knew less of the former and more of the latter.”

“Horses and hounds are not the worst tutors,” said the Colonel. “Not every man is made for learning, yet he may still be a worthy gentleman. What need have we all to be first in the world? His Royal Highness commands, I am a colonel, and Jack Hunt behind us was once a private soldier and is now an honest groom. Each has his place. Who is to say which is highest? When I was young, I dreamed of glory. Now I think of duty—and of those we left behind.”

At Westerham, they were welcomed by Colonel Wolfe’s parents—a stately matron and an old soldier whose recollections stretched back forty years—and by the Colonel himself, home briefly from his post at Maidstone. Harry studied the man with keen interest. Wolfe was not handsome—pale, lean, with high cheekbones and red hair—but his manner was warm, his conversation lively, and his respect for his elders unwavering.

At supper, the elder Mrs. Wolfe whispered to Colonel Lambert, her eyes alight with matchmaking schemes. Glasses were raised to “Miss Lowther,” and the young Colonel drank deeply, his homely face alight with devotion.

“If she were the daughter of King Charlemagne, she would still be worthy of him,” declared the old soldier stoutly.

“Oh, if you knew her, you would say no less!” laughed Wolfe.

The evening passed in easy camaraderie, and the next morning, Colonel Lambert turned his horse toward home, leaving Harry in Wolfe’s charge as they rode on to Tunbridge.

Wolfe spoke earnestly of his longing for quiet joys—a fireside, a loving wife, children at his knee. Harry listened politely, though his own heart yearned for adventure and renown.

At Tunbridge, Harry found his aunt, Madame de Bernstein, holding court in lavish lodgings. She introduced him with pride as the heir to a Virginia fortune vast as Kent and far more fertile.

“You must prepare to be flattered,” she warned him. “The world will treat you as a prince. Do not let it turn your head.”

“But why should I be thought so rich?” Harry asked.

“Because I have said so,” she replied archly. “And because a reputation for wealth opens doors. You need not spend a penny—let them think you hoard your gold. The mothers will smile, the daughters will curtsey. When I was young, I sought advancement too. Now I am old, and cards are my only diversion. You will see high play tonight—but do not look shocked. I cannot live without it.”

Harry, remembering the crude gaming at Castlewood, felt a pang of distaste. But before he could speak, Lady Maria entered, and the Baroness’s sharp eyes darted between them, as if measuring secrets yet unspoken.

The drawing-room of Madame de Bernstein buzzed with activity as her ladyship’s guests prepared for the evening’s entertainment. Cousin Maria, radiant as the blossoms she carried, directed the placement of flowers—roses and sweet-williams arranged in porcelain vases—while liveried footmen laid out card tables with meticulous precision. The afternoon sun slanted through the windows, gilding the wainscoted walls and casting long shadows over the green beyond, where powdered ladies and gentlemen strolled in their brocaded finery. From the Pantiles, the lively hum of the Wells drifted upward, mingling with the strains of a distant band.

For those who thrived on society’s whirl, Madame Bernstein’s apartments were perfection—bright, bustling, and alive with the promise of amusement. Yet Harry Warrington, standing awkwardly amidst the flurry, felt the weight of his mourning attire and the rustic simplicity of his Virginia upbringing. His aunt’s sharp eyes missed nothing.

“You dine at the White Horse,” she declared, rapping her cane. “A young man shouldn’t loiter about old women’s skirts. And for heaven’s sake, spare no expense—you are a gentleman.”

Maria’s downcast glance betrayed disappointment, but Harry bowed and departed, his mind a tumult of pride and unease. His new lodgings were handsomely appointed, complete with a groom awaiting hire and a valet for Gumbo. London tailors, swift as vultures, presented their cards, and Harry, surveying his homespun wardrobe, felt the first prick of self-doubt. The linen, spun by his mother’s diligent hands, seemed suddenly coarse beneath the critical gaze of fashion.

At the White Horse, the ordinary was thinly attended, the cream of society having flocked to an entertainment at Summer Hill. Seated alone, Harry became the unwilling subject of conversation between two gentlemen—one a foppish baron with an exaggerated queue, the other a sharp-featured nobleman addressed as *milor*. Their French was execrable, their insinuations unmistakable.

“A Creole upstart,” murmured the baron. “The Baroness’s latest scheme, no doubt.”

Harry’s temper flared. “You forget yourself, sir,” he interrupted, voice ringing across the room.

The nobleman, startled, turned. Deafness had spared him the insult, but his companion smirked. “My lord plays with anyone who holds cards—and hears even less.”

Yet when the truth of Harry’s identity dawned, the nobleman’s demeanor shifted. With a bow as polished as it was insincere, he offered apologies. “Had I known you, sir, I should never have spoken so.”

Harry accepted the courtesy stiffly, though distrust lingered. The encounter left a bitter taste—a reminder that in this glittering world, reputation was both weapon and shield.

As evening approached, Harry resolved to meet its challenges with the dignity of his name—whether clad in Virginia homespun or the finest lace London could provide.

The White Horse Tavern’s garden stretched before them, its bowling-green bathed in golden afternoon light. Beneath the open windows of the dining room, guests lounged at scattered tables, sipping punch or tea in the warmth of the day. Having finished their meal, the three gentlemen—Lord March, Jack Morris, and Harry Warrington—strolled onto the green, where Lord March proposed a bottle of cool wine.

“Jack Morris would drag us to the Dust Hole if it meant another drink,” Lord March remarked dryly.

“Every man has his own road to ruin,” Jack retorted with a grin. “Mine happens to be a bottle.”

“And Lord Chesterfield’s is dice,” added Lord March. “Cards, horses, wagers—his lordship can’t resist a gamble.”

“Nor can you,” Jack shot back, nudging Harry. “Take care, Mr. Warrington. His lordship will play you for every acre in Virginia.”

“With pleasure,” Lord March interjected smoothly.

“For your tobacco, your slaves, your oxen—everything,” Jack continued with mock solemnity.

Harry laughed. “Fortunately, those are not yet mine to lose. I’ve only just come of age, and my mother, God bless her, is in far better health than I.”

“I’ll wager you outlive her,” Lord March said. “Name your sum.”

Harry shook his head. “You’d have to settle with her first. The estate isn’t mine to pledge.”

“Castlewood, isn’t it?” Lord March mused. “A fine prize, if half the tales are true. How did your family come by the name?”

“Through my mother’s line,” Harry replied. “An ancestor chose the wrong side in ’15.”

“Ah, yes. My grandmother knew of him. Friend of Addison and Steele, wasn’t he? Pity he didn’t stay in England—might have spared your branch the plantations.”

“I’ve just visited Castlewood with my cousin,” Harry said.

“Did you play cards with him?” Lord March asked sharply.

“Only for sixpence with the ladies.”

“But you played with Will Esmond,” Lord March pressed. “I’d wager ten to one you did.”

Harry flushed slightly but admitted to a few evening games.

“And the chaplain, Tom Sampson—was he there?” Jack Morris cut in. “God help you if he was.”

“As it happens, I won against both,” Harry said with a grin.

“And they paid you?” Jack feigned astonishment. “Miracles never cease!”

Harry merely smiled into his wine.

“You don’t betray confidences,” Lord March observed approvingly.

“I don’t speak French, my lord,” Harry replied, earning a laugh.

“Lord March learned his Italian at the opera,” Jack said. “Cost him a fortune.”

“Did it?” Lord March’s tone cooled slightly.

The afternoon wore on with bets and banter, the young men’s camaraderie as effervescent as their wine. A wager was struck over who could leap the farthest—Harry, with Virginian vigor, bested Lord March by nearly three feet, much to the company’s delight.

As evening approached, they joined the promenade along the public walk, where society’s finest mingled. Jack Morris’s loud jests drew glances, while Lord March exchanged nods with passing dignitaries.

“That’s Dr. Johnson,” Lord March murmured as a towering, scowling figure passed. “The dictionary man. Chesterfield patronized him once—until Johnson scorned the favor.”

“And the stout fellow beside him?” Harry asked.

“Richardson. Wrote *Clarissa*.”

Harry’s eyes widened. “The author himself?” He nearly bolted forward, but decorum held him back.

The walk was a parade of notables: bishops, duchesses, and the occasional infamous beauty. Lord March pointed out a garishly dressed woman.

“The Duchess of Queensberry,” he said. “A beauty in her day—now just a relic with a taste for poets.”

Harry barely listened. His attention was seized by a familiar figure—Lady Maria, gliding toward him with a smile.

“Have you forgotten me so soon, Mr. Warrington?” she asked, offering her hand.

Harry’s face burned as he bowed. “Impossible, my lady.”

She took his arm, her touch light but deliberate. “Shall we walk? Or do you prefer the music?”

“I prefer nothing but your company,” he said, too quickly.

She smiled, though her eyes searched his. The past week had altered him—she saw it in his hesitation. Once, he would have flushed with joy at her presence. Now, he was merely polite.

As they strolled, Harry’s thoughts strayed from her entirely. The thrill of the wager, the spectacle of society, even the shadow of his cousin’s betrayal—all eclipsed the woman beside him.

Lady Maria, ever the actress, kept her voice light. “Tell me of your adventures. I hear you’ve been winning fortunes at cards.”

Harry laughed. “Only a few guineas. But the day isn’t over yet.”

She pressed his arm gently, but his mind was already elsewhere—on the next wager, the next laugh, the next distraction.

The distance between them, once so perilously narrow, now stretched as wide as the ocean.

The golden light of late afternoon bathed the common as Maria and Harry strolled arm-in-arm, the crunch of gravel beneath their feet marking their unhurried pace.

“Tell me of those children,” Maria ventured, her voice soft, “the ones who tended you after your fall. I saw them darting about like sparrows when I visited.”

Harry’s face brightened. “The Lamberts? The finest people alive! Mrs. Lambert knew my mother years ago in Europe. The colonel—ah, a true gentleman, seasoned in service. They’ve been kindness itself. I must repay them somehow.” His hand drifted to his pocket, heavy with winnings.

Maria’s laugh was light, though her grip on his arm tightened. “Dolls for the little ones, perhaps?”

“Dolls! Hester and Theo are hardly children,” Harry protested. “Theo sings like an angel, composes her own airs. Hester reads Greek, I vow, and both play the harpsichord as if born to it.”

Maria’s smile faltered. “I scarcely noticed them. They seemed so young.”

“Young? You women are all alike! At home, if George or I praised a girl, Mother and Mountain would pick her to pieces.”

“I would never fault those who cared for you,” Maria murmured, eyes downcast. “Though I confess, I envied them.”

Harry, oblivious, patted her hand. “You’re too good, Maria.”

“No,” she sighed. “I am not good at all.”

Ahead, servants in livery clustered near the tea-garden, their laughter carrying on the breeze. Harry’s gaze followed them, restless.

“I’ve a mind to celebrate,” he declared. “Morris and March didn’t know what hit them. A few more leaps, and I’d have cleared six feet!”

Maria’s pride warred with worry. “You must beware of play, Harry. It ruined half our family—Castlewood, my brother Will, even Aunt Bernstein.”

Harry waved her off. “I’ll match March or Morris any day. Besides, what’s life without a little sport?”

The fiddlers in the pavilion struck up a lively tune, signaling the evening’s revelries. Maria’s shoulders slumped. “Aunt will be waiting.”

Harry barely nodded, already scanning the crowd.

---

The gaming tables hummed with murmured bets and clinking glasses. Bernstein held court in her corner, a fortress of cards and calculation, while Maria drifted between guests, her smiles brittle.

“Cut for us, Maria!” March called as she passed. “Warrington’s bleeding us dry.”

She hesitated. “You’re not playing deep, Harry?”

“Sixpences,” March assured, though Morris’s grimace suggested otherwise.

Harry’s hand was aflush with trumps. Maria lingered, her shadow falling across his cards.

“Double the stakes?” March suggested, casual as a man discussing the weather.

“If you like,” Harry agreed.

Maria’s breath caught. *This is how it begins.*

---

*Our Virginian is a marvel. He bested me at the jump, emptied my purse at cards, and now has half the Wells sighing after him. Even that harpy Cattarina simpers in his direction.*

*He’s won enough to buy a principality—Chesterfield’s six hundred, Morris’s pride, and my last guinea. The man is cursed with luck. Or blessed. Depending on one’s perspective.*

---

The candles burned low. Harry leaned back, gold gleaming in his palm. Across the table, March’s smile was sharp as a blade.

“Again?”

Harry grinned. “Why not?”

Maria turned away. The night stretched before them, endless and glittering—a siren’s call neither seemed inclined to resist.

In the genteel bustle of Tunbridge Wells, young Harry Warrington found himself an object of considerable fascination. Within days of his arrival, whispers of his Virginian wealth and recent gambling successes spread through the spa town like wildfire. Matrons eyed him with speculative interest, their daughters blushed over their teacups, and even the local tradesmen bowed deeper when he passed. His servant, Gumbo, became a minor celebrity himself, plied with ale and gossip by those eager for tales of his master’s exploits.

Harry bore his sudden notoriety with amused detachment. Accustomed to the indulgent admiration of his home, he accepted the attention without vanity, though not without pleasure. He had been raised to believe in his own consequence, and Tunbridge’s flattery only confirmed what he already knew: he was a gentleman, and the world was his to enjoy.

Yet enjoyment, as he soon discovered, came with its own perils. The society of the Wells was a whirl of cards, races, and whispered scandals—a world where reputations were made and unmade between one hand of whist and the next. Harry, with his open purse and open heart, proved an eager participant. He rode with the gentlemen, wagered with the lords, and drank with the officers, all while his aunt, Madame de Bernstein, watched with indulgent amusement.

It was she who encouraged his forays into fashion, selecting lace for his cuffs and jewels for his attire. She delighted in his triumphs, whether over a rival at tennis or across the card table. But not all observers were so charmed. His friendship with the vivacious Cattarina, a French dancer of questionable reputation, raised eyebrows—and when he was seen escorting her through the public walks, the whispers grew louder.

“Boys will be boys,” Madame Bernstein remarked airily to her scandalized niece, Maria. “I’d rather he sow his wild oats now than turn into a dullard.”

Yet not everyone dismissed Harry’s behavior so lightly. Colonel Wolfe, the stern and principled soldier, watched with deepening disapproval. When he confronted Harry one evening, his words were blunt.

“You keep company with men who disgrace their rank and women who disgrace their sex,” Wolfe said coldly. “I will not pretend to tolerate it.”

Stung, Harry defended himself. “Cattarina is harmless! She’s alone here, with no one to speak for her. Must I abandon her because small minds gossip?”

Wolfe’s expression darkened. “Do you truly not know what they say of you? That you won her in a wager? That you parade her like a trophy?”

The accusation struck like a blow. Harry had heard the murmurs, of course—the sly insinuations at the card table, the arch glances when he passed. But to hear them laid bare by a man he respected was another matter entirely.

Wolfe, seeing his dismay, relented slightly. “I tell you this because I believe you are better than your reputation. But reputations, once tarnished, are not so easily mended.”

Harry left the conversation shaken. For the first time, he saw himself as others did: not as the fortunate young Virginian, but as another reckless wastrel in a town full of them. That night, he drank more than he intended, laughed louder than he felt, and lost more than he should have at the faro table.

Dawn found him bleary-eyed and hollow-chested, the taste of champagne gone stale in his mouth. He had come to Tunbridge seeking amusement. Now he wondered—was this truly the life he wanted? Or was there something more?

The answer, if it existed, remained just out of reach.

While Madame de Bernstein found her amusement in the card rooms of Tunbridge Wells, scolding her niece and pursuing her games with the same sharp wit that had long characterized her, young Harry Warrington embraced his idleness with the unapologetic zeal of youth. At twenty, every pleasure is fresh, every diversion a delight. The world is still a place of eager anticipation, where the next ball, the next hunt, the next bottle shared with good company holds the promise of unspoiled joy.

Harry had become something of a prince among the fashionable idlers of the Wells. His pockets were deep, his laughter ready, and his skill at cards, dice, and outdoor sports marked him as a man worth knowing. He dined at the best ordinaries, discoursed with French waiters in their own tongue, and could debate the merits of Burgundy versus Bordeaux with the discernment of a seasoned connoisseur. Yet for all his extravagance, there was a shrewdness in him—a Virginian practicality—that kept him from sinking into the ruinous excesses of some of his companions.

His reputation as a gallant had preceded him, though not all the tales were true. When the scheming mother of the opera singer Cattarina—she of the earlier scandal—came weeping to his door, lamenting her daughter’s debts and her father’s imprisonment, Harry listened with polite skepticism. But when the woman, with a leer, suggested that certain *favors* might settle the matter, his revulsion was swift and final. He showed her the door with a cold dignity that would have done credit to a plantation master dismissing a dishonest overseer.

Nor did he suffer fools gladly at the gaming table. When Captain Batts, a notorious sharp, pressed him for another round of piquet and grew quarrelsome at Harry’s refusal, the young man met him with cool defiance. "I play only with gentlemen who pay their debts," said Harry, and the Captain, finding no weakness to exploit, slunk away, leaving his own markers unpaid behind him.

But it was in defense of others that Harry’s true mettle shone. When a drunken chairman insulted old Miss Whiffler on her way to the assembly, Harry knocked the fellow into the gutter without a second thought. And when the gossipmonger Hector Buckler repeated slanders about Harry and the opera dancer in the pump-room, Harry confronted him publicly, declaring the tale a lie and demanding satisfaction should Buckler dare repeat it.

Word of these exploits reached Colonel Wolfe, who had once doubted Harry’s character. Now, convinced of the young man’s integrity, he rode to the Lambert household to set the record straight. The joy that news brought was immeasurable. Hetty danced, Theo played the harpsichord with uncharacteristic brilliance, and even Dr. Boyle, their evening guest, raised a glass to Harry’s vindication.

"What shall we do now, my dear?" Colonel Lambert murmured to his wife, a mischievous glint in his eye.

"The very thing!" she exclaimed, grasping his meaning at once.

Young Charley, home from school, looked between them. "What thing?"

"Why, celebrate with supper, of course!" laughed the Colonel. And so they did, toasting repentance for those who had doubted, and to the enduring goodness of a certain young Virginian.

Thus ended Harry’s idle days at Tunbridge Wells—days of laughter, folly, and, beneath it all, the unshaken honor of a gentleman.

The White Horse Tavern was alive with laughter when Harry Warrington arrived for his usual dinner, finding none other than the jovial Parson Sampson holding court among the gathered gentlemen. The chaplain, though absent from London for months, regaled the company with the freshest gossip—or at least, what passed for it among country squires. Whether his tales of scandal at court, duels in Marylebone, or the latest toast of Covent Garden were entirely accurate mattered little. A lively story, well told and suitably wicked, was always welcome.

In those days, clergymen like Sampson were not uncommon—men who frequented racecourses, gaming tables, and playhouses as readily as their pulpits. His face flushed deeper—whether from claret or modesty—when he spotted Harry, and he stumbled forward with an eager greeting.

“My dear sir! The conqueror of spades, clubs, and hearts! How splendid you look!”

Harry returned the warmth, pleased to see the chaplain’s rosy cheeks and cheerful demeanor. A place was made for him beside a squire in a drab coat, who demanded Sampson finish his scandalous tale about Lord Ogle—though the narrative was briefly interrupted when the parson trod upon the squire’s gouty toe.

Sampson, undeterred, turned his attention to Harry, delivering news from Castlewood: my lord’s regards, my lady’s retreat to Harrogate, young Will’s lucky streak at Newmarket, and even the housemaid Molly’s tears over Gumbo. All of Hampshire, he declared, buzzed with Harry’s triumphs at Tunbridge—in cards, in society, in all manner of conquests.

As the company dwindled, Harry and Sampson remained, the bottle passing freely between them. The chaplain, though already well-lubricated, saw no reason to stop. “Plenty is no reason to deny oneself plenty more,” he declared, toasting again and again until Harry, equally generous in spirits, insisted Sampson abandon his inn and lodge with him instead. Gumbo was dispatched to fetch the chaplain’s trunk, and in a burst of camaraderie, Harry even restored the horse he had won from Sampson months before. The beast nuzzled its old master fondly, and Sampson, overcome, embraced both steed and benefactor with equal fervor.

Arm in arm, they made their way to Madame Bernstein’s apartments, their laughter and the scent of the stables trailing behind them. The old Baroness received her nephew’s boisterous companion with amused tolerance. She had a fondness for Sampson’s ribald humor—much like a rich dish, best enjoyed in moderation. Cards, however, were her true vice.

“The cards never lie,” she often remarked. “A bad hand tells you the truth to your face. And nothing flatters like a strong suit of trumps.”

Sampson, initially hesitant to join her high-stakes games, soon found himself coaxed into play—thanks to Harry’s open purse. The young Virginian had adapted swiftly to the luxuries of English life: fine lace, French watches, diamond-studded snuffboxes, and a retinue of grooms and tradesmen at his beck and call. He took fencing lessons, mastered the minuet under a French dancing master, and even defended Virginian horsemanship as superior to anything he saw at Tunbridge.

Under Sampson’s jovial guidance, Harry’s education extended beyond the ballroom. They read history, sermons, and Shakespeare together, and the chaplain assured him he was no longer the dunce he had been at home.

All this found its way into a carefully composed letter to his mother, filled with dutiful platitudes, mentions of distinguished acquaintances, and assurances of frugality—though his expenditures on horses, servants, and finery suggested otherwise. A separate, less polished note to the housekeeper, Mrs. Mountain, revealed a freer hand and warmer heart.

* * *

Your letter of June 20th reached me safely, and I thank you for your advice and the news of home. My accident on the road here was trivial, thanks to the kindness of strangers.

Tunbridge proves most diverting. I have been introduced to persons of rank—Lord Chesterfield, the Duke and Duchess of Queensberry, and others. General Wolfe, whom I visited at Westerham, spoke fondly of Grandfather’s service under Marlborough. His son, Colonel James Wolfe, is a gallant officer, soon to marry a lady of beauty here.

Your allowance is most timely, though I endeavor to be prudent. My wardrobe, while not the height of fashion, serves well enough, save for a few finer shirts for grand occasions.

Gumbo thrives on English fare and sends his duty. He has bought trinkets for the maids at home.

I have engaged masters in fencing and dancing, and Mr. Sampson, now lodging with me, assists in my studies. We read history, sermons, and Shakespeare. I met Mr. Richardson, who sends his compliments—his works are as beloved here as in Virginia.

My aunt and cousin Lady Maria send their regards. With respects to Mountain, I remain—

*What do you mean, you silly old dear, sending your dividends to me? Do you think I need your seven pounds? I’ve torn the order to bits! But I thank you all the same. A kiss to Fanny from—*

* * *

The disparity in tone between the two letters was hardly surprising. The first, polished and proper, was surely reviewed by a tutor. The second, dashed off in high spirits, bore the unfiltered warmth of youth.

And if Harry omitted certain revelries from his mother’s account? Well, what son does not, when writing home? There are always corners of life best left unlit by parental scrutiny. The sun shines, the blood dances, and youth will have its day—whether or not it makes the final draft of a letter.

The truth regarding Harry Warrington and the unfortunate Cattarina was known to our fair-minded readers, but the gossips of Tunbridge Wells had spun a far more scandalous tale. To them, the young Virginian was as wild as any rakish nobleman fresh from London’s decadent circles. Madame de Bernstein, ever inclined to believe the worst of youth, took particular pleasure in imagining her nephew steeped in vice. A seasoned cynic herself, she found it easier to credit Harry with mischief than innocence. After all, was it not natural for a young man to sow his wild oats?

At the Wells, Harry had been nothing but courteous—flattering the ladies (those of far better repute than poor Cattarina) with his easy charm, pouring tea, and even offering music to the assembly. Such behavior hardly matched the whispers of debauchery, yet the old Baroness preferred the scandal. It relieved her of the tiresome duty of guarding Lady Maria so closely. Age had dulled her vigilance; her suspicions, once sharp as talons, now dozed like an old cat in the sun. Perhaps she believed the danger had passed. Or perhaps, like many in their twilight years, she had simply grown weary of policing the passions of youth.

The rumors, however, thrived without her encouragement. When the little dancer fled back to London, it was said to be Harry’s doing—that he had abandoned her for another, whose name the gossips mouthed with relish. Tunbridge Wells was a crossroads of society: ladies of spotless virtue brushed shoulders with those of more flexible morals, all mingling under the same roof, dancing to the same tunes, sipping the same waters. A century ago, such resorts were the stages where human folly played out freely, much as they do today in grander spas across Europe.

For Madame Bernstein, Harry’s tarnished reputation was a blessing. It soothed her fears about his attachment to Lady Maria. So confident was she that when the chaplain arrived to escort her niece home, she scarcely bothered to part with her. Instead, she took perverse delight in regaling Maria with tales of Harry’s imagined escapades, whispering knowingly that *boys will be boys*, and hinting at her plans to find him a suitable bride—one of proper age and fortune, of course. Maria endured these jabs with quiet fortitude, her face a careful mask.

There had once been a certain French nobleman, a marquis whose memoirs scandalized Europe. He boasted of conquests from Versailles to the colonies, yet curiously, his exploits in America yielded no names. The New World’s women, it seemed, proved resistant to his charms—a fact he omitted with rare discretion. Travelers often lamented the roughness of the colonies, but modesty, at least, was not among their faults.

Harry had brought that colonial modesty with him. Though surrounded by men of loose tongues and looser morals, he held his own counsel. He shrank from bawdy jests, had no conquests to boast of, and flushed when others recounted theirs. His tutor, Mr. Sampson, had noted this diffidence back at Castlewood, where Harry would shift uncomfortably at Will’s ribald tales.

Now, the chaplain found himself oddly protective of the boy. The gift of the horse had touched him, and he felt a genuine kinship. *"I may be of the world,"* he admitted, *"but I’ve a little sister at school, and I keep a clean tongue around her. Let me do the same for you."*

To Madame Bernstein, Sampson sang Harry’s praises—his bright eyes, his blushes, his sturdy honesty. She listened with approval, for she was fond of the boy in her own way. He amused her. Had he been clever or bookish, she might have tired of him, but his simplicity was refreshing. *"We must find him a proper wife,"* she declared. *"He’ll not go back to his savage land, nor to that Methodistical mother of his."*

Sampson, too, had thoughts of marriage—for Harry, and for himself. A good living dangled before him if he could steer Lady Maria into a match. And so, with a mix of calculation and goodwill, he set about his task.

The chaplain was excellent company—quick with a jest, eager for sport, yet careful not to lead Harry too far astray. He drank with him, gamed with him, but kept his darker stories to himself. He played the role of jovial mentor, never pushing the boy toward true vice.

One evening, over claret, tongues loosened.

*"They’ve made quite a reputation for you here,"* Sampson remarked. *"Monsieur de Richelieu himself couldn’t rival it!"*

*"Who?"* Harry frowned.

Sampson explained—the widow Patcham’s sighs, Mrs. Hooper’s scandalous flight. The Wells buzzed with tales.

Harry’s fist hit the table. *"Name the man who says so, and I’ll call him out!"*

Sampson chuckled. *"Best not. You’d have no ears left in the county."*

*"In Virginia, a man who slanders a woman doesn’t keep his ears long,"* Harry growled.

The chaplain raised his glass. *"To your honor, sir. Let’s drink to that."*

And so they did, glass after glass, until the room swam.

*"You’ve done well to resist temptation,"* Sampson mused. *"Not all men are so strong."*

Harry groaned. *"Sampson—I must confide in someone."*

*"Ah!"* The chaplain leaned in. *"Then you do care for her?"*

Harry buried his face in his hands. *"I am miserable."*

Sampson clasped his shoulder. *"My dear boy, I am here."*

And under the warm haze of claret, the confession spilled out.

The secret that Colonel Lambert had whispered to his wife at the close of the previous chapter—one which sent the entire Oakhurst household into delighted anticipation—was now revealed. With the hay safely gathered and the corn not yet ready for harvest, the farm horses stood idle. Why not, then, harness them to the family coach and set out for Tunbridge Wells, stopping first at Westerham to visit their friend James Wolfe?

Mrs. Lambert embraced the idea—and very likely her husband as well—while the children erupted in joy. The girls immediately set about assembling their finest attire: calamancoes, paduasoys, furbelows, and all the delicate fripperies of the day. Even Mrs. Lambert’s most cherished gowns, usually reserved for solemn occasions, were liberated from their lavender-scented seclusion. The Colonel himself dusted off his laced hat and silver-hilted sword, while young Charley was fitted into a repurposed suit of his father’s—the very one in which the elder Lambert had been married.

The coach itself was a relic of bygone grandeur, rumored to have once carried George I from Greenwich to London. To the Lamberts, it remained the most splendid carriage in the kingdom. Brian, the family’s coachman (and, when necessary, ploughman), took his place on the box beside Charley, while the luggage was secured atop. Pistols were stowed within easy reach, and the blunderbuss hung at the ready—though no highwayman dared trouble their journey.

By Saturday evening, they arrived at their lodgings in Tunbridge Wells, secured by young Colonel Wolfe himself. The rooms faced those of Madame Bernstein, whose card parties were already in full swing. From their window, the Lambert ladies watched the parade of powdered beaux and brocaded ladies arriving for the evening’s entertainment.

Colonel Wolfe, ever the obliging guide, named the guests as they passed. The Duchess of Queensberry drew a quotation from Prior from the Colonel’s lips, while the Countess of Yarmouth’s grand entrance—escorted by a bishop and a peer—sparked a hushed scandal.

“Think of the Church and the nobility bowing before *that*,” muttered Colonel Lambert, his old republican blood simmering.

Theo and Hetty observed it all with wide eyes—until Harry Warrington himself appeared, arm-in-arm with a clergyman. Theo’s heart lifted at the sight of him, though he did not notice her.

As evening fell, the Lamberts drew their curtains, shutting out the glow of Madame Bernstein’s candles. Their own lodgings were quieter, filled with simple prayers rather than cards and champagne.

The next morning, the family rose early, strolling through the town before church. Hetty, sharp-eyed, spotted Gumbo at Harry’s window, preening like a peacock. But Harry himself remained unseen.

In church, the Lamberts noted the illustrious company—Madame Bernstein, Lady Maria, Mr. Richardson, and even Dr. Johnson amidst the charity children. But Harry was absent.

“He ought to have been here,” Theo murmured, earning a sharp glance from Hetty.

Later, Colonel Wolfe revealed why: he had found Harry and the parson deep in a game of piquet that very morning. Hetty flared in defense of her friend, while Theo, ever gentle, sought to smooth the tension.

By afternoon, the family walked the common. Harry, radiant with pleasure at seeing them, greeted the Lamberts warmly.

“Why did we not see you sooner today?” Theo asked softly.

Harry looked puzzled.

She pointed toward the church. “There.”

Her reproof was gentle, her smile kind. And in that moment, one could not help but hope that every wayward soul might have such a friend to guide them back to the light.

Colonel Lambert’s first impulse upon discovering his younger daughter’s secret was indignation—not at her, but at the young man who had unwittingly stolen her heart.

“Confound all reckless youths, English or otherwise!” he exclaimed to his wife. “Must this one stumble into *our* lives of all places?”

Mrs. Lambert, ever the peacemaker, offered a gentler perspective. “Perhaps Providence guided him here, my dear. If not Heaven’s doing, then whose? His arrival may yet prove a blessing.”

The Colonel groaned. “We raise them, nurse them, fret over every ache and fancy—only for some stripling to sweep in and eclipse us in an instant. A lifetime of devotion undone by a pair of blue eyes!”

His wife’s hand rested softly on his arm. “And did I not leave my own father for you, Martin? Is it not the way of things?”

He sighed. “Aye, human nature is a fickle jade. But mark my words—when our girls take husbands, I shall despise them as your father despised me!”

“What unnatural talk!” chided Mrs. Lambert. “Would you deny them happiness?”

“Happiness? Bah! It’s the parting I dread. Already you speak of Virginia as if she were packed and gone.”

His wife’s eyes grew distant. “Thousands of miles… I shall have to cross the sea. Oh, how I loathe ships!”

“Shall we order lace for the christening gown while we’re at it?” Lambert teased.

She ignored him, lost in thought. “I do hope Rachel Esmond and I shall reconcile. She was ever proud-spirited.”

---

Meanwhile, Hetty lay abed, flushed with feverish misery—not of body, but of heart. Shame and fury warred within her. She had betrayed herself, and the knowledge burned like a brand.

“What folly!” she stormed to her pillow. “As if he’d spare me a thought! He prefers painted actresses or silly milliners—as he should! I’m a greater fool than he is, and he’s dull enough.”

She railed against her own weakness, vowing never to forgive herself. When Theo tried to soothe her, Hetty turned fiercer still.

“Suppose he scorns me? I’ve given him cause enough! Called him goose, mocked his looks—though God knows his eyes are… No! I’ll not pine for a man who’s merely tall and handsome. Better to stay with Papa.”

Yet long after Theo slept, Hetty lay awake, the truth lancing her heart anew. “A punishment,” she whispered, “for my pride. But why must I suffer so? I’d have stood aside for Theo—helped her elope, even! Now I must bear this alone.”

Dawn found her at last in exhausted slumber, tear-stained but resolute. Youth’s wounds cut deep, yet seldom prove mortal. Time would tell whether this arrow, loosed by Love’s careless hand, had struck too deep—or whether the barbs might yet work free.

---

At tea that evening, Hetty’s manner toward Harry was all frost and thorn. When he invited them to a dance at the Assembly Rooms, Theo clapped her hands in delight.

“How splendid! Might we go tonight?”

Lambert chuckled. “For a colonial, Mr. Warrington, you’ve the manners of Versailles. My dear—” He bowed theatrically to his wife. “Shall we lead the minuet?”

Only Hetty sat sullen. “I’ve no taste for dancing,” she muttered.

Harry’s face darkened. “Have I offended again?” he asked Mrs. Lambert afterward.

The kind matron could only sigh. Later, Theo scolded her sister: “Why be so cruel when he meant only kindness?”

Hetty’s eyes flashed. “To vex myself, Theo! Don’t you see? I must drive him away before I disgrace us all.”

Yet when darkness fell, her defiance crumbled. Alone, she wept into her pillow. “Let him think me a shrew. Better that than pity.”

Outside, the moon silvered the eaves where Love, relentless marksman, kept his watch.

Generous with his newfound wealth and eager to play the gracious host, young Harry Warrington could do no less than invite his country acquaintances to an evening of merriment at the Assembly Rooms. Nearly every soul remaining at the Wells found their name upon his list, for Harry was determined to prove himself a man of fashion.

The entertainment was arranged in the style of the day: card-tables stood ready in one chamber for those who could not pass an evening without the rattle of dice or the shuffle of a deck; another room offered a lavish supper, complete with champagne and steaming bowls of negus; while the grand ballroom, lit by chandeliers, awaited the dancers.

The revelry, by modern standards, might seem stiff and formal. The minuet was called first, that stately dance of measured steps and bows, and two or three couples took their turns. Lady Maria Esmond, as an earl’s daughter, held precedence among the ladies (save for the lame Lady Augusta Crutchley), and so Harry led his cousin out for the opening dance. He acquitted himself admirably, his movements far more graceful than those of Mr. Wolfe, who partnered the blushing Miss Lowther. When the first minuet concluded, Harry turned at once to Miss Theo, whose cheeks warmed with pleasure as she accepted his hand. Her parents watched fondly, while Miss Humpleby of Liphook—who had expected to follow Lady Maria—fumed in silent indignation.

Once the minuets concluded, the company turned to country dances, the music provided by a modest ensemble—harp, fiddle, and flageolet—perched in the gallery. The tunes, now faded by time, might strike modern ears as plaintive, even mournful. Yet in their day, they set feet tapping and hearts fluttering. There is a melancholy in old airs, as if the ghosts of past merriment linger in their notes.

Among Harry’s guests was a duchess—none other than the celebrated Duchess of Queensberry, once the toast of London. Yet despite her rank and wit, she found herself eclipsed by another lady: the Countess of Yarmouth-Walmoden, the Hanoverian favorite of His Majesty. The countess had been particularly gracious to Harry that morning, promising him a game of cards that evening. The poor, half-blind Colonel Blinkinsop, mistaking her invitation for himself, bowed deeply—only for the lady to laugh and declare, *"Pooh! I mean the young Firshinian!"*

By nightfall, loyalty (or ambition) drew the crowd to the countess’s side. Lords and ladies, generals and clergymen, jostled for her attention. Lady Maria attended her with quiet deference, while Madame de Bernstein maintained an air of stately courtesy. Harry, for his part, bowed as politeness required—no more, no less.

Yet Miss Hester Lambert, his dance partner, took offense at even this civility. She scarcely spoke during their minuet, and when Harry escorted her to supper, her replies were curt. Passing the countess’s card-table, the lady called out, *"Does your pretty little partner like dancing?"*

*"I thank your ladyship, I do not like dancing—nor cards,"* retorted Hester with a frosty curtsey before marching away.

Harry, stung, followed in silence until his temper snapped. *"We are rough in Virginia, madam,"* he said, his voice low and dangerous. *"And we do not suffer disrespect gladly."*

For a moment, Hester faltered. She had never seen him so transformed—his eyes dark with anger, his bearing fierce. Then, with a flimsy excuse about the heat, she fled to her mother’s side.

Elsewhere, another clash brewed—this time between the Duchess of Queensberry and Madame de Bernstein. The duchess, sharp-tongued as ever, needled the Baroness over the company’s fawning loyalty to Lady Yarmouth.

*"Ah, what a Christian country we are!"* the duchess sneered. *"So quick to forgive—and quicker still to flatter!"*

The Baroness, though provoked, refused the bait. With a diplomat’s skill, she deflected the quarrel, reminding the duchess of their shared youth. The tension dissolved into a pair of mocking curtseys, and the battle was averted.

Later, as Harry recounted the evening to Chaplain Sampson, the clergyman chuckled. *"Egad, it was like two ships of the line on the verge of engagement! Yet your aunt sailed clear with all flags flying."*

*"Why should she not?"* Harry demanded, loyal to the last.

Sampson hesitated. *"My dear boy, no one escapes gossip. Even the best of us bear scars."*

Harry’s face darkened. *"If any man speaks ill of her in my hearing—"* His fist struck the table.

*"Peace, peace!"* laughed the chaplain. *"The world is full of slander. Even you have felt its sting."*

At that moment, Colonel Lambert arrived, and the conversation shifted. The colonel, ever kind, invited Harry to visit Oakhurst again—though with a curious haste, as if concealing something.

When Harry pressed to accompany him, Lambert demurred. *"Not now—we expect company."* (A lie, as he later confessed to himself.) *"But come when Tom returns! The girls will be glad to see you—even Hester, though she scolds."*

Harry, puzzled but touched, clasped his hand. *"You have all been good to me, sir."*

Yet after the colonel left, Harry’s mood darkened again. *"Confound women!"* he muttered, throwing himself into a chair.

Sampson, ever observant, hid a smile. *"Ah, which one troubles you? The little termagant, perhaps?"*

*"She treats me as if I were a fool!"* Harry burst out.

The chaplain wisely returned to his sermon. Some battles, he knew, a man must fight alone.

As for the missing pocket-book—the one with its mysterious notes and papers—Harry had no answers. It had vanished the night of their revelry, and neither Gumbo nor the toy-shop could account for it.

*"I can trust Gumbo with my life,"* Harry said bitterly. *"But not myself."*

Sampson, pen poised, quoted softly, *"‘Oh, that a man should put an enemy into his mouth to steal away his brains!’"*

Harry groaned. *"Aye, and his memory too."*

And so the evening’s gaiety faded into regret—for lost property, for sharp words, and for the fleeting company of those who had, for a time, made the Wells feel like home.

Our good Colonel and his prudent wife had resolved to remove their dear Hetty from the reach of a certain charming young gentleman, believing that distance might cure what they suspected to be a youthful infatuation. But while absence often works wonders in cooling the ardor of men’s hearts, women—ah, women are not so easily swayed. Some will wander to the ends of the earth, yet carry their affections with them, unshaken by time or separation.

A woman’s devotion is a stubborn thing. You may scold her, neglect her, even wound her pride, and still she clings with quiet constancy. Indeed, I have observed that a touch of indifference, a dash of dignified neglect, may secure a lady’s admiration far more effectively than lavish attentions. Too much sweetness palls; the heart, like the palate, tires of perpetual sugar. A woman spoiled is a woman lost.

But let us not mistake jest for earnest! Dear ladies, I assure you, I speak in irony—though, as you are well aware, irony is often lost upon your sex, which prefers sincerity even in jest. So let me plainly declare: I hold women to be infinitely more faithful, more loving, than men.

And so, what good would it do to carry Hetty away, if her heart remained with Harry? She bade him farewell with a quiet smile, concealing the ache beneath. He, preoccupied with his own concerns, saw only her composure, never guessing the sorrow veiled behind her bright eyes. He rode off without a backward glance, supposing her happy to return to her music, her garden, her simple pleasures.

But when the Lambert carriage rolled away, Hetty’s cheerfulness vanished. That night, she lay awake, haunted by thunder—or perhaps by thoughts that would not quiet. By morning, the roses in her cheeks had faded to pallor, her eyes shadowed. *It was the heat*, she said. *The storm had kept her restless.*

They arrived home at last. There was the gate where Harry had fallen, the bed where he had lain—how distant those days seemed now! The child who had once laughed among her roses was gone; in her place stood a girl who had aged years in a single night.

Meanwhile, Maria Esmond watched the Lamberts depart with grim satisfaction. When Harry returned, flushed from wine and billiards, she marked his high spirits with narrowed eyes.

"Which of the Lambert girls has stolen your heart, Harry?" Madame Bernstein teased.

"Both, aunt—like sisters!" he laughed. "A finer family I’ve never met."

Maria’s lips tightened. The Baroness, ever the strategist, urged him to court favor at Kensington, to seek advancement through Lady Yarmouth.

"Why not introduce me yourself, aunt?" Harry asked.

A flicker of discomfort crossed the old lady’s rouged face. "I am not welcome at court," she admitted. "Kings prefer to forget certain faces—and mine is among them."

A silence fell. Maria’s gaze darted between them, sensing some unspoken tension.

Later, the chaplain, Mr. Sampson, drew Lady Maria aside. "I fear Mr. Warrington is entangled in some trouble," he murmured.

Maria stiffened. "What trouble?"

"A pocket-book—lost at the White Horse. Letters inside. He is deeply troubled by it."

*Letters.* The word struck Maria like a blow. Her face drained of color.

"You are ill, my lady?" Sampson asked, feigning concern.

Before she could answer, Madame Bernstein awoke with a start. "What has happened?" she demanded, her voice sharp.

Sampson bowed. "I merely mentioned Mr. Warrington’s lost letters, and her ladyship swooned."

The Baroness’s eyes flashed. "Letters? Ah, *letters*!" she hissed, as if the word itself were cursed.

Maria, reviving, faced her aunt’s livid stare.

"You know what this means," the old woman spat. "Seals broken, secrets spilled—the old Esmond curse!"

Maria lifted her chin. "I have nothing to hide."

"Liar!" the Baroness snapped. "You think I don’t know? You’ve set your trap for that boy—just as others have done before you!"

Maria’s voice turned icy. "I have his word. And his letters. That is enough."

"His word!" Madame Bernstein laughed bitterly. "What is a man’s word against time, against temptation? You would ruin him—for what? A title? A fortune?"

Maria smiled faintly. "For *him*. And I can wait."

The Baroness, trembling, sank into her chair. The game was far from over—and the stakes were higher than ever.

Though the Baroness Bernstein had been bested in their previous encounter, she greeted her niece, Lady Maria, with perfect composure the next time they met. There was no trace of resentment in her manner—only the cool detachment of a woman who had long since mastered the art of concealment.

“Naturally, my dear Lady Maria,” she began, “you cannot expect me to rejoice at the prospect of my nephew Harry marrying a woman nearly his mother’s age and without a penny to her name. But if he insists on such folly, the affair is none of my concern. And I daresay my offer of five thousand pounds during our little quarrel was made more in heat than sincerity. So, this match was settled at Castlewood, was it? Had I known matters had progressed so far, I might have spared myself the trouble of opposition. After all, what use is scolding over a shattered vase?”

“Madam!” Lady Maria interjected sharply.

“Peace—I cast no aspersions on your honor. Harry vouches for you, and you for yourself. What more could one ask?”

“You have spoken to Mr. Warrington?”

“And he confessed to making you a promise at Castlewood—in writing, no less.”

“I have that writing, madam,” Lady Maria replied stiffly.

“Ah.” The Baroness did not flinch. “And I admit I misjudged the nature of your letters to him at first. They do, however, implicate other members of the family—”

“Who have slandered me relentlessly and sought to poison Mr. Warrington’s mind against me. Yes, I wrote to defend myself.”

“And now you fear those letters might fall into unscrupulous hands and become public scandal. Hence your distress.”

“Precisely,” said Lady Maria.

“Upon my word, Maria, I begin to admire you. It seems I have underestimated you these twenty years.”

“I am gratified, madam, that you finally grant me justice,” the younger woman replied coldly.

The Baroness’s eyes gleamed with amusement. “When I saw you dancing with Harry last night, do you know what I recalled?”

“I cannot fathom what might occupy your thoughts, madam.”

“Your performance of the same minuet with the dancing-master at Kensington, my dear.”

“That is a vile falsehood!”

“For which the poor man was thrashed, though entirely innocent!”

Lady Maria’s face flushed with fury. “To dredge up such calumny is cruel indeed. I shall not remain under the roof of anyone who repeats it.”

“You wish to leave? I daresay Tunbridge will grow rather warm for you if those letters surface.”

“They contain nothing against you, madam—rest assured of that.”

“Harry said as much, and gave you full credit. Well, my dear, we weary of each other. A separation will do us good.”

“I could not agree more,” Lady Maria said, dropping a curtsey.

The Baroness smiled. “Mr. Sampson can escort you to Castlewood. A postchaise will suffice for you and your maid.”

“A postchaise will suffice, and Mr. Sampson may escort me,” echoed Lady Maria. “You see, madam, I comply as a dutiful niece should.”

“Tell me, my dear—do you suppose Sampson has those letters?” the Baroness asked bluntly.

“The same thought has crossed my mind.”

“And you mean to coax them from him in the chaise? Delilah! Well, they are no use to me. I hope you succeed. When will you depart?”

“The sooner the better.”

“We are women of the world, Maria. We trade insults in passion, but part with civility. Shall we attend Lady Yarmouth’s card party? A change of scene will steady your nerves, and cards are better medicine than any doctor’s draught.”

Lady Maria agreed. She was ready first, waiting in the drawing-room when the Baroness descended. The elder lady noted that Maria’s door stood ajar.

*She carries them on her person,* Madame Bernstein thought.
