Together, they presented a picture of perfect cordiality at the Countess’s gathering—proof that women could feign amity even after the bitterest quarrels.

That night, as Lady Maria prepared for bed, Madame Bernstein’s maid, Mrs. Brett, appeared in place of the absent Betty, who was—shamefully—incapable of service after a night of revelry with Gumbo and other servants. Lady Maria dismissed Mrs. Brett, insisting she needed no assistance.

*The letters are hidden in her stays,* the Baroness concluded.

The next morning, Betty endured a scolding for her overindulgence in Gumbo’s rum punch. Meanwhile, Case, the Baroness’s usually sober manservant, was conspicuously absent—ill, it was said, from the same excess.

At supper, the Baroness was all solicitude. She prescribed fortifying tonics for Maria’s recurring fainting spells and urged her to consult a London physician. Then, casually, she mentioned that Harry would soon travel to the city on her behalf.

Maria’s face fell. “You are sending him away?”

“Only for two days. I wish my solicitor, Mr. Draper, to include him in my will. And since I plan to travel soon, I shall entrust him with my jewel case—highwaymen are so bold these days.”

Maria protested, hoping Harry might escort her to Castlewood instead.

“Nonsense,” said his aunt. “The boy has been tethered too long. Let him have his freedom.”

Harry, eager for adventure, promised to guard the diamonds with his life. He would lodge at an inn—some lively place in Covent Garden, perhaps.

“Ah, I once dreamed of making this journey,” he said, his voice softening.

“With whom?” asked Lady Maria.

“One who promised to share it with me.” His grief was still fresh, though time had dulled its edge.

Madame Bernstein watched him with sympathy. “He had more heart than most,” she murmured.

The next day, Harry set off, his spirits lifting with each mile. London unfolded before him—Greenwich’s grand domes, the bustling Thames, the looming Tower. Gumbo gaped at the sights, while Harry marveled at the reality of scenes he had only read of in books.

At Middle Temple Lane, he delivered the jewel case to Mr. Draper, who received it with marked interest. The lawyer, eager to play host, suggested a tour of the city. Harry, though feigning nonchalance, was dazzled by the Strand’s thronging crowds, the rebel heads on Temple Bar, and the storied dueling ground of Leicester Fields.

That evening, over a lavish supper, Draper regaled him with tales of the Esmonds. Yet Harry’s mind wandered—until he spotted a familiar shabby figure in the gardens.

“That is Mr. Johnson, the author,” he said, bowing.

Draper sneered. “Avoid such men, sir. They bring nothing but trouble.”

Harry bristled but held his tongue. The lawyer’s condescension grated, yet he endured it for the sake of his aunt’s business.

The following days passed in a whirl—Harry bought fine horses, wandered through Westminster Abbey and the Tower, and lost himself in the city’s wonders. Yet beneath his excitement lurked a restless dissatisfaction.

News from Virginia in the morning paper struck a deeper chord. The colony’s gentlemen had taken up arms against the French.

“Why are we not there, Gumbo?” he cried.

The servant grinned. “Why indeed, Master Harry?”

But the call of duty was drowned by the distractions of London. That night, as St. Paul’s bells rang for morning prayers, Harry thought of Sampson’s impending sermon—and smiled. He had learned to see through hollow words.

The storm brewing in his heart, however, was another matter entirely.

The morning’s *London Advertiser*, laid beside his breakfast plate, brought word of a spectacle too tempting for any lover of sport to resist: a duel of skill between the famed pugilists Sutton and Figg, set to take place at the Wooden House in Marylebone Fields. With the eagerness of youth and a pair of newly acquired horses harnessed to his phaeton, Mr. Warrington set off at once—though his navigation of London’s labyrinthine lanes proved less than expert. After several wrong turns past the Tottenham Road Tabernacle and the solitary grandeur of Middlesex Hospital, he arrived at last to find a raucous assembly already gathered.

The scene was a riot of color and clamor. Beggars jostled for alms, piemen hawked their wares, and a squadron of shoeblacks scrambled for the honor of polishing his boots. Above it all, a banner fluttered from the arena’s eaves, while a trumpet and drum heralded the imminent clash of champions. Inside, the air thrummed with anticipation. The gallery afforded Harry an unobstructed view of the platform where the combatants would meet, while below, his servant Gumbo vanished into the sea of footmen and coachmen, their powdered wigs and liveries a testament to the era’s excess.

The preliminaries were underwhelming—a lackluster boxing match, a cudgel bout that left the crowd restless—until at last the cry went up: *"The masters!"*

Sutton appeared first, sword in hand, offering a salute to the audience and a particular bow to a private box where a stout gentleman sat in evident authority. Figg followed, receiving an approving nod from the same dignitary. Both men were stripped to their shirts, their shaven heads bearing the scars of past glories, their sword-arms adorned with colored ribbons.

The duel began with a thunderous strike from Figg—a blow that might have cleaved Sutton’s head clean had it landed. Instead, Sutton parried, and Figg’s blade shattered from the force of his own swing. Fresh swords were supplied, and the fight resumed in earnest. Steel flashed, blood was drawn (amid much dispute over fairness), and the crowd roared its approval. When swords gave way to quarterstaffs, the battle grew even fiercer, until at last Figg emerged victorious—his legend intact.

As the storm clouds burst overhead, Harry found himself offered refuge in the chariot of his Tunbridge Wells acquaintance, Lord March. The young Virginian, exhilarated by the spectacle, could not help but wish his elder brother, George, had been there to share it—though he quickly checked himself.

*"He wouldn’t have liked this rough sport,"* Harry admitted. *"But by heaven, he was the bravest man I ever knew."*

Lord March, amused by Harry’s earnestness, prodded further. *"You truly wish he were here?"*

*"I do. And yet—perhaps not. He was the scholar, the thinker. I’m only good for riding, cards, and Burgundy."* Harry grinned, then added with characteristic candor, *"But at those, I’ll back myself against any man."*

March laughed. *"By God, Warrington, you ought to be exhibited at Exeter ’Change—a Virginian who misses his elder brother! A rare specimen indeed."*

The evening unfolded as such evenings did: a lavish dinner, wine that flowed as freely as conversation, and then—cards. Piquet was the game, and though Harry started strong, fortune soon favored March. Their play was interrupted twice: first by the arrival of Harry’s lawyer, Draper (who endured the earl’s glares with stoic admiration), and then by the far more dramatic entrance of Betty, Lady Maria’s maid, in tears.

*"Help, sir! They’ve taken her!"* Betty cried, thrusting a letter into Harry’s hands.

Lady Maria’s missive was brief and frantic:

> *"I am fell into a trapp, which I perceive the machinations of villians. I am a prisner. Betty will tell you all. Ah, my Henrico! come to the resque of your MOLLY."*

March, ever the cynic, scoffed. *"Debt, no doubt. Must you rush off tonight?"*

But Harry was already summoning a postchaise.

*"A wager,"* March declared, scribbling in his pocketbook. *"Four to one you don’t reach Bromley within the hour. Another that you’re robbed on Blackheath. And a third you’re not at Tunbridge Wells by midnight."*

*"Done,"* said Harry—and with that, he was gone, the wheels of his carriage churning the mud as he raced to his kinswoman’s aid.

My fortunate acquaintance in youth with a gentleman hailing from a certain island—renowned, so I am told, as the first jewel of the sea—introduced me to a gallery of family portraits so darkly painted that my youthful blood would curdle at the recital. Murder, theft, forgery, perjury—every sin catalogued in the Decalogue seemed the common pastime of his kin. His brother Tim had driven their father to an early grave; his brother Mick had plundered the parish church with pious regularity; his sister Annamaria had jilted a captain, eloped with an ensign, forged a will, and filched the family silver, leaving an innocent scapegoat to swing for her crimes. The house of Atreus was a nursery of virtue compared to this brood.

Yet, what struck me as most curious was not their villainy, but their tenderness toward one another. These same parricides and perjurers exchanged letters brimming with affection—*"My dearest brother," "My beloved sister"*—and lived for months in perfect harmony. Tim, his hands still reeking with parental blood, would mix Maria a tumbler of punch; Mick, fresh from swearing away his nephew’s inheritance, would kiss his sister’s cheek and weep over childhood memories of their ancestral castle. Such scenes of reconciliation! Such embraces! One almost forgot they had sworn to hang each other but yesterday.

Is there not something noble in forgiveness? Something Christian in casting aside yesterday’s curses, withdrawing the decanter flung in fury, and resuming friendship as though no ill words had passed? Some possess this angelic gift. At Tunbridge Wells, for instance, our two ladies—erstwhile foes—now sat cozily at cards, toasting one another’s health, carving the same chicken, while their tongues dissected the reputations of every soul in the room. It put me in mind of soldiers during a truce, sharing wine one moment, priming muskets the next.

Aunt Bernstein’s gout? Quite vanished, to Maria’s delight. Maria’s fainting spells? None since last night—though the Baroness murmured to her maid that such fits were surely inherited from *that scrofulous German mother*. Maria, in turn, instructed her own woman to taste every glass of the Baroness’s offered tonic, lest it be poisoned. These little precautions—these loaded firelocks at rest on the grass—were but the wisdom of veterans.

With Harry’s bond secure in her pocket, Maria granted her captive generous parole. She knew too well the follies of youth to chain him needlessly. *"Amuse yourself, cousin,"* she purred, like a cat permitting a mouse its gambols before the final pounce. And while her young knight errant roamed London’s temptations, Maria consoled herself with diversions of her own—chief among them Mr. Jack Morris, whose chief joy in life was losing money to persons of title.

Thus passed the days until Friday, when the Reverend Mr. Sampson, chaplain to Madame Bernstein’s nephew, was to deliver a sermon much anticipated by the Wells’ fashionable flock. Sampson, ever mindful of patrons, had polished his discourse to a shine, particularly as Lady Yarmouth—whose whisper could secure bishoprics—was expected. One could not blame him for nerves. Even the humblest curate stammers when a duke darkens his pew.

The Baroness, in high spirits, hosted a breakfast in Sampson’s honor. Resplendent in a new cassock (yet unpaid for), the reverend gentleman marched to church between his patronesses, their footmen bearing gilt prayer-books. At the chapel door, the crème of the Wells mingled with less savory figures—two ragged Irishmen reeking of whisky, a pair of sallow-faced Hebrews who bribed the beadle for entry with promises of conversion.

The sermon began. Sampson, ruddy and robust, mounted the pulpit—then paled as if struck. His eyes darted to the western door, where the Hebrew sentinels lurked, then to the vestry, where two Hibernian shadows now flanked the exit. How he concluded his oration, none could say—not even Sampson himself. Yet the ladies pronounced it *exquisite*, and Lady Maria waved her handkerchief in approval, though the reverend scarcely noticed, his gaze fixed on those ominous figures by the font.

At last, the congregation dispersed—save for the two Irishmen. As Maria stepped from the pew, a strip of parchment unfurled before her. *"At the suit of Mrs. Pincott, mercer, I arrest your ladyship,"* declared Mr. Costigan, with a bow. Maria’s shrieks echoed through the empty chapel as she swooned. The Baroness, with remarkable presence of mind, snipped the purse from her niece’s neck and coolly inquired the debt.

Meanwhile, Sampson, like an opossum resigning itself to the hunter, surrendered to his own captors. *"Buckleby Cordwainer’s suit—forty-eight pounds,"* sighed he, as the bailiffs led him off. Chairs were summoned; Maria and her chaplain were borne to lodgings more secure than grand.

The Baroness, ever dutiful, dispatched a note full of tender regrets—her own losses at cards, alas, left her powerless to assist. Maria, seething, pawned a gold chain to send her maid galloping to London—to Harry, her last hope. *"She shall pay!"* Maria vowed, though whether she meant her aunt or Fate, none could say.

That evening, as the Wells’ elite gathered at Lady Trumpington’s, not a word was breathed of the morning’s scandal. The Baroness played whist, serene as ever. Only when the last candle guttered did she retire—to a sleep so sound that when morning came, she woke in surprise to find not her maid, but Lady Maria herself drawing back the bed-curtains with a radiant smile.

And with that shock, we close the chapter.

"My dear Lord March," wrote Mr. Warrington, his quill scratching briskly across the page, "I am pleased to inform you that I have won all three of our wagers. My new horses proved excellent—I drove them myself, with the postilion beside me to guide the way, and my man inside with Mrs. Betty. We made Bromley in just under the hour, encountered no trouble on Blackheath (though two riders approached us, thought better of it, and turned tail), and reached Tunbridge Wells by a quarter to twelve. This settles our account after yesterday’s piquet, and I remain—Your obliged and faithful servant, H. Esmond Warrington."

Now, the reader may well wonder how Lady Maria Esmond came to burst so unexpectedly into her aunt’s bedchamber that Saturday morning, triumphantly free from her confinement. The truth was, she had scarcely hoped for Mrs. Betty’s return that very day. Indeed, she and the chaplain were deep in their midnight card game, fortified by a modest supper provided by the bailiff’s wife, when the sudden clatter of carriage wheels sent her heart racing. The vehicle halted at the door, voices exchanged at the gate—and then, radiant despite her tears, Mrs. Betty appeared, followed by a tall young gentleman.

Need I name him?

The chaplain, with a joyous shout, flung down his cards. Lady Maria, pale as linen, swayed to her feet, took one trembling step—then collapsed into her cousin’s arms with a cry that was half sob, half laughter. How many kisses passed between them? A hundred? A thousand? Let decorum draw a veil. He had come for her. She had known he would. He was her champion, her deliverer from humiliation and captivity. And as she wept freely against his shoulder, I dare say she had never looked more beautiful in her life.

No fainting this time. She leaned upon his arm as they departed, though a fit of hysterics might have overtaken her in the night. Not that Madame Bernstein heard—she slept soundly through it all.

"You are both free to go," Harry announced at once. "Fetch your mistress’s hat and cloak, Betty. And Chaplain—we’ll share a pipe at my lodgings. I’ve need of one after that ride."

The chaplain, a man of excessive sensibility, was overcome. He seized Harry’s hand, kissed it, and with tears streaming down his face, invoked heaven’s blessings upon his noble, generous patron. Harry himself swelled with pride. There is no purer joy than turning sorrow into gladness, lifting the weight from another’s shoulders. And so, with hat cocked jauntily, he escorted his rescued princess through the night, imagining trumpets heralding his triumph, flowers strewn in his path.

Vanity? Perhaps. But let him indulge it. He had earned the right.

Sampson, that night, was a study in contrasts—now weeping gratitude, now toasting Harry’s health with fervor. The chaplain’s emotions were as fluid as his principles. He loved easily, hated just as swiftly, and tonight, brimming with Burgundy and admiration, he exalted Harry as the paragon of men.

"The junior branch of our family has treated you ill," Harry declared magnanimously, "but by God, Sampson, I’ll stand by you!"

(It must be noted that when in his cups, Harry was prone to wax eloquent about the grandeur of the Esmond lineage.)

"I’m glad to have been of service," he continued. "Rely on me whenever you’re in need. Didn’t you mention a sister at school? Here—" He slid a banknote across the table. "For her tuition."

Sampson’s face crumpled. "I don’t deserve this," he choked out. "I’m a villain, sir."

"Nonsense," Harry scoffed.

"I am. That pocket-book you lost last week—I found it that night at Barbeau’s. When you were... indisposed."

Harry stiffened. "You read the letters?"

"They told me nothing new," Sampson said heavily. "You’ve had spies about you, sir—ones you’d never suspect. You’re too young to guard your secrets."

Harry’s pulse quickened. "Then the stories about Lady Fanny—and my cousin Will—they’re true?"

"Every word."

A pause. Then, low and dangerous: "And Maria? If you breathe a word against her—"

Sampson spread his hands. "I would never! Though I might think her... *unsuited* to your youth."

Harry drained his glass. "She has my word. That’s enough."

Later, when Sampson returned from fetching the letters—or rather, the empty pocket-book, his trunk having been rifled—Harry’s anger cooled. The chaplain swore oaths of eternal loyalty, and Harry, in his magnanimity, forgave him.

By noon, Madame Bernstein summoned her nephew for chocolate. She praised his gallantry, lamented her own losses at cards ("I had to send Case to London for funds!"), and pressed fifty pounds into his hand.

"But I cannot approve your entanglement with Maria," she sighed. "A boy of twenty, bound to a woman of forty-two? It is folly."

Harry flushed. "Who showed you my letter to her?"

The Baroness arched a brow. "An accident. When she fainted, her locket fell open. I burned the letter, child. For your own good."

For a moment, Harry wavered—between duty and desire, between pride and relief. Then he squared his shoulders. "My word is given. In Virginia, we do not break such promises."

The Baroness’s eyes glistened—perhaps the first genuine tears in decades. "Then go," she whispered. "But remember: I loved you best."

And Harry, torn, could only kiss her hand and vow silence.

---

Our party had grown weary of Tunbridge’s stagnant diversions. Madame de Bernstein, ever restless for livelier company, declared her intent to remove to Bath once autumn arrived, pausing only to visit a few country acquaintances along the way. Harry, with little enthusiasm, agreed to escort Lady Maria and the chaplain to Castlewood. Their route carried them once more past Oakhurst village, where Harry had once been so warmly received. Maria, however, could not resist needling him about the young ladies there—their obvious designs upon him, their mother’s transparent scheming—until Harry, irritated, declared they would bypass the house entirely. His mood remained sour that evening at the inn, impervious to Maria’s coquetry, her tears, even her sighs as she retired without the private conference she had hoped for.

Ten minutes later, a horse galloped from the yard.

When the chaplain, lingering over his pipe, was asked after Harry’s whereabouts, he offered vague replies—abed, or perhaps taking a moonlit stroll. The next morning, Harry resumed his place beside Maria’s carriage, his expression stormy. Had she confessed to stealing the keepsake? she wondered in dread.

For on their walk the previous afternoon—a steep ascent near Westerham—Maria, clinging to his arm and flushed with ardor, had whispered fervent vows. Yet the more she pressed, the colder he grew. The sunlight had cruelly etched the lines upon her face, and Harry, feeling the weight of her years—and the prospect of a lifetime bound to her—cursed his own rash pledge.

Her raptures wearied him. She quoted plays, called him her champion, swore eternal fidelity. "Ah, child! Do I not cherish your image, your hair, your very words here?" she murmured, pressing a hand to her bosom. Then came the jolt: the silken case—hadn’t Bernstein burned its contents? Maria still claimed to possess them!

At Castlewood, Harry’s temper did not improve. Only Will was present, returning from shooting, his face paling at the sight of his cousin. Supper passed stiffly until Harry, with ominous calm, inquired after his horse—the brown one won at cards.

Will feigned ignorance. "Your horse? What horse?"

"The one you lost to me the night you fled on it," Harry said.

Sampson, summoned as witness, confirmed the bet.

Will sneered. "Well, you got the other nag—sold it for thirty shillings!"

Harry smiled thinly. "A fine price for a broken-kneed hack."

The servants were dismissed; the door locked. Will, sensing danger, lunged with a knife. Harry, quicker, felled him with a blow that sent him crashing against the oak table.

"Bear witness, Chaplain—fair fight," Harry said, breathing hard.

Will revived with curses, his nose bloodied, pride shattered. The servants, loyal to Harry, showed no sympathy.

The next morning, Harry sent a mocking note, inviting Will to ride. The reply was predictably violent. Then came Maria’s letter—protestations of love, pleas against quarrels—which Harry skimmed with indifference.

His stay at Castlewood was over. A dignified letter to the lord explained his departure. Maria wept; Harry did not. Crossing the bridge, his spirits lifted. London beckoned—its clubs, its races, its thrill.

His funds, however, were dwindling. A draft on his patrimony would suffice, he decided blithely. Luck at play would replenish it. Thus resolved, he settled into Bond Street, ordering new finery, powdering his hair, and entering society with a Virginian’s swagger.

White’s Club, though not yet in season, admitted him on Lord March’s recommendation. He dined, he gamed, he spent. Far away, his mother prayed; at Oakhurst, a gentler heart still beat for him. But Harry, like the Prodigal, saw only the feast before him.

**A Letter from Mr. H. Walpole** *(abridged)*

*"A young Iroquois has descended upon London—a Virginian Esmond, no less! They say he’s bled Lord March dry. I encountered him at Vauxhall, gaping at fireworks like a child. Later, hearing his family slighted, he near drew his sword on me! A spirited savage, this..."*

And so Harry Warrington, coins slipping through his fingers, strode headlong into the whirl of London, blind to the reckoning ahead.

In Harry Warrington’s day, the debate between the merits of ancient and modern learning still raged—though our young gentleman paid it little heed. The moderns of that age are now our ancients, just as our own era will be judged by future generations. And though our ancestors may have boasted superior Greek scholars, we surpass them in science, morality, and the refinement of society.

Consider the novels of their time—*Clarissa*, *Tom Jones*, *Roderick Random*—paintings of a coarser age. Were we to depict such scenes today, the virtuous Nineteenth Century would blush and banish the offending volumes from respectable shelves. Yet, in their excesses, there was life—vibrant, unchecked, and often reckless.

And so we find young Harry Warrington in his Bond Street lodgings, swept into the whirl of London’s fashionable vices. White’s Club, the gaming tables of Mayfair, the races at Newmarket—he indulged in them all. If he brawled in the streets, lost at cards, or drank too deeply, who could blame him? The age demanded it.

Yet his letters home betrayed none of this dissipation. To his mother, he remained the dutiful son, recounting visits to Cambridge, evenings with lords, and even an audience with the Duke himself. He spoke of investments, of friends like the gallant Wolfe, of his admiration for King’s College Chapel—but never of the dice clattering across the table, nor the debts mounting in his wake.

Then came the whispers of an entanglement—an older woman, a scheming cousin, a promise rashly given. His aunt, the shrewd Baroness Bernstein, saw the danger and wrote in warning: *"She hath laid herself out for twenty husbands these twenty years past."* But Harry, proud and obstinate, would not be swayed.

So the dance continued—between virtue and vice, duty and desire. And though his mother, reading between the lines, might have wept, she could not yet see the full extent of his folly. For Harry Warrington was still young, still bold, and still convinced that the world would bend to his will.

But London, as he would learn, was not so easily mastered.

Though Harry Warrington remained steadfast in his resolve to honor the dreary promise wrung from him by his cousin, let no charitable reader suppose the arrangement sat lightly upon his spirit. Indeed, he would have rejoiced to be free of it. Perhaps the thrashing dealt to poor Will had been, in part, an attempt to hasten that liberation. Harry might have reasoned thus: *A brawl between us will surely provoke a feud with the family. Maria, bound by blood, may side with her brother. I, of course, will offer no retraction. Will, in his wounded pride, may call me out—then we shall see who is the better man. Amidst the rupture, the engagement might dissolve, and I shall be released.*

So Harry laid his plans and struck his blow—yet the explosion left little damage in its wake. Will Esmond’s nose swelled, his eye blackened for a week, but no challenge followed. The quarrel ended as so many do among sensible men: a brief, private reckoning, with no witnesses to trumpet the outcome.

Maria, far from defending her kin, championed her cousin. Even Lord Castlewood, once informed, declared Will the aggressor. The chaplain’s testimony confirmed it: Will had struck first, and Harry’s retaliation was only natural. The earl dismissed any notion of apology—let Will seek forgiveness, not Harry.

Yet Harry stood firm: no reconciliation until his horse was returned or his wager settled. History does not record how that debt was resolved, but the cousins soon moved in the same circles again without further violence.

Maria’s elder brother had once welcomed the match, content to see his sister—long past the bloom of youth, her reputation frayed by life’s wear—married off to any willing suitor, particularly a Virginian. Let her retire to his wilderness estate; Harry might console himself as he pleased once time claimed her. But after a private interview with Madame Bernstein, the earl’s enthusiasm waned. He even ventured to dissuade Maria, lamenting the "misery" awaiting Harry, bound by a rash pledge made at one-and-twenty.

*Misery?* Maria scoffed. Had Castlewood not urged this very union weeks prior? She saw through his sudden pity. Madame Bernstein had offered money to break the engagement—this was no secret. But Harry was a man of honor. She trusted him.

With a shrug, the earl retreated to his haunts—White’s, or wherever else his kind gathered. Maria’s suspicions were sharp: the baroness had indeed dangled gold to sever the match. *And so,* he mused bitterly, *my scruples profit others. While I play the guardian, others line their pockets. Why spare him? Let March and the rest pluck him bare—they will, whether I hold back or not.*

Here, the chronicler must confess: to transcribe a man’s unspoken thoughts strains credulity. Yet, knowing Lord Castlewood’s character, is it not fair to infer his resentments? Livy and Thucydides shaped speeches for their heroes; shall we not sketch the workings of a nobleman’s mind?

The truth was plain: wolves circled the lamb. Why should one hungry hunter abstain? Had not Clive himself marveled at his own restraint in India? *"When I think of my opportunities, I am surprised I took so little!"*

To expose a gentleman’s flaws is distasteful. Yet Castlewood’s peers—March and his ilk—would have stripped Harry bare without qualm. They played ruthlessly but paid their debts. Madame Bernstein, though reluctant to tarnish her nephew’s name, had heard Castlewood’s cynicism firsthand. *Why spare a lad doomed to ruin?* he’d sneered, listing Harry’s extravagances: nights at faro, jaunts to Newmarket.

The baroness, alarmed, resolved to warn Harry. She drafted a note—slowly, for she detested writing—then sent a servant to summon him. But fate intervened: illness kept her abed the next day, then engagements piled up, and Harry, ever in motion, slipped through her grasp.

When they finally met, she chided him gently for his wildness (though secretly delighted by it). He laughed—youth would be youth, and his current companions were met in her own drawing room. Pressed further, he dismissed her concerns about Castlewood. *"The world has already enlightened me,"* he said darkly.

Had he played with his cousin?

*"Lost and won, madam,"* Harry replied, gallant as ever. *"In Virginia, we don’t run to our mothers after a bout at cards or a quarrel."* He kissed her cheek, then departed in splendor—velvet and gold, lace at his throat—leaving her servants enriched by his customary largesse.

At White’s, Fortune smiled on him. Nights of staggering luck swelled his purse. He spent lavishly: fine horses, grand entertainments, gifts bestowed without hesitation. For Harry, generosity was instinctive.

And so, discovering Maria’s debts, he paid them without a second thought. Her stepmother and half-sister, though they scorned her, received lavish tokens. *"You might as well get yourself arrested, Will,"* Castlewood drawled. *"The Mohock will ransom you, even after that horse business."*

Will cursed his own shortsightedness. *"Forty pounds! I might have had a thousand."*

Maria, ever pragmatic, accepted her windfall graciously—and promptly contracted fresh debts. Her maid whispered of an impending marriage to a wealthy suitor, coaxing credit from wary tradesmen. When she rolled through London in her coach, Harry at her side, they marveled at her luck—though some questioned his taste in choosing a woman so long past her prime.

Mr. Sparks, the jeweler, arrived at Harry’s lodgings with pearls and a gold étui selected by Maria. Harry added a ring from the man’s tray, dismissed him with a regal wave, and turned his thoughts to others who had shown him kindness. For Theo and Hetty, he chose trinkets, wishing he possessed his brother’s gift for verse. *"Goodwill alone cannot make a poet,"* he sighed, then enlisted Parson Sampson to craft a suitable note.

Thus flowed the days of the Fortunate Youth—gold streaming through his hands, laughter on his lips, and seldom a thought for tomorrow.

Here is the rewritten chapter, maintaining the historical fiction style and spirit of the original while refining the prose for modern readability:

---

Mr. Harry Warrington, the so-called Fortunate Youth of Virginia, now resided in splendid lodgings on Bond Street, living lavishly upon the finest fare London could offer, his table never lacking the best wines, his company ever filled with the loftiest names of the town. Youth, wealth, good looks, and luck—these were his companions, and society welcomed him with open arms.

Let us not too harshly condemn the world for favoring such a one while turning a cold shoulder to those less blessed. If a man may choose his friends, why should society—being but the sum of all men—not do the same? Harry was liked because he was likable: rich, handsome, well-born, and well-mannered. He drank jovially with those who loved a song and a bottle; he rode boldly with gentlemen of the field; he blushed becomingly in ladies’ company; and to those beneath him in station, he was ever generous, sparing them any humiliation his rank might permit.

True, our young Virginian carried himself with lofty pride, but in those days, when distinctions of birth were sharply observed, such hauteur was expected, not resented. A Secretary of State knelt before his King; an Under-Secretary stood in his superior’s presence. There were lines then drawn which few dared cross—lines now blurred, to the dismay of many a gentleman who finds himself clapped on the back by men who ought to know their place.

For Harry, London in the winter of 1756-57 offered every diversion. There were operas, though he cared little for them (despite the outcry against their foreign frivolity, the houses remained full). There were plays, with Garrick and Pritchard at one theater, the divine Clive at another. There were masquerades, ridottos, assemblies where cards ruled the evening—though Harry preferred White’s, where the play ran higher and fairer.

One day, his cousin Lord Castlewood presented him at Court. The King, newly arrived from Kensington, received him coldly.

"Heard of you from Lady Yarmouth," muttered His Majesty in German to Castlewood. "Tell him he plays too deep."

With that, the royal back turned.

"What did he say?" Harry asked.

"His Majesty disapproves of the stakes at White’s," whispered Castlewood, pale.

"Then we shan’t trouble him again," said Harry, shrugging. "I never thought much of German kings for English thrones."

"For God’s sake, hold your tongue!" hissed Castlewood. "These walls have ears!"

"And what of it?"

"Look around you! An hour ago, these men bowed and smiled. Now they avoid me like the plague!"

A broad-shouldered gentleman in a scarlet-laced waistcoat thrust out a hand. "Shake, nephew. I heard you. My name’s Sir Miles Warrington. Your father’s brother."

Castlewood, paling further, confirmed it.

"Might’ve visited us in Norfolk instead of dawdling at Tunbridge Wells," growled Sir Miles. "Or do you call yourself Esmond now?"

"My mother is proud of her father’s name, and so am I," Harry retorted.

"Well said! Come dine with us in Hill Street—roast mutton, none of your White’s kickshaws. And don’t fret, my lord," he added, seeing Castlewood’s unease. "I’ll keep your secrets."

At dinner, Lady Warrington—stately at first—soon confessed she had expected a far worse rogue, given his reputation. The young ladies offered polite curtsies, while the heir, young Master Miles, blurted:

"Cousin Harry, are you truly wicked? Papa says you drink and game and keep bad company!"

"A little pitcher with big ears," laughed Sir Miles, dispatching the boy.

Over port, the Baronet grew frank. "My wife’s taken to Methodists of late. One Ward, a preacher, filled her head with tales of you and your brother."

"Speak of me as you please," Harry flared, "but if that fellow slanders my brother George again, I’ll thrash him!"

"Loyalty becomes you," said Sir Miles, refilling his glass. "To your mother’s health!"

Harry drank, though the toast pricked his conscience. Later, he sent trinkets to the girls "from their aunt in Virginia," a kindness that warmed him—until Colonel Lambert arrived one afternoon, grim-faced.

"Your gifts are too costly for a man of modest means," said the Colonel, laying them aside. "My daughters cannot accept what was won at cards."

Harry stiffened. "You question my honor?"

"I question nothing but the wisdom of gaming. At Court today, they spoke of your winnings—thousands here, thousands there. Such talk follows you."

"Must I account for every shilling?" Harry’s voice turned icy.

"Only to yourself. But Theo and Hetty cannot wear trinkets bought with dice."

The words struck like a blow. "You return my kindness with insult?"

"With truth. Keep your gifts for those who do not love you."

"Then take your leave, sir!" Harry’s hand trembled as he pointed to the door. "I’ll not endure such reproach from any man!"

The Colonel’s face fell, but he turned without another word.

Alone, Harry stared into the fire, the taste of triumph ashen in his mouth.

---

This version tightens the narrative while preserving Thackeray’s wit and the chapter’s themes of pride, reputation, and the perils of excess. The dialogue remains sharp, and Harry’s hubris is clear as his fortunes begin to waver. Let me know if you'd like any further refinements!

The rejection of his peace offerings stung young Harry Warrington like a lash. Wounded pride flared into anger—not only against Colonel Lambert but the entire family. *They humiliated me before the girls,* he fumed. *First they preach morality, then scorn my gratitude as if I were some scoundrel unworthy of their company.*

Parson Sampson, ever the sycophant, eyed the morocco boxes with covetous admiration. "Why, sir, these trinkets must be worth a fortune!"

"They cost enough," Harry muttered, though the expense mattered little to him—or so he told himself.

"The repeater alone is a hundred pounds," he said, recalling Hetty’s wistful remark about desiring such a watch.

"Oh, what a prize!" Sampson simpered. "A lady’s heart could be won with such a trinket!"

Harry’s jaw tightened. "Perhaps I should toss it to the next apple-woman who passes."

Business in the City saw Harry withdraw a substantial sum from his dwindling funds. The broker, ever the opportunist, led him to a bustling dining-house before guiding him through the Virginia merchants’ walk on ’Change. Every glance felt like judgment. *They know,* Harry thought. *They whisper of Madam Esmond’s son, squandering his patrimony at the tables.*

By afternoon, he was back at White’s, where idleness bred temptation. Lord Castlewood lounged over the *Public Advertiser*, yawning.

"Piquet?" Castlewood suggested.

"An hour," Harry agreed.

The game stretched longer. The stakes climbed higher.

By evening, the club’s whispers followed him:

"Fortunatus has lost again."

"Thirteen hundred last night. How much tonight?"

Harry left in a storm of self-loathing. The losses mounted—his ready money, his patrimony, everything.

Dawn brought no comfort. The weight of his folly pressed upon him like a physical ache. Beneath his pillow, the repeater watch—meant for Hetty—chimed mockingly.

The thought of Virginia, of home, was a knife’s twist.

Sampson arrived, groveling as ever—until Harry revealed the night’s disaster.

"Every shilling gone," Harry admitted bitterly.

The parson’s face crumpled. "My landlord—his family—they’ll be ruined! I borrowed against your promise!"

Harry’s anger flared, then cooled. He reached for his mourning coat, not the fine damask of recent weeks.

"Here." He thrust a purse at Sampson. "Eighty guineas. All I have left."

The parson fell to his knees, weeping gratitude. Harry endured the display, torn between satisfaction and disgust.

Outside, the drums of the Guards echoed at Charing Cross. *At least I can shoulder a musket,* Harry thought grimly.

He strode to Sampson’s lodgings, where the chaplain’s despair was palpable.

"Castlewood owes me three years' salary," Sampson confessed. "He gave me five guineas—claimed it was all he had."

Harry’s lip curled. "He won eleven hundred from me last night."

The truth settled between them.

Harry pressed the money into Sampson’s hands. The parson’s effusions were excessive, but the relief in the household was real.

As Harry walked away, the weight of his losses lingered—but so did the quiet satisfaction of one decent act.

For now, it would have to be enough.

The world is full of men who wear their virtues like a cloak, yet beneath the pious fabric hides a heart untouched by faith. We have read of such creatures in books—the sanctimonious preacher who thunders damnation while his own soul rots, the widow who weeps over scripture yet counts her coins with greedy fingers. But in truth, I have met few of these monstrous hypocrites in life. The common sinner is far simpler: the maid dreaming of her sweetheart during prayers, the merchant tallying debts as the psalm is sung, the schoolboy fidgeting through the sermon, his mind fixed on supper. None are saints, yet none pretend to be. Their religion is a quiet thing, kept like a remedy in a cupboard—brought out in illness, recommended to others, but seldom tasted in health.

Lady Warrington was such a dispenser of moral physic. Convinced of her own righteousness, she dosed her household with unrelenting virtue. Her daughters—Flora the Beauty and Dora the Genius—were paragons, polished by maternal exhortation until they shone. To Harry, fresh from the riot of London’s pleasures, their household was a haven of order. Sir Miles boomed with jollity, clapping him on the back; Lady Warrington beamed with maternal solicitude, pressing hymns into his hands. The girls, dutiful and bright, entertained him—Flora with her practiced smiles, Dora with earnest lectures on self-improvement.

Yet beneath this tranquil surface, Harry’s fortunes had crumbled. His luck at the tables had deserted him. Castlewood, his own kin, had stripped him clean. Even the trinkets set aside for poor Sampson had vanished into the pawnbroker’s clutches. Now bailiffs lurked in the shadows, and Harry, still playing the prosperous gentleman, sat in Hill Street, sipping watered wine while his aunt extolled his virtues.

*"We must reclaim you, dear Harry!"* Lady Warrington sighed, gazing heavenward. *"You have strayed, but love will guide you back."*

Love, perhaps—or necessity. For that very night, as Harry departed, full of cousinly kisses and paternal blessings, the law seized him. A grimy hand clapped his shoulder; a voice declared him prisoner. The scion of Virginia, who had dined with lords and charmed countesses, was hauled to a spunging-house in Cursitor Street.

There, in the stench of stale drink and the jeers of hardened debtors, Harry Warrington faced his ruin. No velvet, no lace, no adoring kin to shield him now. Only the dark, the rats, and the relentless tally of his folly.

Let him lie there, this prodigal. Let him gnash his teeth on the bitter bread of consequence. For youth must learn, and the school of shame has hard masters.

Harry Warrington, once so proud and untouchable, now found himself locked within the cold walls of a debtor’s prison, his arrogance humbled by iron bars and the mocking indifference of the world. Remorse gnawed at him—not for the debts, but for the indignity of capture. Surely, he thought, his friends would rally. His uncle, Sir Miles, who had welcomed him as a son; his cousin Castlewood, who had bled him dry at cards; his noble acquaintances at White’s—any one of them would surely intervene. Yet beneath this confidence lurked a deeper dread: the fear of becoming a spectacle.

The world, after all, cared little for a gentleman’s disgrace. A man might stumble, might even fall, and the crowd would scarcely pause before moving on. Harry knew this, yet vanity still whispered that his ruin would echo through London’s drawing rooms. He imagined the sneers of Selwyn, the whispered jests at the Savage’s expense. The thought was unbearable.

At dawn, he dispatched a messenger to his one reliable confidant—the ever-resourceful, ever-indebted chaplain, Sampson. If anyone understood the art of evading creditors, it was he. But as the hours crawled by, no Sampson appeared. Instead, a letter arrived, its ink barely dry.

*"Oh, sir, I have done all a man can for his patron!"* Sampson wrote, his desperation palpable. *"Did you not know to what you subjected me? Else, why am I locked but three doors away, seized by the same wolves who hunt you?"*

The irony was bitter. Sampson, his would-be savior, was now as trapped as he.

Meanwhile, Gumbo arrived, wailing like a tragedian, swearing eternal loyalty—though his tongue had already wagged the tale to every servant in Mayfair. The news had reached Lady Maria, who, upon hearing of Harry’s plight, turned her fury upon her brother.

"You took his last shilling, and now you leave him to rot?" she raged.

Castlewood merely shrugged. "Every man plays his own game, sister. Yours was to snare him as a husband. Mine was to take his money. Neither of us is a saint."

Maria’s face flushed with fury, but Castlewood’s smirk never wavered. "A clever woman," he mused, "might turn even this to her advantage. Suppose a certain promise of marriage were to be… reconsidered?"

Her eyes narrowed. "You think me so mercenary?"

"I think you practical," he replied.

Across town, Madame Bernstein received the news with icy calculation. Harry’s recklessness was no surprise, but his arrest was an inconvenience—one she would resolve, though not without conditions.

"Pay his debts," she instructed her lawyer, Draper. "But when he walks free, let him walk clear. No more liabilities. No more folly."

Draper bowed. "And if there are other creditors?"

"Then let them wait," she said. "A lesson must be learned."

Harry, pacing his cell, knew none of this. He knew only the slow passage of time, the sting of humiliation, and the dawning realization that pride was a luxury he could no longer afford.

The world had not ended. It had merely moved on without him.

Mr. Gumbo’s loyalty had been a balm to his master’s wounded spirit, and Harry Warrington’s second night in the spunging-house passed more peacefully than the first. Yet though the young man’s anger had cooled toward his faithful servant, it burned all the fiercer against the world beyond his prison walls. His Aunt Warrington’s sanctimony, Lord March’s indifference, Castlewood’s mocking detachment—all had proven hollow. Never again, he swore, would he debase himself by begging favors from such false friends.

Fool that he had been, to trust their promises! This cold, grasping England had no room for honesty. Better to flee—to Germany, to fight beside the King; or home to Virginia, where he might vanish into the wilderness, hunt, tend his mother’s lands, wed some plain, good-hearted girl, and leave these perfumed aristocrats to their scheming.

Morning found him dressed with care, his pride armor against humiliation. Gumbo arrived with letters from Bond Street—mostly bills, and a curt note from his landlord, threatening seizure of his belongings unless debts were settled. The servant himself had been turned away, finding shelter with friends at Lord Wrotham’s.

"Colonel Lambert’s people," Gumbo added meaningfully. "Miss Hetty took ill when she heard you was taken up. The Colonel says if you need him, send word, and he’ll come."

Hetty’s distress touched Harry, though he doubted the tale of her swooning. But the mention of Lambert reignited his bitterness. Ask help from the man who had scorned him? Never—even if he starved.

Breakfast dispatched, he penned a terse summons to his attorney, Mr. Draper, whose arrival did little to soothe his temper. The lawyer, affronted by Harry’s unbroken hauteur, resolved to humble him.

"Your friends are deeply concerned," Draper began unctuously, "that a gentleman of your station should face such disgrace."

"Disgrace?" Harry’s jaw tightened. "I’ve been reckless, not dishonest."

"Yet pawning watches you never paid for might suggest otherwise to some."

The insinuation struck like a slap. Harry surged to his feet. "You dare accuse me of fraud?"

Draper retreated a step but pressed on, relishing his advantage. "Merely stating how the law may view it. Your aunt, however, stands ready to settle your debts—on conditions."

Conditions. The word hung like a blade.

"Name them."

"The marriage—that unfortunate promise to a certain lady—must be renounced."

Harry flushed. The memory of his boyish pledge to Maria Esmond was torment enough without Draper’s smirk.

"Her ladyship has your written vow, recovered by chance," the lawyer continued. "Burn it, and your freedom is bought. Refuse, and you rot here."

The choice was clear, yet Draper’s gloating made acceptance bitter. Harry turned away. "I need an hour."

"An hour?" Draper’s smile curdled. "Most men would leap at such mercy."

"Then come back, and I shall answer."

Snubbed, the lawyer departed, fuming.

When he returned, dusk had fallen. A cloaked figure brushed past him on the stairs—a woman, swiftly gone. Inside, Harry stood firm.

"Well?" Draper demanded.

"I’ll not be bargained with like a coward. Tell my aunt I thank her—but my debts are my own."

Draper gaped. "You fool! You’ll hang rather than bend?"

"Better hanging than dishonor."

The door slammed on the lawyer’s curses. Alone, Harry smiled grimly. Chains might hold his body, but his spirit remained unbroken.

---

*Note: The rewritten chapter tightens the dialogue, amplifies Harry’s internal conflict, and heightens the tension between pride and pragmatism. Draper’s oily manipulation contrasts with Harry’s defiant integrity, while fleeting hints (the mysterious woman, Hetty’s distress) preserve intrigue for future revelations.*

Harry Warrington sat brooding in his prison chamber, his pride wounded, his spirit simmering with resentment. The lawyer Draper had come and gone, leaving behind him a sting of humiliation. Harry clenched his fists, replaying the bitter exchange in his mind. He had been trapped—by Maria, by her brother, by his own reckless trust. They had preyed upon his youth, his generosity. Had they ventured to Virginia, he would have shown them a far different welcome.

A knock at the door startled him. Gumbo’s grinning face appeared, announcing a visitor. Before Harry could react, a figure swept past the servant—a lady in a capuchin, her face shadowed by the hood. She rushed to him, her hands trembling as she grasped his.

"Oh, Harry!" she cried, her voice breaking. "Did I ever think to see you like this?"

He recoiled, but she sank beside his bed, pressing his feverish fingers to her lips. Tears streaked her cheeks as she gazed at him with desperate tenderness.

"I—I thought none of you would come," Harry muttered, his voice hoarse.

Her answer was a flood of whispered endearments, her hands clutching his as if she could will strength into him. "I cannot bear to see you suffer," she wept. "Some of this is my fault—my greed, my vanity! I brought them all back—every trinket, every jewel you gave me."

From her reticule, she spilled brooches, rings, a watch, a handful of guineas—all into his lap. Harry’s throat tightened. His pride warred with gratitude, but the sight of her sacrifice undid him.

"Dearest cousin," he murmured, his defiance crumbling.

She hushed him, smoothing his hair as a mother might. "Do not speak. Only know that you are not forgotten."

For a time, they spoke in quiet tones—of debts, of regrets, of small hopes kindled anew. The shadows lengthened before she rose, pressing a final kiss to his brow.

When she left, the room felt colder. But Harry’s heart was lighter.

Then came Draper, his entrance like a gust of foul air.

"Your servant, sir," the lawyer sneered. "Have you packed your trunk?"

Harry stiffened. "I have reconsidered. My word is given."

Draper scoffed. "She lost the paper! There’s nothing binding you!"

"Enough!" Harry’s voice cracked like a whip. "You mistake me for one of your pettifogging clients. I keep my promises."

The lawyer’s face purpled. "Promises? To a scheming old—"

Harry was on his feet in an instant. Draper scrambled back, sputtering threats before fleeing.

Alone again, Harry exhaled sharply. He would not betray her trust, even if it meant rotting in this cell.

Meanwhile, the Baroness de Bernstein waited in her dining room, the untouched meal before her. The chair meant for Harry stood empty.

"Eight o'clock," murmured her servant. "You must eat."

She waved him away. "Bring coffee."

But when the door opened again, it was not the servant who entered.

A figure stood in the threshold—tall, unmistakable in resemblance. The Baroness froze.

"Mr. Warrington!" she gasped.

The stranger bowed. "You expected my brother, madam?"

Her breath hitched. "Who—?"

"I arrived in London tonight. His servant gave me this letter for you." He extended it, his gaze piercing. "I am George Warrington. And I have come to ask—what have you done with my brother?"

The Baroness swayed. "He was to dine here—that chair—!"

George’s expression hardened. "Then I will find him."

"Bring him back!" she pleaded. "Take whatever you need—money, influence—!"

But George was already turning. "Keep your gold, madam. I want only my brother."

With that, he strode into the night, leaving the Baroness trembling in the flickering candlelight.

The hackney coach rattled through the dim streets of London, carrying George Warrington on a desperate errand—to free his brother from the clutches of debt and disgrace. The journey through the Strand and Fleet Street seemed interminable, the roads little more than rivers of mud under a starless sky. The feeble glow of link-boys’ torches flickered in the darkness, illuminating potholes deep enough to swallow a wheel. To George, every jolt and delay was torment. His heart pounded with urgency.

He had written to Harry from Bristol, announcing his return. He had sent word months before, declaring himself alive after the long, mistaken death at Braddock’s defeat. But fate had intervened—war between England and France meant letters vanished into the holds of captured ships. The message that might have spared Harry this shame had never reached him.

Now, standing before Harry’s abandoned lodgings, George felt a chill of foreboding. The maid had taken one look at him—so like his brother—and fled with a shriek. The landlady, tight-lipped, claimed ignorance of Harry’s whereabouts. But George was no fool. He knew Clarges Street lay close to Bond Street, and Bond Street was home to their formidable aunt, Baroness Bernstein.

Aunt Bernstein—Beatrix—had shown Harry kindness, or so his letters claimed. Even their mother, Rachel Esmond, who had long scorned her eldest daughter, had softened at the news. But forgiveness had its limits. Some sins, especially those of a woman’s past, were never forgotten.

Gumbo, trembling with shock at the sight of his resurrected master, stammered out the truth. Harry was imprisoned, ensnared by debt. The revelation hardened George’s resolve. He stormed to his aunt’s house, demanding answers. Her distress, though carefully veiled, betrayed her. She had not abandoned Harry entirely.

Now, as the coach lurched toward the bailiff’s house, George’s anger cooled into resolve. He would free his brother, no matter the cost.

---

The bailiff’s dwelling was a grim place, its doors locked and guarded by a wary-eyed Jew with a sputtering torch. Inside the cramped office, two men waited—Colonel Lambert and Lieutenant-Colonel James Wolfe, officers of rank and honor.

“We are not angry any more, Harry!” Lambert declared cheerfully, mistaking George for his brother in the dim light. Then he froze. “Angels and ministers of grace! Who are you?”

George seized his hand. “God bless you, sir! You must be Colonel Lambert—Harry wrote of your kindness. I am his brother, given up for dead after Braddock’s defeat. I have come back to thank you—and to bring him home.”

Lambert’s face transformed. “James! Here is news! The dead are alive!”

Wolfe, stern but not unkind, stepped forward. “Your brother has been wayward, Mr. Warrington. But Lambert here would not abandon him.”

“Nor shall I,” George vowed. He turned to the bailiff. “Name the debt.”

The sum was steep—over five hundred pounds. Without hesitation, George drew out his purse. “It is paid tonight.”

Lambert gripped his shoulder. “You do your family proud, sir.”

---

Upstairs, Harry sat in gloom, wrestling with regret. His reckless choices had led him here. Worse, Maria’s devotion weighed on him like a chain.

Then Gumbo burst in, eyes wild. “Mas’r Harry—ghost! Ghost downstairs!”

“What nonsense—”

But the door flew open. George stood there.

For a heartbeat, neither moved. Then Harry stumbled forward, and the brothers embraced—the prodigal reclaimed, the lost restored.

Lambert and Wolfe discreetly withdrew. Some reunions needed no audience.

---

Later, as they left the bailiff’s house, Harry exhaled shakily. “I don’t deserve this.”

George slung an arm around his shoulders. “No. But family doesn’t keep accounts.”

The night air was cold, but the road ahead was clear.

When first we were introduced to Sir Miles Warrington at the King’s drawing-room in St. James’s Palace, his round, jovial face, broad waistcoat, and hearty country manner led me to believe we had encountered the very model of an English gentleman—a figure praised in ballads and toasted in taverns, the very pinnacle of earthly contentment.

To be such a gentleman is to occupy a station just shy of divinity. Imagine: an unencumbered estate, rents paid promptly by grateful tenants who bless their luck in having so benevolent a landlord; a life of hunting three days a week, robust health, and a perpetual appetite; a pew at church where the parishioners whisper blessings as you pass, the first man in the parish, the benefactor, the moral exemplar. Could such a man truly call himself a *miserable sinner*? Nonsense! Give a man health, wealth, admiration, and the devotion of his family, and you will find no humility in him. Gout and solitude may humble a prince, but in the full flush of vigor and prosperity, misery is but a word in the prayer book.

Sir Miles was such a man—content, self-satisfied, convinced of his own goodness. His wife was the very pattern of matronly virtue, his children obedient, his tenants deferential. When his grand coach rolled through the market square, hats flew off, curtseys dipped, and tradesmen fawned. The ladies within dispensed gracious smiles and condescended to purchase a yard of ribbon or a packet of seeds, as if their patronage were a royal favor.

But what if those smiles were hollow? What if the poor hid their geese when her ladyship passed, knowing she would demand them at half-price? What if the charity broth were watered down and the tenants’ beer thin as dishwater? Such gentlemen and ladies existed—pious in form, parsimonious in fact, performing virtue while practicing thrift.

Take, for instance, young Harry Warrington’s recent misfortune. When he appealed to his relations for aid, his aunt sent him a tract and her blessing; his uncle was *unavoidably detained* by business out of town. How often does this scene play out among the respectable? Macbeth might shudder after his crime, but Lord and Lady Skinflint sleep soundly after refusing a kinsman in need. No remorse troubles them—only indignation that the boy should be so reckless.

*"A fortune wasted among gamblers and actors!"* lamented Lady Warrington. *"Let this be a lesson, my dears!"*

*"Dreadful, dreadful!"* echoed Sir Miles, shaking his head.

The children, well-trained in morality, chimed in with dutiful horror—all except young Miles, who, with the brutal honesty of childhood, declared, *"I like Harry. If he’s in prison, I’ll give him my half-guinea!"*

But then—a revelation! News arrived that Harry was no penniless outcast, but the victim of a case of mistaken identity. His brother, George, the *true* heir, had arrived, and the two had been seen at Ranelagh, so alike that only their coat colors distinguished them.

*"An impostor!"* roared Sir Miles. *"A beggarly second son!"*

The virtuous indignation was swift. Lady Warrington clutched her bosom. *"To think we harbored a deceiver!"* The sisters gasped. Only little Miles remained loyal, weeping at the injustice until the butler announced the arrival of the two young men themselves.

George, grave and composed, bowed with icy courtesy. Harry, flushed and defensive, stood by his brother. The family’s welcome was as stiff as their starched collars.

*"We dine elsewhere today,"* said George, cutting short Sir Miles’s forced joviality.

*"Oh? With those country Lamberts?"* sneered one sister.

*"They are the best people in England!"* Harry burst out, his loyalty undimmed by their scorn.

The ladies exchanged glances. *"How very charitable,"* murmured Dora.

As the brothers departed, the family dissected their characters anew. *"The elder seems more sensible,"* mused Lady Warrington. *"The younger is clearly reckless."*

*"Or perhaps,"* said Dora with a smirk, *"they are both as poor as each other."*

And so the evening passed—in moral reflections, veiled insults, and the occasional petty quarrel—while the virtues of economy, prudence, and self-righteousness were upheld with unwavering conviction.

The reader is kindly invited to cross the way with us to Lord Wrotham’s house, presently lent to General Lambert, where our young gentlemen find themselves gathered among familiar faces—those very friends encountered at Oakhurst and Tunbridge Wells. James Wolfe, though expected for dinner, is conspicuously absent, no doubt lingering in the company of Miss Lowther, for whom a single glance holds more allure than the finest dishes Lord Wrotham’s kitchens might prepare. You, dear reader, shall take his place as the sixth at table—though we may be certain the enamored lieutenant-colonel will not appear. As for myself, I shall stand aside, a silent observer, ready to recount the evening’s discourse.

First, mark the radiant contentment upon the ladies’ faces! When Harry Warrington fell into the clutches of bailiffs, I had meant to describe how the tender-hearted Mrs. Lambert, upon hearing of his plight, rushed to her husband, imploring, insisting—nay, commanding—that he aid the boy.

“Never mind his rebellious words the other day,” she pleaded. “Never mind his pride over those returned gifts—what young man of spirit would not have taken offense? And though we’ve little to spare, with our own boys at college, surely there must be some way to free him! Did you not once rescue Charles Watkins from debt? And did he not repay every penny? Think of the good you did then—how that family still blesses your name! I dare say it was such kindness that brought you favor—why, I’ve no doubt it helped make you a major-general!”

Now, Martin Lambert, ever a man swayed by compassion, needed little persuasion. Resolving to seek out his friend James Wolfe and stand bail for Harry, he took his leave, pressing Theo’s hand (who, perhaps through maternal indiscretion, seemed to divine his purpose) and kissing Hetty’s flushed cheek before departing. His wife, unable to contain herself, followed him into the hall.

Once alone, her restraint dissolved. She flung her arms about his neck, showering him with kisses, calling down blessings upon him, weeping freely upon his shoulder—a scene abruptly interrupted by the housekeeper, Mrs. Quiggett, whose astonished face appeared round the door.

“A mere marital squabble, now mended!” Lambert declared with a laugh, striding away.

“Well, I never!” cackled the old woman, her laughter like the screech of some ancient parrot.

“Oh, Quiggett!” sighed Mrs. Lambert, dabbing her eyes. “What a man he is!”

“A pair of angels, if you ask me,” Quiggett retorted. “Now, what’s for supper?”

When Lambert returned late that night, bearing news of Harry’s release—and the miraculous resurrection of his brother—the household erupted in joy. If Mrs. Lambert had adored her husband before, she now worshipped him outright. Hetty nestled silently against her father, sipping from his glass; Theo and her mother glowed like twin moons. After supper, the four knelt together in gratitude, their hearts lifted in the purest exultation—until a sudden knocking at the door startled them.

Past midnight, long after all expected visitors, who could it be?

“George,” came a voice, as the door opened to reveal Harry, leading his brother forward. “We’ve been to Aunt Bernstein’s, but we couldn’t sleep without thanking you all.”

No more words were needed. Mrs. Lambert embraced Harry; Theo, pale with emotion, clutched Hetty; George bowed deeply and kissed Mrs. Lambert’s hand; the General wiped his eyes. It was a moment of perfect grace—when wrongs were forgiven, peace restored, and love, once thought lost, returned.

“We saw your lights,” Harry explained. “We leave for Aunt Bernstein’s tomorrow, but the day after—?”

“Dinner!” declared the General. “And you shall tell us everything.”

And so here we are, gathered not in the grand dining hall but in the snug parlor, the cloth drawn, the servants dismissed, the guests leaning forward as Mr. George Esmond Warrington prepares his tale.

---

“At General Braddock’s table in Virginia,” George began, “I once heard a shrewd Philadelphia agent—none other than Mr. Franklin—warn against the perils of our march. ‘Your Excellency,’ said he, ‘may easily take the fort once you reach it. But the wilderness itself is your foe. Your line will stretch for miles through untamed forest, exposed to ambush at every turn.’

“‘Pshaw!’ replied Braddock. ‘Savages may terrify your militia, but they are no match for His Majesty’s troops.’

“How fatally mistaken he was.

“On that ill-fated July day, our orderly advance dissolved into chaos. The French and their Indian allies struck from the trees, their shots picking us off unseen. Our men faltered, then broke, officers falling faster than the ranks. I was sent forward with a message—only to fall wounded, my leg shattered by a musket-ball.

“As I lay helpless, a French-Indian hybrid, his musket raised, rushed to finish me. My fusil—Harry’s gift—saved me. But before I could reload, another figure loomed: an Indian, scalping-knife in teeth. I was too weak to resist.

“Then—a miracle. A French officer dashed forward. ‘*À moi, Florac!*’ I cried.

“It was my old dueling-second from Quebec! With a curse, he ran the Indian through. ‘*Eh, la belle affaire!*’ he muttered, binding my leg. ‘Play dead, or the rest will scalp you.’ He pressed a dead man’s coat and cockade upon me, then vanished into the fray.

“Florac’s ruse spared me. When the massacre ended, he returned with Indians—scalp-trophies at their belts—and bade them carry his ‘brother’ to the fort. Had he abandoned me, they’d have stripped and killed me without a thought.

“For weeks I languished in fever at Duquesne, nursed by a half-breed woman. My possessions—watch, money—were stolen by the fort’s commandant, a rogue named Museau. When I begged to send word to Virginia, he dictated a ransom letter, threatening to cast me to the Indians if I refused.

“Months crawled by. Spring came, then summer. No reply arrived—the messenger had been shot. Museau, grown brutal, kept me close, though his sergeant’s Indian mistress showed me kindness.

“I feigned lameness long after my leg healed, biding my time. And then—” George paused, glancing at his audience. “But that is a tale for another evening.”

The room sat in stunned silence. Theo clasped Hetty’s hand; Harry’s fists clenched.

“By Heaven,” the Colonel muttered, “such devils must be hunted down.”

George nodded gravely. “In Pennsylvania, bounties are offered—a hundred and thirty dollars per scalp.”

A shiver passed through them all. The candles flickered. Outside, the night lay still—but within, the echoes of war lingered.

For months, life at the fort dragged on in weary monotony. The commandant and I quarreled and made up, played greasy games of cards, and scraped out dismal duets—his wheezing flute against my cracked guitar. The poor Fawn endured her master’s drunken rages and nursed him through gout, me through fever, and her child through whatever ailed it. So the days passed until the first chill of autumn, when a hunter arrived with news that set the little garrison buzzing like a stirred hive.

The Marquis de Montcalm was sending reinforcements—fresh troops, artillery, supplies—up the St. Lawrence to secure French holdings and push further into British territory. Museau, our commandant, would be replaced by a superior officer, one who might trade me as a prisoner or hand me over to the Indians in retaliation for British cruelties. The men cheered at the prospect of driving the *Rosbifs* into the sea, but Museau’s face darkened.

*"Eh, commandant!"* I said, *"All very fine—but what of your farm in Normandy? The cider, the tripe, the apple trees?"*

He scowled. *"And where will you be, my boy, when another has my post? Not all officers are as kind as honest Museau. You’ll rot in a cell, or be handed to the tribes. Remember what they did to Braddock’s men? Eyes burned, nails ripped out—ah, but why frighten you?"*

La Biche, his Seneca woman, listened without flinching. She had seen such horrors before. War had made savages of red and white alike.

*"They may roast me,"* I said coolly, *"but you’ll never taste Normandy cider again."*

Museau called for whisky, and that night, his mood shifted between bravado and melancholy. La Biche sat apart, her dark eyes fixed on me, until the crying of her child drove her away. Then Museau and I spoke plainly.

*"How do you know this hunter will help you?"* he demanded.

*"That’s my secret,"* I said—though I might have told him. The man had lingered at the fort, trading furs for drink, gambling with the soldiers. I had sung for them, strumming my battered guitar, and one evening, I chose an English tune: *"Over the hills and far away."*

The hunter’s eyes gleamed. The next day, as he passed me, he hummed the melody under his breath—in perfect English. *"If you’re ready,"* he murmured, *"I am. Talk to the Fawn."*

She was the key. For weeks, she had tended me, slipping in and out of the fort freely. Now she brought me a pick, hidden in her basket of herbs. *"Hide it,"* she whispered. The next evening, she flung a coil of rope at my feet. *"Tonight,"* she said.

Museau feigned ignorance, grumbling over his soup, playing piquet as though nothing were amiss. But when he left to fetch more whisky, La Biche fell at my knees, weeping. Her devotion shook me.

At last, he dismissed me with a mocking bow. *"Goodnight, my little lord—don’t forget your crutch!"*

The storm outside masked my work. I dug beneath the logs, wriggled through, and lowered myself by rope into the ditch. Rain lashed my face as I crept toward the river, heart pounding. The sentry’s tune faded behind me.

By moonlight, I reached the island, and there, from the shadows, came the hunter’s voice—*"Over the hills and far away."*

(Theo’s fingers flew to the harpsichord, striking up the tune, and the room burst into chorus.)

Through forest and over mountain we traveled, avoiding French patrols and hostile tribes. Frost silvered the leaves; fires burned low at night. We passed maple-sugar camps, where families shared venison and shelter. Then, at last, the towering cliffs of Will’s Creek, eagles circling above, and below—Cumberland, and the flag of England flying once more.

Harry gripped my hand. *"While I was wasting my days at the Wells, you were fighting for your life!"*

Mother’s eyes shone. *"You are your mother’s son."*

And so, after a year of captivity, I stood again on free soil, beneath the colors of home.

George Warrington had recounted his tale to Madame de Bernstein the evening prior—or at least, a portion of it. The old lady dozed intermittently, rousing only when his pauses stretched too long, murmuring vague approval before drowsiness reclaimed her. Her interest was feigned, her attention fleeting. George, stilted and hesitant, lacked the ease he had displayed before his small, earnest audience in Hill Street, where Hetty’s wide-eyed wonder, Theo’s tender glances, and the General’s dry humor had spurred him on.

“Charming, quite charming,” Madame Bernstein remarked, stifling a yawn behind lace-mittened fingers. “And your mother must have been overjoyed at your return.”

George’s lips tightened. He offered a shallow bow. “She received me as family ought, madam.”

“Family!” The old woman’s laugh was brittle. “Had your mother written to me sooner, I might have spared you both some grief. But no matter. You are here now—though I daresay she half-wishes you’d stayed buried.”

George flushed but held his tongue.

That night, as her maid prepared her for the evening’s revelry, Madame Bernstein mused aloud: “That boy’s mother mourns his resurrection more than she’ll admit. And I—well, I am furious! Why couldn’t he have stayed dead beneath that tree? Why did young Florac drag him back?” She sighed. “Still, he has his grandfather’s bearing. The same eyes, the same voice. Colonel Esmond’s boy, through and though.”

Dismissing her nephews with a flick of her fan, she clasped Harry’s hands. “Poor child! Disinherited and discarded. Only a wicked old woman will treat you kindly now.” She pressed a purse into his palm. “Don’t lose it at Ranelagh tonight.”

At Ranelagh, Harry introduced George to the glittering throng. The elder Warrington, wide-eyed, absorbed the music, the laughter, the spectacle—so unlike the quiet plantations of home. Harry watched him with fond pride, even as whispers trailed behind them.

Later, at the club, Harry declined the gaming tables. “I’ve burned my fingers enough,” he declared.

“But surely you can afford another round?” teased Mr. Morris.

Harry’s smile was thin. “Months ago, I was heir to an estate. Now? I am heir to nothing.”

The room stilled.

“You mean your brother—” Lord March began.

“—has returned,” Harry finished. “Alive.”

A ripple of disbelief passed through the company.

“Convenient,” muttered Jack Morris. “First the younger brother swans in, claiming fortune and favor. Then, just as his schemes collapse, the elder rises from the dead?”

Laughter followed, sharp and unkind.

Harry bore it silently. His thoughts were elsewhere—on Maria, on his debts, on the life he must now forge. He had been a fool, squandering wealth and time. But George was home. That, at least, was a blessing.

And yet, as he smoked his pipe alone, the weight of his choices pressed upon him. He was no longer a Virginian prince, but a man adrift—bound to a past he could not undo, and a future he could scarcely envision.

Here is the rewritten chapter in a refined historical fiction style, maintaining the essence of Thackeray's prose while enhancing readability for modern audiences:

---

The ancestral halls of the Warringtons had long echoed with tales of their forebear, Colonel Esmond, whose memoirs painted a vivid portrait of youth in the New World. Among those pages, young George had encountered the dazzling specter of Madame de Bernstein - or Beatrice Esmond as she once was - years before meeting the formidable lady herself. Now, observing her rouged cheeks and dimmed eyes across the drawing room, he couldn't help but reflect on time's cruel alchemy.

"How strange," mused George, watching his aunt arrange her jewels after an evening assembly, "that this should be the same creature who once set London aflame with her beauty. Like some spent firework from our Williamsburg celebrations - the brilliant colors faded, leaving only the charred framework."

Their supper concluded, the Baroness directed her attendant to retrieve a particular letter from her escritoire. George recognized his mother's hand immediately.

"Your mother's correspondence suggests certain... difficulties between you," remarked the Baroness, adjusting her spectacles with fingers that had once bewitched half the peerage.

The letter unfolded Madam Esmond's grievances - her disapproval of Harry's romantic entanglements, her careful accounting of familial finances, and not least, her wounded pride regarding George's own perceived disobedience.

"You quarreled over money, I presume?" the Baroness inquired, sipping her Burgundy with the relish of one who remembered when the cask was laid down.

George confessed the nature of their disagreement - his insistence on honoring a debt to his French captor, his mother's objections, and the unfortunate scandal involving the Indian woman who had aided his escape.

"Scandal flourishes as readily in Virginia as in St. James's," observed the Baroness wryly. "I've weathered my share. Another glass, nephew - cherry brandy possesses remarkable restorative properties."

The conversation turned to Harry's ill-considered engagement. The Baroness recounted her failed attempts to dissolve the match through financial persuasion, her narrative peppered with cynical asides about Maria's mercenary motives.

"Honor binds him," George admitted, "though affection seems secondary in his devotion."

The evening wore on, the Baroness holding forth with increasing candor about family intrigues and the petty machinations of society. Her sharp tongue spared no one, least of all herself. When George finally took his leave through the lamplit streets, his mind whirled with conflicting impressions.

"Are these the inheritors of noble tradition?" he wondered, observing departing revelers at great houses along his route. "What would our crusader ancestors make of such self-interested scheming?"

Arriving home, he found Harry asleep by the dying fire, pipe ashes scattered across the table. At George's approach, his brother awoke with boyish enthusiasm.

"There you are! I dreamed we were boys again, struggling through Latin declensions under Ward's rod." Harry's face shone with unaffected joy. "Come, share a last pipe with me - I've news from Newmarket to tell."

As smoke curled about them, George felt his worldly cynicism melt away. Here at least was something genuine - a brother's love untouched by pretense. The hour was late, the fire reduced to embers, but in that quiet chamber, the true wealth of family revealed itself far beyond any material inheritance.

---

This version maintains the original's character dynamics and plot points while streamlining the prose for contemporary readers. The dialogue preserves period authenticity while improving clarity, and the narrative flows more smoothly between scenes. Key themes of family loyalty, societal hypocrisy, and brotherly affection remain central to the chapter's impact.

The reunion of the Warrington brothers was, as might be expected, a torrent of confidences, each pouring into the other’s ear the events of their separation. Harry, ever eager and affectionate, demanded every detail of home—how the hounds fared, whether little Dempster still chased squirrels, if old Nathan kept his place in the kitchen, and if Fanny Mountain had bloomed into the beauty their mother predicted.

“So Polly Broadbent is to marry Tom Barker and exile herself to Georgia?” Harry laughed. “Lord, I near lost my wits—and my purse—over that girl once. Drank the parson’s port till my head spun, just to linger near her. Not that Mother would have stood for it. A Broadbent in the family? She’d sooner have welcomed a highwayman.”

George, with dry amusement, recounted the state visit of Colonel Washington and his bride to Castlewood. “Picture it, Harry—Mother stiff as an oak, doling out courtesies like a queen granting pardons. And Mountain, of course, whispering that we’d nearly acquired a stepfather in Washington himself.”

“Mountain!” Harry groaned. “She’d marry off the cook to the boot-boy if left unchecked.”

But George’s humor faded as he spoke of his own reception at home. The hero’s welcome he had expected—the sole survivor of Braddock’s massacre—had curdled into suspicion. The ladies deemed him cold; the officers, dull. And then came the final blow: the wretched Biche, the Indian woman who had nursed him in the wilderness, arrived bearing Museau’s ransom demand.

“The whispers started before she’d left the courtyard,” George said bitterly. “By sundown, I was the villain of every fireside tale—a seducer, a libertine. The pious Mrs. Stack preached sermons on my corruption. The boys pelted the poor creature with stones if she dared show her face. And Mother—God help me—she thanked Biche with a look that could freeze hell itself.”

Harry, pacing like a caged bear, swore vengeance. “Name me one of those lying dogs, and I’ll thrash him senseless! Clubber got his already—”
