“Ah, Clubber.” George’s mouth twisted. “A pity the magistrate intervened. I’d have preferred to finish the job in Maryland.”

Yet it was Harry’s own troubles that now loomed largest. Of Castlewood’s clan, he spared no one—not the Earl, whose false coin of friendship had bought Harry’s ruin at the card table; not Will, whose cheating at horseflesh had earned him a broken nose. As for Lady Fanny and her mother, his words were guarded but damning. “Gossip paints them as sharp as adders,” he muttered. “But then, who’s to say what’s true in such a nest?”

But Maria—here his voice warmed. “She gave me her jewels, George. Every trinket she owned, flung into my hands to pay my debts. I’ll not cast her off, not if the King himself ordered it.”

George, tasked by their mother to sever the match, could only sigh. “I’ll not ask you to break your word. But weigh it well, Harry. A lifetime is longer than a promise.”

Yet in the days that followed, George’s doubts festered. Aunt Bernstein’s warnings gnawed at him. Was it love that drove Maria—or ambition? Harry’s fortune was no secret; his heart, alas, was all too trusting. Resolved to test her, George began to weave a plan, one that might reveal the truth—even at the cost of Harry’s fleeting happiness.

*The world is quick to judge, and slow to pardon. And he who sets himself as judge of another’s heart may find his own weighed in the balance.*

My Lord Castlewood’s residence in Kensington Square stood as a spacious yet seldom-visited retreat for his noble family, conveniently near the palace when the king chose to lodge there. Her ladyship hosted evenings for those willing to brave the journey—though a century ago, Kensington was a perilous distance from London. George Selwyn once jested that he feared the trip, not knowing whether to dread highwaymen or ladies lying in wait over the card tables.

On the morning after Harry Warrington’s reappearance at White’s, the family gathered for breakfast, including Mr. Will, who was due at court that afternoon. The ladies arrived first, sipping chocolate; Will joined them in his court finery; and finally, my lord descended, languid in his bedgown, his wig yet unworn.

Will brought news from the Star and Garter—tales heard at White’s and sightings at Ranelagh.

“Heard what? Seen what?” asked Lord Castlewood, glancing up from his *Daily Advertiser*.

“Ask Maria,” said Lady Fanny. My lord turned to his elder sister, her face pale as linen, her expression grave.

“Another of Will’s elegant fabrications,” Maria declared.

“No,” swore Will. “Tom Claypool saw them at Ranelagh. Jack Morris heard it from Harry’s own lips at White’s. The Fortunate Youth is a second son—his brother lives!”

“The elder not dead?” my lord murmured.

“No more than you are. A fine trick, if you ask me.”

“Mr. Warrington is incapable of deceit!” Maria cried.

“I never encouraged the boy,” said Lady Castlewood. “Nor did Fanny. Not we, indeed!”

“Not we, indeed!” echoed Fanny.

“The fellow’s a beggar now,” Will sneered. “I’ll wager he hasn’t paid for the coat on his back. Good riddance—I hate him!”

“You’ve held a grudge since he blacked your eye,” my lord remarked dryly. “So the lad’s found his brother and lost his fortune.” His gaze lingered on Maria, and something in her woeful expression struck him as absurd. He burst into laughter.

“A shame!” Maria sobbed, hiding her face.

Lady Castlewood and Fanny exchanged glances. “We do not always follow your humor, my lord,” the former said stiffly.

“Nor need you,” he replied coolly. “Maria, forgive me if I’ve wounded you.”

“Wounded me? You stripped him in prosperity and mock him in ruin!” She rose, glaring at them all before sweeping from the room.

Before the quarrel could deepen, a phaeton arrived—Harry’s once-extravagant purchase, now shared with his brother. They entered to find only Lady Castlewood and Fanny, the others absent—Maria, no doubt, composing herself.

“We’ve heard of your astonishing news,” Lady Castlewood said warmly to Harry. “My lord means to call on you today. And, dear cousin, poverty shan’t lessen our regard.”

“Now we may prove we never loved you for your acres!” Fanny added brightly.

George bowed. “And I, who inherit them?”

Fanny laughed. “We shall love you for resembling Harry!”

The ladies’ ease in shifting their attentions was masterful. They spoke kindly to George but lavished Harry with fond reproaches—why had he stayed away?

“Tea and piquet with us would have been safer than with others,” Lady Castlewood chided.

“Now you’ve no money to lose, you may play with us!” Fanny teased.

George watched, perplexed. This warmth toward the now-penniless Harry defied expectation. Perhaps these people were not so black-hearted as rumor painted.

Lord Castlewood soon joined them, impeccably dressed. “Had you come sooner,” he mused, “that unfortunate piquet evening might never have occurred. A younger son ought to be more prudent.”

“Or a kinsman more merciful,” Harry said ruefully.

“Play is in our blood,” my lord sighed. “I’ve squandered as much as any. Your banknotes left me within a day.”

“I play, but never to ruin,” George remarked.

“When we heard my lord had played with Harry, we were furious,” the ladies exclaimed.

“Better friends won it than strangers,” my lord said.

George laughed. “A loss is a loss, even to a brother. Harry must bear his debts like any man.”

“Well said!” my lord clapped Harry’s shoulder. The ladies smiled.

“Your sister raised true gentlemen,” Lady Castlewood declared.

Fanny sighed. “You grow too amiable in poverty, Harry! George, you cannot match him.”

“I fear I am no Fortunate Youth,” George replied gravely.

Upstairs, a harpsichord played. Harry flushed; the ladies smiled.

“Maria is above,” said Lady Castlewood.

Harry followed eagerly, but George was detained by my lord. “Stay—I long to hear of your captivity.”

Once alone, Lord Castlewood grew serious. “Has Harry told you all?”

“Including his engagement to Lady Maria,” George said. “He’s had ill luck here.”

“None regrets it more than I. My brother consorts with jockeys and rakes. Poverty forces us together.” He sighed. “Pride is my curse. I cannot sell my name.”

George listened, masking skepticism.

“We all have faults,” he said. “Play is common among gentlemen. I wish you’d won, not lost.”

“As do I! Your kindness speaks well of you. Harry has heart, but little head.”

“He makes friends easily,” George said.

“We love him as a brother,” my lord insisted.

“His letters praised your hospitality. My mother keeps them all.”

“And you—you’ve heart and wit both,” my lord said pointedly.

“I’ve had more sorrow than most my age.”

“This turn of fortune leaves Harry without means?”

“Only what our mother or I may spare. What is a younger brother’s portion here?”

My lord shrugged. “You know what a younger brother is.”

George nodded. “We are friends, but flesh and blood. I’ll do no more than custom demands. Why fund his racing and cards? He’d squander it as he did here.”

“Poor lad,” my lord murmured.

“Our estate is vast, our income meager. Harry may return to Virginia—a horse, venison, a little coin. But if he crosses our mother, his lot will be bleak.”

My lord listened respectfully. “You speak well. Ever considered public life?”

“Every man of station must,” George replied.

Upstairs, they found only Lady Maria, her eyes red.

“You are like your brother,” she said softly. “He says you are as good.”

George, struck by remorse, thought, *How could he love her?* But Harry saw differently.

She soon excused herself, overcome.

“Nerves,” my lord murmured.

As they departed, George mused, “So you’ve parted friends?”

“She is good,” Harry said fiercely.

“And when you take her to Virginia? Mother will never forgive us.”

“I told her the truth—that Madam will come round. And that you’d share with me.”

George arched a brow. “I told Lord Castlewood the opposite—that as elder brother, I claim my rights.”

Harry paled. “You won’t help me?”

Madame de Bernstein was nearly as delighted as her Virginian nephews by the resolution of Harry’s ill-fated entanglement with Lady Maria. That very evening, George dispatched a note to the Baroness, recounting the final interview. Soon after, Lord Castlewood—who seldom paid his aunt the compliment of a visit—presented himself at her house with remarkable candor.

The game was over, and my lord saw no reason for further dissimulation. "She is my sister, after all," he said with an air of fraternal tenderness. "Opportunities like this do not come often at her age. I may have pitied your young Virginian, but blood is blood. One must stand by one’s own."

"Such noble loyalty does you credit," remarked the Baroness dryly.

Castlewood bowed. "The stakes are decided. No bitterness remains for the loser—and surely you, as the victor, bear no grudge?"

The Baroness assured him she was in the finest of spirits. "Confess, Eugene—that tender scene at the spunging-house, where Maria returned all Harry’s gifts? That was your invention?"

"Pure devotion," Castlewood replied, feigning innocence. "What else could move her to such generosity toward a betrayed lover?"

"But it was *you* who advised it?"

With a shrug, he admitted as much. "We might still have won, had not the elder brother appeared. I’ve since consoled Maria by pointing out the mercy of our defeat. Imagine if she had married Harry, only for George to emerge afterward!"

"*Effectivement*," said the Baroness, taking snuff. "Better the grave should yield its dead before the vows are sworn. Your Mr. George seems a sensible man—no more selfish than most eldest sons. Poor Molly fancied him a greenhorn, like his brother. She dreamed he might divide his fortune, in which case she would have taken Harry even so."

"Harry with half a loaf might suffice, but Harry with none?" Castlewood smirked.

"And what does that mean?"

"Only that the elder made it clear Harry would dine at *his* table, not his own."

"What a hardened creature!" cried Madame de Bernstein.

"Come, aunt—*cartes sur table*! He did no more than any man would. Even Molly admitted it, once her weeping was done. The woman was absurd—so infatuated at her age!"

"Yet I think it was a true passion," mused the Baroness. "Her farewell—*c’était touchant, ma parole!* I wept myself. The boy, mud-spattered and wild-haired, burst in upon us at dinner, declared himself nobly, and she answered like a tragedy queen. It put me in mind of *Douglas*—the very dignity of despair! If she had stumbled, none could retreat more gracefully."

"She will not die of it," said Castlewood, baring his teeth.

"At her age, such maladies are seldom fatal," agreed the Baroness, sipping her chocolate.

The great world, as any observer knows, quarrels and reconciles without lingering resentment. The Baroness and the Castlewoods, having battled over Harry like wolves over a bone, now resumed their civilities as if no rupture had occurred. Madame de Bernstein’s carriage rolled up to Lady Castlewood’s rout; the Esmond ladies smiled at her assemblies—all affection restored.

When next the Baroness encountered George, she wagged her stick at him in mock reproach. "So, sir! You have played the stern elder brother to perfection!"

George reddened. "I only followed your ladyship’s advice—testing whether it was himself or his fortune they loved."

"Nay, Maria could hardly marry a pauper whose own brother denied him!"

"I acted for the best," George insisted.

"Hypocrite!" cried the old lady, laughing.

"Hypocrite? How so?"

"I know *all*, child!" she exclaimed in French. "You are your grandfather reborn! Come, let me embrace you. Harry has told me—you divided your patrimony with him!"

"It was no more than right," George said, eyes brightening. "We have shared all since birth. I feigned hardness only to unmask those who sought him."

"And will you divide Virginia as well?"

"That would not be just. The land must follow the elder’s right—Harry would not have it otherwise. But half of what is mine is his. His extravagance sprang only from error."

"A knight of old! A Bayard!" the Baroness declared that night to her maid. And when the brothers departed, it was Harry who received her fingers, while George was granted the rouged cheek—a favor that flushed him as deeply as the paint itself.

Yet for all his generosity, Harry could not escape a pang of humiliation. His fall from golden youth to dependent younger brother weighed upon him. At Kensington, George now reigned supreme, courted and flattered, while Harry was left to Maria’s mournful glances—reproachful eyes that accused him of accepting his freedom too readily.

Ah, the folly of love’s aftermath! What begins in poetry ends in farce. Damon and Phillis set out hand in hand, dreaming of eternal devotion—only to part at the first toll-gate. And when years later they meet again, he a worn-out rake, she a matron with a brood of children, how absurd the memory of past sighs!

So Harry found it at Kensington. Maria’s sighs, once enchanting, now rang hollow. The pipe in his chambers offered better company. He excused himself from George’s visits, pleading engagements.

Even in Hill Street, his uncle’s family grew cold. The ladies now denied him outright—those who had once fawned upon him! Once, calling upon Aunt Lambert after a rebuff at his aunt’s, he saw Lady Warrington’s chairmen arrive—and the lady herself, stepping in without a blush as he bowed from the window.

"My own father’s brother’s wife!" Harry lamented. "What have I done to earn this?"

"You will always find warmth here," said Aunt Lambert, with a glance toward her youngest.

"Your house is different," Harry replied. "I am always at peace within it."

The General, overhearing, quoted cheerfully:

"Where did I find that, scholar?" he challenged his son.

"Catullus," ventured the young man.

"Wrong! My old friend Burton—Democritus Junior—who has furnished many a lazy student with learning!" And the General chuckled, well pleased with his borrowed wisdom.

Our Virginians, like many young men of their time, had embraced staunch Jacobite sympathies, professing fervent loyalty to the exiled Stuart line. The Old Pretender himself had once granted Madam Esmond’s father the title of Marquis, a distinction she could not entirely dismiss, however fallen the family’s fortunes. As for the whispers about her sister, Madame de Bernstein, and the Chevalier—well, such scandals paled beside the domestic irregularities of the Hanoverian kings. Yet, king *de facto* or *de jure*, a sovereign was a sovereign, and Madam Esmond was adamant: her sons must present themselves at court. Who knew? The King might even bestow some mark of favor—a post, a ribbon—upon them. After all, the Virginia Esmonds were as noble as any in England.

Thus commanded, the young men prepared to make their bows at Kensington. Had their mother known George borrowed Harry’s best laced coat for the occasion, her displeasure would have been severe. In those days, the King’s drawing-room welcomed his loyal subjects almost daily, and with war brewing, patriotic fervor ensured a steady stream of visitors. The monarch himself, though longing for Hanover, remained prudently in England—his ministers wisely deterring any risky voyages. A certain German countess, elevated to British peerage, provided consolation, her native tongue soothing his homesick heart.

It was in this setting that George Warrington, accompanied by his uncle and General Lambert, approached Kensington Palace. They arrived by hackney-coach—Lord Wrotham’s carriage having been reclaimed—and entered through the grand portico, passing through tapestry-lined galleries adorned with masterpieces. Titian’s *Venus*, Rubens’ *St. Francis*, Van Dyck’s royal portraits—George, wide-eyed, absorbed it all, barely heeding Lambert’s whispered commentary on the assembled courtiers.

“There’s Admiral Boscawen, who fired the first shot in your colonies,” murmured Lambert. “That gold-laden figure is Mr. Fox, once Minister, now content with a lucrative sinecure. And yonder—ah, the Duke’s approach cuts short my lecture.”

The royal party soon emerged: first the King, a keen-eyed little man in plain snuff-colored suit and Garter ribbon; then the Duke of Cumberland, broad and scarlet-faced; and finally, towering above them all, the formidable Mr. Pitt. Even George, awestruck, felt an instinctive urge to doff his hat as the great statesman passed, his thunderous presence dwarfing the monarch himself.

His Majesty moved through the room with brisk familiarity, pausing here and there. He recognized Lambert—a veteran of Dettingen—and praised his promotion warmly. Then his gaze fell upon George.

“This is the other brother?” the King remarked dryly. “Timely arrival, else the first might have spent the whole fortune.”

Sir Miles, bursting with pride, nearly wept at this royal notice. “The greatest monarch! The finest gentleman!” he exclaimed afterward, as they departed in his coach, where he insisted on hosting Lambert and George for a modest collation—six-year-old mutton, no less, and a questionable cordial of Lady Warrington’s own making.

The evening dissolved into a blur of flattery. Sir Miles extolled George’s (largely imagined) heroic reception at court; Flora and Dora gazed adoringly; even young Miles piped in with an inconvenient truth. Lambert, observing the spectacle, could only marvel.

Later, recounting the day to his wife, the General was succinct.

“Humbugs,” he declared.

“Hypocrites!” she agreed.

“Bo,” said Lambert.

“Why ‘Bo’?”

“To a goose, my dear.”

And with that enigmatic close, the chapter of courtly farce ended.

Life, as it is truly lived, seldom fits neatly into the pages of a novel. The daily grind of men—selling spices, poring over legal texts, stitching coats—holds little of the drama that stirs the blood. A writer must content himself with moments snatched away from labor: the laughter at a coffeehouse, the whispered confessions in a garden, the fleeting passions that flare between duty and drudgery. For it is only in these interludes that characters step free of their toil, and we, the idle observers, may glimpse them as they truly are—not as merchants or barristers, but as men and women aflame with hopes and grievances.

Thus, we find young Harry Warrington, once the darling of London’s glittering set, now shunned by the macaronis of White’s and Arthur’s. The world, so eager to embrace him when fortune smiled, now turned its back with a shrug. His aunt, Madame de Bernstein, who once doted on him, now met him with cold civility, her wit sharpened to a sting. "You mope, Harry," she chided. "The world kicks you—must you groan so loudly? Others swallow their pride and smile. You must do the same."

But Harry could not dissemble. His honest face betrayed every hurt. More and more, he sought refuge in the Lambert household, where kindness still dwelt unchilled by fashion. There, he would sit glumly by the window, while the girls moved about their tasks, and Mrs. Lambert watched him with a mother’s pity.

"Are we such dull company, Harry?" Hetty teased one day, though not unkindly.

"Dull? You are the best souls alive!" he protested. "But I—I am the lump among you."

Theo, ever gentle, interceded. "If he is quiet, it is because he grieves. There is no shame in that."

Hetty tossed her head. "I would not let them see my wounds. I’d smile if that old painted harridan struck me!"

"Theo is right," Harry said, flushing. "When those you love turn from you, it cuts deep."

"And if George did so?" Hetty dared to ask.

Harry’s fists clenched. "Do not speak of him so. You do not know him as I do. There is no man more loyal, no brother more true."

Hetty paled, and for once, words failed her.

The brothers, though sharing lodgings, lived in separate orbits. George, studious and reserved, buried himself in books or wandered the new museum at Montagu House. Harry, adrift without occupation, fretted like a caged thing. "What is there for me, George?" he burst out one evening. "I cannot go back to Virginia—to planting and duck-shooting and sermons, over and over till I die. You’d make your way anywhere, but I—what am I fit for?"

George exhaled smoke. "Perhaps Florac should have left me to the Indian’s scalping-knife. Then you’d still be the Fortunate Youth."

Harry sprang up, furious. "Damn it, George—!"

"Peace, brother," George said, laughing. "I jest. But mark this: you’ll find your path."

The Lamberts, now settled in Soho, became their second family. With them, the young men shared the city’s diversions—the bustle of Vauxhall, the thrill of the playhouse. And it was to the theatre they all repaired one evening, lured by the talk of the town: Mr. Home’s *Douglas*, the new tragedy that had set London weeping.

"A Scotsman’s play!" scoffed General Lambert. "And a Whig at that!"

"But they say ’tis sublime," protested Theo.

"Better than Shakespeare?" Harry grinned.

"Folly!" cried Mrs. Lambert. "As if any could be!"

"Yet women weep more for Mr. Richardson’s novels than for *Hamlet*," the General teased.

Theo flushed. "Papa, that is unjust!"

"Then prove me wrong. Tonight, you shall all judge—handkerchiefs at the ready!"

And so they went, the General shepherding his flock through the thronged galleries of Covent Garden. Royalty graced the opposite box—the Prince of Wales, his stately mother, and the much-whispered-about Lord Bute. The candles flared, the curtain rose, and soon the tale of love and sacrifice unfolded. Mrs. Lambert, true to prediction, wept behind her pillar. Hetty gasped at the heroine’s plight. Harry, transported, forgot his woes in the grandeur of the verse.

Only George, watching sidelong, noted how Theo’s fingers trembled—not for the play, but for the man beside her, whose face, in the flickering light, seemed to her more moving than any stagecraft.

And so the night passed: in applause, in murmured praise, in the quiet certainty that for some hearts, at least, the real drama lay not on the boards, but in the shadows just beyond.

When the curtain fell on *Macbeth*, our party departed in high spirits for Mr. Warrington’s lodgings, where the young Virginians had arranged a lavish supper. Eager to play the gracious host, George spared no expense, and General and Mrs. Lambert accepted the invitation with pleasure, delighted to indulge their young friends. The gathering included the General, his lady, their son Jack—fresh from college—their two fair daughters, and Mr. Spencer of the Temple, a new acquaintance George had met at the coffee-house.

The seating was a matter of some mystery, though it was clear Miss Theo found herself beside the roasted chickens and Mr. George Warrington, while Miss Hetty divided her attention between Mr. Harry and a fine ham. Mrs. Lambert, no doubt, presided at George’s right hand, leaving the General, his son, and the Templar to arrange themselves as they saw fit.

The conversation turned to the evening’s performance, though opinions were divided.

“You mustn’t believe a word these gentlemen say, Mr. Spencer,” protested Mrs. Lambert. “It was a perfectly delightful play, and my husband and Mr. George behaved disgracefully!”

“We laughed where we ought to have wept,” admitted the General.

“You caused such a commotion, the whole box turned to hush you!” cried his wife. “I declare, I’ve never been so mortified!”

“Mamma thought it a tragedy; we thought it a farce,” said the General. “George and I were perfectly composed—weren’t we, Theo?”

“Not when I was watching you, Papa!” Theo replied with a sly smile.

“Insolent chit!” the General growled, though his eyes twinkled.

“I was moved, I confess,” Theo continued softly. “Mrs. Woffington was sublime—so tragic when the mother lost her child. But I suppose I ought not to have been affected?”

“Women are not as clever as men, you know,” Hetty teased from her end of the table, casting a mischievous glance at Harry.

“Next time, Jack must nudge us when to laugh and when to cry,” Theo agreed.

The General lamented the missing duel between Norval and the giant Norwegian. “Now *that* would have been worth seeing! Jack, you must write to Mr. Rich and suggest it.”

Harry, ever earnest, remarked, “I’ve not seen that, but I *have* seen Slack and Broughton at Marybone Gardens!” The company burst into laughter, though Harry looked puzzled at his own success.

Mr. Spencer, the evening’s wit, praised Garrick’s performance. “His Macbeth was masterful! And Lady Macbeth—how she chilled the blood! You should have seen Mr. Warrington in the greenroom, ladies. Mrs. Pritchard herself took a pinch from his snuffbox!”

“Did the Thane’s lady sneeze?” the General asked gravely.

“She thanked him in such ghastly tones, poor Macbeth started back and sent a cloud of rappee into the air!”

“Macbeth, Macbeth, Macbeth!” the General intoned, while the table dissolved into laughter.

Theo sighed. “Oh, to see behind the scenes! The ropes, the candles, the painted faces!”

George smirked. “You’d weep less if you saw the artifice. The hero’s grandeur is but rant, the mother’s grief a handkerchief.”

“You think me foolish for being moved?” Theo challenged.

“I think you kind-hearted,” George replied, meeting her gaze until Hetty’s bright eyes made them both glance away.

The talk turned to plays, old and new. Mr. Spencer dismissed the ancients. “Shakespeare knew nothing of true pathos compared to the moderns. Where is his Belvidera, his Monimia?”

“Have *you* written, Mr. Spencer?” someone asked.

“Garrick himself rejected a piece of mine,” the Templar admitted.

George confessed to four acts of a tragedy lurking in his trunk.

“Fetch it at once!” Hetty demanded.

“Alas, it lies buried beneath linen and boots at Bristol,” George lamented.

With mock despair, the ladies groaned, and the General proposed a toast: “To Madam Esmond Warrington! Who knows? The boys may return to find a stepfather awaiting them!”

Mrs. Lambert raised her glass. “I shall write and tell her what fine hosts her sons are—though I’ll omit their conduct at the play!”

The party departed in good humor, the General’s coach waiting to convey them home.

***

The next morning found George at the Lamberts’ door in Dean Street, feigning casual inquiry after their well-being. Inside, he found Theo alone, her cheeks as fresh as the roses beside her.

“Your brothers are gone to visit the youngest at Charterhouse,” she said, needle busy in her hand. “Harry comes often—now that the fine folk no longer court him.”

“And you, Theo—do you still think me heartless for laughing at the play?”

She met his eyes. “Perhaps I was wrong.”

Their talk was interrupted by the return of Mrs. Lambert and Hetty, full of news: Harry was restless, longing for purpose. He haunted the Military Coffee-House, poring over campaigns.

“He said to Jack, ‘Why can’t I be a soldier? I eat my brother’s bread while he shares his last crust with me,’” Mrs. Lambert confided.

George revealed he had written home, offering half—or all—his patrimony for Harry’s commission.

“He has a good brother,” Mrs. Lambert said softly.

“The Danes come to those who wait,” George murmured.

“Ah! You heard that? My Theo is—” She broke off, eyes shining.

Later, George recounted his disastrous audience with the Duke. Offered a place in the Prince’s campaign, he had dared to propose Harry in his stead.

The Prince’s face darkened. “You refuse *me*?”

“I study law, sir, but my brother—”

“Enough!” The Duke turned his back.

“George, George,” groaned General Lambert. “What a fine mess you’ve made!”

And so the chapter closed—on laughter, hopes, and royal displeasure.

Loyal Britons may well shudder at the audacity of Mr. George Warrington’s refusal—his graceless dismissal of a princely offer, extended by none other than His Royal Highness, the Captain-General of the British Army and beloved son of the Crown. To spurn such favor! To cast aside the golden ladder of advancement! Even Will Esmond, never one to spare an oath, cursed his cousin’s folly with relish.

"A savage may scalp himself if he pleases," remarked Cousin Castlewood dryly. "Or perhaps young George has heard one war-whoop too many since Braddock’s folly and lost his taste for battle."

Will sneered. "The elder brother races to ruin, and the younger follows. The next time I see George, I’ll give him a piece of my mind."

"And if you do," snapped Lady Maria, "pray ensure Harry is not within earshot."

At this, Will’s curses multiplied.

My lord, ever the peacemaker—or at least the amused spectator—waved a languid hand. "Must we all be warriors? Will is champion enough for this house. As for the Warrington boys, let George be the scholar and Harry the fighter. A pity, Will, that when you sought a quarrel, you found Harry’s fist instead of George’s bookish chin."

"Your own hand excels at cards, my lord," retorted Will’s mother.

"Indeed," simpered my lord, admiring his fingers. "A delicate instrument, unfit for war but most adept at piquet. Yours, my lady, is no less skillful—though I bless the day it was bestowed upon my late father."

Thus the family sparred, my lord always the victor in their barbed exchanges.

Madame de Bernstein, upon hearing of George’s blunder, raged like a tempest—though age had softened her once-ferocious spirit, and she dared not storm at George himself. When he visited her, she greeted him with a groan.

"You have murdered your prospects at court, nephew!"

"I never sought them, madam," George replied stiffly.

"You might have aided Harry’s rise had you accepted the Duke’s favor. Princes do not forgive slights."

"General Lambert said as much," George admitted, flushing. "I see now I was wrong. But I am no soldier, and Harry would have served better."

"Ah, so you offered the Prince a better mount?" The Baroness arched a brow.

"*Expende Hannibalem*," George muttered. "Our Hannibal is no lightweight."

She blinked. "Your Latin is wasted on me, sir."

"I made the same defense to Wolfe and Lambert. They granted my excuse, if not my wisdom. Who knows? Perhaps they’ll plead my case with His Highness. Battles weary me—I prefer Homer’s wars to real ones."

"Then why play the soldier at all?" she demanded.

"My mother loved Harry best," George said darkly. "And when the enemy came, duty called. Had I been born Scots, I might have—"

"Silence!" she cried—though her face betrayed amusement.

Yet where Madame de Bernstein chuckled, Sir Miles and Lady Warrington recoiled in pious horror.

"What upbringing," lamented her ladyship, "could produce such ingratitude?"

Sir Miles, ever dutiful, attended the Prince’s next levee to proclaim his own unwavering loyalty. The Prince, with icy grace, remarked, "No one ever doubted Sir Miles would refuse a royal offer." A compliment her ladyship trumpeted far and wide.

Thus when George next faced them, their looks were funeral. Ah, that familial pity—the mournful gaze reserved for those who have blundered beyond redemption! Yet Sir Miles’s anger thawed as the Prince’s star waned. Defeat at Hastenbeck and the capitulation that followed saw His Highness stripped of command, retreating into private life—a fallen idol.

One day in St. James’s Park, George and Harry, walking with Lambert, encountered the Prince—no star upon his coat, no retinue at his heels. He paused, spoke briefly, and to George said:

"You did well, sir, not to follow me in the spring."

"Then I was sorry, sir," George replied with a deep bow. "Now I am sorrier still."

The Prince touched his hat and walked on.

Lambert, watching, laughed. "Harry, your brother is like Charing Cross’s lion—turning his back on the rising sun to salute the setting one."

"Because I honor fallen greatness?" George asked.

"Because you are a fool," Lambert chuckled. "Your uncle will rage anew—and so would I, if I weren’t so amused."

And with that, the three strode on, the lion at their backs gazing steadfastly westward.

The drums of war echoed across Europe, and in London’s coffee-houses and drawing rooms, Harry Warrington found his restless spirit stirred by the clash of empires. While Britain’s own king had tasted defeat, Frederick of Prussia carved his name into history with victories that set the world aflame—Rossbach, Leuthen—battlefields where Harry might have sought glory had fate spun a different thread. But no, his musings remained just that: idle longings, for his pen was not destined to chronicle such deeds. That task belonged to fiercer hands, mightier quills.

Harry was no scholar. Books bored him; novels struck him as trifles, and the ladies who wept over Richardson’s pages seemed to him absurd. His laughter came slow but hearty, rumbling after the jest had passed. Must every gentleman be a wit or a poet? Surely not. Yet idleness gnawed at him—ruined, dependent, his strong hands empty of purpose. A man bred for action, left to dawdle in drawing rooms, was a man at war with himself.

The Lambert women pitied him—all but one. Theo and her mother softened his gloom with kindness, but Hetty’s tongue was sharp as a saber’s edge. Why should he linger, useless, when the world was in arms? "If I were a man," she declared, pacing like a caged officer, "I would not waste my days sighing over misfortunes. I would make my own fate!"

Harry winced. "You think I lack courage?"

"I think you lack nothing but the will to use what you have," she shot back. "Must you wait for permission to be a man?"

Her words struck deeper than she knew. Pride and shame warred in him. He was no coward—had he not faced worse than battlefield perils? Yet here he stood, shackled by debt, by duty, by his mother’s unyielding will.

"I would volunteer tomorrow if I could," he muttered. "But commissions aren’t bought with empty pockets, and I’ll not beg from George again."

Hetty scoffed. "Must it be bought? Can you not earn it?"

A silence fell between them. Outside, London carried on—coaches rattling, hawkers calling, the distant pulse of a military march. Somewhere, men were rising, striving. And Harry Warrington, with his broad shoulders and idle hands, felt the weight of his own inertia like a leaden coat.

Mrs. Lambert’s entrance broke the tension. "What troubles you, my dear?" she asked, eyeing Harry’s stormy brow.

"Nothing new," Hetty said lightly. "Only Harry fretting over his lack of occupation."

But the truth hung unspoken: a man who aches for purpose and finds none is a man half-alive. And Harry, for all his strength, had never felt weaker.

George Warrington did not permit his legal studies to interfere unduly with his pleasures or his health. His mother, Madam Esmond, had written to remind him that though he might wear a student’s gown and dine in hall among the undistinguished, he bore a name of ancient honor, and ought to conduct himself as befitted a gentleman rather than a laborious drudge. George, ever obedient to her wishes, complied readily enough, treating the law as a voluntary pursuit rather than a grinding profession.

The law in those days, though less exacting than in our own, was still a demanding mistress. A man might rise to the ermine and the woolsack, as Murray had done, but George Warrington had other inclinations. The black-letter pages of Chaucer pleased him far more than the stern Gothic texts of Hale or Coke.

In those days, the classics still held sway. Gentlemen quoted Virgil in Parliament and cited Tacitus in the courts. But the age of the Olympians had waned. Who now cared for the Muses, or knew whether *Melpomene* had three syllables or four? The age of economists had dawned, and the Pantheon stood deserted.

George, in his youth, had courted the Tragic Muse during idle hours in Virginia and his captivity at Fort Duquesne. The fruit of that dalliance was a tragedy—now lost to Bell’s *Theatre*, though likely no worse than many that were printed. It is the way of young men to aim first at the highest, dreaming of epics and tragedies before contenting themselves with humbler verse.

Upon arriving in London, George became a frequent spectator at both playhouses, a patron of theatrical coffee-houses, and a familiar figure among wits and actors. He supped at the Bedford, exchanged jests at the Cecil, and found himself welcomed in these circles—partly for his wit, partly for the rumor of his wealth. To drink with Bobadil, to take snuff from Aristarchus himself—these were delights for a young man of poetic fancy. And if his mother frowned upon such dissipations, so much the sweeter the forbidden fruit.

He sought advice on his tragedy from his new acquaintance, Mr. Spencer of the Temple, who hosted a breakfast in Fig Tree Court where George read a portion of his play. The assembled critics—even the formidable Mr. Johnson—pronounced it promising, though it violated the unities. "But so have others," Johnson conceded. "There is something here of *Coriolanus*, something of *Othello*."

"And both good models, I hope," said George.

"The catastrophe is terrible—and, being partly true, all the more awful," remarked Mr. Spencer.

The plot was bloody enough. Drawing from his grandfather’s tales of the mercenary George Frundsberg, George had fashioned his hero, Colonel Carpezan, a Lutheran soldier storming a convent on the Rhine. The first act ended in flames and screams as the nuns fled before the besiegers.

Act II found Carpezan ennobled, married to the rescued Lady Sybil, and wretched. His wife taunted him for his low birth; his conscience gnawed at him for past crimes.

Act III brought them to Prague, where the young King of Bohemia’s attentions to Sybil grew unmistakable. By Act IV, Carpezan’s jealousy erupted in tragedy—he had her slain by his executioner, the deed done under a pale moon as the King strummed his fatal serenade outside.

Act V saw Carpezan, now a renegade in the Turkish camp, confronting the King at Mohacz. He struck him down, only to be rewarded with treachery—the Vizier, praising his valor, had him shot by archers. As Carpezan lay dying beside his old comrade Ulric, he gasped, *"Forgive me!"*—and the curtain fell.

When George finished reading, he turned to Johnson. "Well, sir? Is there hope for me?"

But Johnson, who had dozed through the latter acts, admitted he had missed the climax.

The assembled critics murmured their verdicts. Some quibbled over details—should the Vizier cry *"Loose!"* rather than *"Fire!"* when ordering the archers to shoot? Others wished Ulric had survived to wed some comic heroine.

"History forbids it," Johnson rumbled. "Mohacz was a slaughter. Let Ulric die with honor."

And so the verdict stood—flawed, perhaps, but promising. George Warrington’s tragedy might yet find its stage.

Gumbo’s grief at his master’s departure was a spectacle to behold—great, noisy, and unabashed. The poor fellow wept openly, his cries echoing through the Lambert household, where the maidservants, ever tender-hearted, gathered to console him. Dark skin notwithstanding, Gumbo found no shortage of sympathy among the fairer sex. Europe, it seemed, was less particular about such matters than certain other quarters of the world. And so, between sobs, he fortified himself with generous portions of beer and cold meat in the kitchen, his sorrow temporarily assuaged by the comforts of the larder.

Hetty Lambert, usually so quick with a jest, had grown uncharacteristically subdued. The girl who once chattered like a sparrow now moved through the house in near silence, her spirit weighed down by thoughts of far-off battlefields. She flinched at the sound of cannonfire in her imagination, trembled during prayers for soldiers, and scanned the papers daily for news of the expedition. Every report of danger sent a fresh stab of fear through her heart. Was he wounded? Captured? Worse? She would have given anything—even her own limbs—for his safe return.

“Hetty, you never used to fret like this when Papa was in Minorca,” Theo remarked one day.

“Ah, Theo,” Hetty sighed, “that was before George was safe at home and Harry was not.”

Theo blushed, and the conversation ended there.

Meanwhile, Harry Warrington’s first taste of war proved less glorious than anticipated. The British fleet, after landing at Cancale, encountered little resistance—just a few dragoons who prudently retreated, and a solitary old Frenchman who fired his two guns before being captured. The troops marched to St. Malo, burned some ships, and returned to their vessels without ever meeting a proper foe. Harry, though disappointed, wrote home in high spirits:

*"Dearest Brother—I am returned, unwounded and unglorified, but alive and well. We saw dragons—or at least dragoons—who declined to fight. We burned a few ships, slept under canvas, and marched through narrow lanes where any hidden enemy might have picked us off. It was not the grand campaign I had hoped for, but I have smelled powder, slept rough, and endured the sea like a proper sailor. Next time, perhaps, the French will stand their ground."*

The letter sent George rushing to Dean Street, where the Lamberts greeted the news with relief—though Hetty’s nerves were hardly soothed.

But the true test came soon enough. Emboldened by their minor success, the British commanders turned their sights on Cherbourg. This time, the news was triumphant: the town had surrendered, its forts seized, its ships burned, its harbor dismantled. London erupted in celebration. The captured French cannon, emblazoned with arrogant mottoes, were paraded through the streets. Hetty, in her excitement, nearly convinced herself that Harry had single-handedly hauled the guns from their batteries.

Then came St. Cas.

Disaster.

The troops, re-embarking under fire, were caught by a swift French advance. The rear guard, outnumbered ten to one, broke and fled to the boats. Seven hundred were lost—killed, drowned, or taken. The General lay dead on the beach.

And Harry?

No word.

In London, the jubilation turned to dread. George’s face was grim as he brought the news to the Lamberts. Hetty, pale and silent, clutched her sister’s hand.

Where was their Virginian?

The vanity of men—especially Frenchmen—knows no bounds. Can it be believed that after the skirmish at St. Cas—a mere rout of a rearguard—they had the audacity to waste powder on celebrations at the Invalides in Paris, crowing over our misfortune? What honor is there in triumphing over a handful of brave men, outnumbered and cornered upon the shore? I had half a mind to paint the scene of London’s own revelry after our success at St. Malo—the polished guns paraded by our tars, the horse-grenadiers gleaming in their pride, the mob roaring, the balconies of Fleet Street thronged with well-wishers. But now that the French have cheapened victory with their mimicry, I wash my hands of the subject.

Let others boast. For my part, I care only that Harry has returned, whole and sound. Did I not assure you, gentle reader, that he came back unscathed? The sea had bronzed his cheeks; the musket ball that whistled past his ear had spared him. His coat was damp, not with blood, but salt spray. True, he had turned his back—but only briefly, and what are lapels but reversals? A man may retreat and yet hold his honor intact.

* * *

**"I tell you,"** Harry recounted, **"it was every man for himself when the line broke. The French horse and foot were upon us, cutting down our fellows as we scrambled for the boats. Poor Armytage fell—I dragged him through the surf, but he never reached the ship. A fine soldier lost, and Miss Howe a widow before she was a bride."**

**"It is her I think of,"** murmured Hetty, her fingers twisting in her lap. **"And the King. He must bear the guilt of sending Sir John to his death."**

Papa Lambert shook his head, amused. **"His Majesty will mourn in his own fashion, my dear. Kings cannot weep for every soldier."**

**"But if he commanded it—"** Hetty pressed, her voice quivering.

**"Then let us hope his conscience pricks him,"** the General replied dryly. **"Though I doubt Miss Hester’s Pretender would offer more tenderness."**

George, ever the skeptic, arched a brow. **"Why not celebrate defeat and mourn our victories? I find no piety in burning ships at Cherbourg."**

**"But you’re glad all the same,"** Harry retorted. **"I know I am—though I’ll not pretend I’m proud of running. It sits ill with me, an Englishman retreating before Frenchmen."**

George said nothing, though his silence spoke volumes.

* * *

The tale of Harry’s valor spread. The newspapers lauded the volunteers; White’s and Almack’s blazed with toasts to their return. Even those who had once sneered at Harry’s idleness now clasped his hand. His commission as Ensign was secured, his name gazetted among the brave.

Aunt Bernstein’s letter arrived, crisp with praise—and a banknote tucked within. Maria Esmond’s missive, blotted and earnest, spoke of pride and a heart still tender. Even Sir Miles sent game from Norfolk, a young pheasant felled by his son’s first shot.

But it was Hetty’s silence that spoke loudest. No more barbs, no taunts—only a quiet, humbled respect. The girl who had once spurred him to folly now met his eyes with something akin to remorse.

War had tested him. Retreat had chastened him. And in the end, Harry Warrington returned not just a soldier, but a man.

The Regent’s Park, with its menagerie of finned and feathered captives, often draws visitors who, in observing nature’s marvels, find their own thoughts mirrored in the creatures before them. So it was that I, while gazing at the flounder’s graceful dance through its watery sphere, found my mind wandering not to fish, but to our Virginian friends.

Here was a creature plain upon a plate, yet in its element, a vision of elegance—its dark back arching, its pale belly flashing, every motion a harmony. Out of water? A pitiful flapping, a gasping struggle. So too with men: place them where they belong, and they thrive; exile them, and they wither. Harry Warrington, long adrift, had at last found his sea.

The epaulet upon his shoulder was more than ornament—it was a summons to life. The camp, the march, the camaraderie of the mess, the crisp orders of the parade ground—all invigorated him. Had I the pen of a campaign chronicler, I would paint Harry in his scarlet and gold, recount his midnight vigils, his dawn drills, his laughter among comrades. But that task is for abler hands. Let it suffice that Harry had become a soldier, and a good one.

His brother George, ever generous, rejoiced in Harry’s renewal. With pride, he wrote to their mother, extolling Harry’s courage—modest as ever, Harry would never boast for himself. George proposed to share the cost of Harry’s advancement, feeling duty-bound to restore what fortune had taken. For had not Harry, believing himself heir, lived as one? Now George, restored to life, would make amends.

But George’s letter held more than fraternal duty. He spoke of love.

The Lamberts—kind, steadfast friends—had been a second family to the Warringtons. In their home, George had found more than shelter; he had found a heart that answered his. Miss Theodosia Lambert was no Helen, nor a Sappho—yet in her presence, George’s own spirit brightened. Her voice was the sweetest music; her company, his keenest joy. To her, he was not the heir of Virginia, but the man she cherished.

Their courtship was a quiet idyll—stolen glances, shared books, walks through St. James’s where the sermon went unheard. He sent her verses, flowers, notes that begged no reply (yet received them). He charmed her siblings, sparred with her sister Hetty, and paid such court to Mrs. Lambert that the General jested his wife was half in love herself.

But love, however pure, must reckon with the world.

At Castlewood, Madam Esmond received George’s tidings with a measured nod. A prudent match, she allowed, if the girl was bred as befitted an Esmond. Yet Mountain, her companion, sniffed at the notion. "They’ll all be scheming for his fortune," she muttered.

"Take care your own schemes don’t show too plain," Madam retorted. The barb struck home—Mountain had nursed hopes for her Fanny. A bitter exchange followed, revealing more than either wished.

Meanwhile, George, dutiful son, obeyed his mother’s commands to live as his station demanded. His table was lavish, his liveries fine, his credit unquestioned. The Lamberts, though no mercenaries, could not ignore his prospects. A virtuous, well-born suitor, devoted to their Theo—what more could they wish?

Yet one obstacle remained: Madam Esmond’s consent. Until her word arrived, hearts hung suspended. The French privateers, ever a threat, made each post-day an agony. Would the letter come? And when it did—what then?

Love, like the flounder, thrives in its rightful sphere. Out of it, even the noblest heart may gasp. But place it where it belongs, and behold—it shines.

James Wolfe, the newly appointed colonel of Harry’s regiment, returned from America shortly after the young Virginian had joined its ranks. Wolfe, already celebrated for his valor at Cape Breton, carried with him the aura of a man destined for greatness. The nation, starved for military glory, watched him keenly, as did the ministers who hoped he might deliver it.

Harry, sincere in his admiration, paid his respects to the new commander, while in London, the bonds of friendship between Wolfe, Lambert, and George Warrington grew ever stronger. Wolfe, with the frankness of a born hero, spoke freely of his campaigns, his ambitions, and even his romantic longings. He was a man of both sword and pen, as enamored with poetry as with battle—a kindred spirit to George, who found in him not only a soldier but a philosopher.

The success of George’s tragedy, *Carpezan*, became the talk of London. Rejected by Garrick in favor of another work, it found a home at Covent Garden under Rich’s management. The cast was brilliant: Barry’s tormented Carpezan, Shuter’s noble gravitas, and the striking young Hagan as the King of Bohemia. Mrs. Woffington, though perhaps too mature for the heroine, delivered a death scene so harrowing that the audience gasped as one.

On the night of the premiere, George hosted a gathering at the Bedford Head before his friends departed for the theatre, leaving him in nervous solitude. Reports arrived in bursts—applause here, a stumble there—until, at last, the messenger came: the play was a triumph. Barry announced its repetition to thunderous cheers, declaring it the work of a young Virginian, a debut to remember.

Theo Lambert, pale with suspense through the performance, flushed with pride at its acclaim. Harry, nearly fainting in the fourth act, roared louder than any when the curtain fell. Even Hetty, feigning indifference, could not suppress her delight. The Lamberts, Wolfe, and Sampson led the ovations, while Gumbo, weeping openly in the gallery, proved the most vocal critic of all.

Supper that night was a raucous affair. Actors, officers, and friends toasted George’s success—none more effusively than Hagan, who, emboldened by wine, pledged undying devotion to Lady Maria in terms so florid they drew side-eyes from both Lord Castlewood and George. The evening ended with Carpezan himself being carried home, the King of Hungary nursing a royal headache, and the poet discovering, to his horror, that he had invited half the party to repeat the revel the next night.

The morning after brought quieter joys. Mrs. Lambert fretted over the dangers of actresses, to which George replied that his heart was safely guarded. Theo, blissfully unaware of such perils, saw only the play’s beauty. Hetty, ever sharp, rolled her eyes but later embraced her sister with fierce tenderness.

Yet shadows loomed. Madam Esmond’s letter, though granting reluctant consent, carried a tone of resignation rather than joy. She spoke of Virginia’s lands but not of settlements, leaving Lambert uneasy. George, ever optimistic, swore his pen would provide—had he not already earned three hundred pounds?

Sir Miles Warrington’s disapproval arrived like a thunderclap. Penniless! Reckless! Had the boy no shame? The General and Mrs. Lambert were painted as schemers, their daughter a fortune-hunter. Sir Miles’s letters to Virginia painted George as a wastrel, Harry the sole redeemer of the family name.

Yet George, indifferent to their scorn, walked through London with his head high. He took young Miley to the theatre, sent tickets to his disapproving relatives (who took them, albeit with sneers), and laughed at their hypocrisy when the play won royal approval.

For now, the future was bright. Let the uncles rage. George had his pen, his friends, and—most of all—Theo. The curtain had risen on his life’s great act, and he would play his part to the end.

The heart aches at farewells, and so I shall spare you the details of Harry Warrington’s parting from his brother and friends. Were there not thousands of men in those days who faced the same wrench? Did not General Wolfe himself kiss his grieving mother goodbye—his father having died while the son was away winning glory at Louisbourg—and embrace his sweetheart one last time? Even Admiral Holmes, that stout old sea-dog, had his tearful farewells with Mrs. Holmes and their daughters before hoisting his flag aboard *The Dublin* and leading his squadron westward: *The Somerset, The Terrible, The Northumberland, The Royal William, The Trident, The Diana, The Seahorse*. And Admiral Saunders, who sailed a day later—was he not human too?

So the ships press on, cutting through the waves, while those left behind stand on the shore, watching the sails shrink into the horizon. Night falls, the ocean darkens, and though the fleet is gone, the hearts aboard still linger with those they have left. Prayers rise—silent, desperate—from the decks and the empty homes where wives kneel by cold beds, where children huddle close to their mothers, where sailors gaze at the stars through the salt spray. Tomorrow, the sun will rise on duty once more, and life will march forward.

George stays with Harry in Portsmouth, waiting for the wind to turn. He clasps Wolfe’s hand one last time, studying the man’s pale, determined face before the cannons roar and the bells clamor as the ships depart. The next day, George returns to London, to the selfish bustle of life—that relentless business which, by thirty, claims every man. He pauses at Harry’s old lodgings, staring at the half-burnt papers in the grate, then shakes off the melancholy and hurries to Dean Street, where a certain pair of bright eyes and whispering lips await him.

She is happy—too happy, she thinks, when poor Hetty mopes in the corner. *It’s wicked of me to be so glad when she’s so low!* Theo scolds herself, though the firelight hides her blushes. Then supper arrives, the lamps are lit, and the family gathers. The fleet’s destination is common talk now: enough force to crush the French in Canada, led by Wolfe—a man too frail in body but mighty in spirit. The ministers have staked everything on him.

After supper, as is his custom, Mr. Lambert calls his household to prayer—George included—and as they kneel, Theo and Hetty side by side, their thoughts chase the ships into the west. Night passes. Dawn comes. Life resumes.

For George, it means burying himself in work. *Carpezan* has had its run; the theater has moved on to fresher spectacles. Some grumble at the fickleness of audiences, but George, ever practical, counts his earnings—a tidy sum from Dodsley the bookseller, another from the theater manager—and sets to work on a new play, determined to outshine his first.

Months slip by in a haze of ink and whispered words. Love, in its first bloom, makes every hour radiant. The joy of reunion each morning, the lingering *goodnight*, the certainty that the world is kind and fortune smiles—who would trade such days for thrones or glory? Even the elders, watching from the sidelines, soften at the sight of youth’s sweet comedy.

In letters to Harry, George recounts the world they’ve left behind: the bungled charge at Minden, where Lord Sackville’s deaf ear (or was it cowardice?) cost them a decisive victory; the dying Duke, his body failing but his pride unbroken; the grand speeches in Parliament, all pomp and no substance. He writes of *Pocahontas*, his new play, and Hagan’s outrage at being cast as an Irish buffoon. (*As if he needs to imitate brogue when his own tongue is musical enough!*)

And then—should he tell? Should he stir the pot? Maria Esmond, the widow who once set her cap at Harry, now haunts the theater, eyes fixed on Hagan. Let her console herself; George has no interest in playing informer. Let the Esmonds chart their own ruin.

*O beati agricolae!* he muses. Dull as Virginia was, it spared them the corruption of London’s gaming hells and false friendships. Harry’s misfortunes began only when he left home. But now—now he has a chance to reclaim his honor, to rise as a soldier, while George contents himself with quieter dreams: a snug house, a well-stocked library, and a certain sweet voice murmuring across the hearth.

As for Miss Van den Bosch, polished to perfection at Kensington School—she is a vision of brown beauty, all grace and wit, though her pretensions amuse Theo and infuriate Hetty. She will marry a lord, no doubt, and leave Monument Yard far behind.

But George’s heart, full as it is, has no room for her. His letters trail into verses—extravagant, tender, foolish—the kind that youth alone can write without shame.

(And if you, dear reader, are too young to understand such raptures, wait. Your spring will come.)

George Warrington had noted in his recent correspondence that despite Lord Castlewood’s past dealings with Harry—transactions of a dubious nature—the ties of kinship remained unbroken between his lordship and George himself. Castlewood was ever the obliging relation, offering franks for George’s letters, securing introductions at court, or even procuring him a seat in the House of Lords should he wish to observe a debate. The viscount was a man of wit and charm, always ready to advance his cousin’s interests—so long as his own were not compromised.

Now, he went so far as to promise his influence in securing a position for George, who found himself increasingly reluctant to return to Virginia. The prospect of resuming life under his mother’s stern dominion held little appeal. London’s society and pursuits delighted him far more than the idle existence of a planter, which might have suited him had it come with independence. In Virginia, he was but the first—and, as he believed, the most ill-treated—of his mother’s subjects. The thought of subjecting his young bride to the tedium, the quarrels, and the stifling hierarchy of home filled him with dread. Better freedom and modest means in England, with its promise of intellectual companionship and future distinction, than the wearisome submission awaiting them across the Atlantic.

Thus, George eagerly embraced Castlewood’s offer of patronage, though the man’s own fortunes were far from secure, and his reputation even less so. But hope clings to what it desires, and George chose to believe in his kinsman’s goodwill.

When George’s engagement to Miss Theo Lambert was announced, the Warringtons might have sniffed at the match, but Lord Castlewood played the gracious relation to perfection. He paid his respects to the Lamberts, extolled George’s virtues to the parents, and Miss Theo’s charms to George, all with such polished ease that the good-natured family forgot his notorious past. In their company, he was the picture of amiability—witty, engaging, even virtuous. It was as though he shed his worldly vices with his cloak at the door, reclaiming them only when he stepped back into his chair.

“I do believe he is improved among us,” Mrs. Lambert murmured, her charitable heart softening further when Castlewood openly admired Theo—without, of course, any serious intent.

“It does not trouble my sleep,” he remarked with a smile, “nor shall I break my heart that Miss Lambert prefers another. Though I confess, had I met such innocence in my youth, it might have spared me much folly.”

“It is not too late for redemption, my lord,” Mrs. Lambert ventured gently.

He startled, misreading her meaning before laughing at his own assumption. “Too late for courtship, certainly—but repentance? Ah, madam, if only the heart could be remade as easily as one learns a language in the nursery. Some natures are cut for virtue, others—like mine—are shaped otherwise.”

She persisted, as women do, in her hopeful prayers for his reform, though he shook his head with a melancholy grace. “You do not know the whole of it,” he said. “Our lives were molded early—selfishness, then necessity, then habit. Fate drives the chariot, and we cannot leap from its path. Yet it grants us glimpses of better souls, like yours.”

Whether the lady’s faith or the gentleman’s cynicism proved truer remained to be seen. But while the debate lingered, an unexpected friendship blossomed between the Lamberts and Castlewood’s household. His ladies paid polite calls; Mrs. Lambert attended their card assemblies, where play was universal—even among bishops and peers—though none were compelled to join. The world, it seemed, was not so black as rumor painted it.

Among the visitors to the Lambert household was young Lydia Van den Bosch, the American granddaughter of George’s mother’s old neighbor. Madam Esmond’s letter had made her ambitions clear—hopes of a match between George and the girl—but with George pledged to Theo and Harry abroad, Mrs. Lambert’s thoughts turned to her own son, Jack.

“Why should he not have her?” she mused to her husband. “She is handsome, rich—”

“And ten times as clever as our Theo, no doubt,” George teased, earning a mock scowl from his betrothed.

“She may be all that,” Theo replied serenely, “but I am not afraid.”

Her steady gaze needed no further words.

Lydia, fresh from her Kensington schooling, returned to her grandfather’s care, and under his indulgent eye, her world expanded. From humble lodgings in the city, they moved to Bloomsbury, where society’s doors creaked open a little wider. The merchant’s granddaughter, once confined to tradesmen’s company, now caught the eye of loftier circles—particularly that of George Warrington, who resided nearby.

Old Mr. Van den Bosch was refreshingly candid about his past—his rise from Albany obscurity, his estranged son, his fortune built on land and trade. “They say I was hard on my boy,” he admitted, ruffling Lydia’s hair. “So now I spoil this one to make amends.”

Lydia, with her lively wit and fearless charm, needed no spoiling to shine. She met George’s gaze with laughing defiance when her grandfather jested that the young man had refused her.

“Nay, sir,” George protested, amused.

“Oh, he is right to deny it!” Lydia retorted, her eyes dancing.

The old man chuckled. “She’ll have more than a trifle when I’m gone—but she’d rather keep her Gappy awhile yet, eh?”

Lydia’s answering embrace was answer enough.

And George, watching, wondered if the girl’s artless affection was as guileless as it seemed—or if those bright eyes hid calculations as shrewd as her grandfather’s.

We must now, before returning to the affairs of Miss Lydia, address a matter left ambiguous in Mr. Warrington’s letter to his brother—namely, the curious fate of Lady Maria Esmond. To those unfamiliar with the caprices of the heart, it may seem unfathomable that a woman of noble birth, seasoned by years and experience, who had so recently been entangled in one ardent attachment, could so swiftly transfer her affections to another—and one so far beneath her station! Yet love, as any observer of human nature knows, is no respecter of rank or reason.

Consider: if men are permitted their fickle diversions, why should women be denied the same? The great Hamlet himself railed unjustly against his mother’s remarriage, blind to the natural longing for companionship that persists even in widowhood. Hearts are not monuments, unchanging through the ages, but living things—hungry, restless, and ever seeking sustenance. Lady Maria’s affection, once fixed upon her young cousin, had not expired so much as wandered, alighting now upon a new object: Mr. Hagan, the actor who had so movingly performed in George’s ill-fated tragedy.

Clad in gilt armor and a grand periwig, Hagan had declaimed his lines with such fire that the lady’s heart was irrevocably conquered. And he, though doubtless startled by the ardor of so distinguished a suitor, was not insensible to her devotion. Their union, hastily solemnized by Parson Sampson in a Mayfair chapel, sent shockwaves through the ranks of polite society.

The outcry was immediate. Lady Maria’s kin disowned her; the Countess of Castlewood donned mourning as if for the dead; Mr. Will vowed violent retribution upon the "upstart player"; and Madame de Bernstein, most incensed of all, retreated to Tunbridge Wells in high dudgeon. Even General Lambert, though he jested about the match at home, admitted that no respectable household could now receive the fallen lady.

Yet for all the scorn heaped upon them, the couple endured. Hagan, though his theatrical income dwindled under the scandal’s shadow, remained steadfast. They shared cramped Westminster lodgings with his aging mother and schoolboy brother—a far cry from the luxuries of Esmond Hall, but Maria, to her credit, did not waver.

When George visited the pair, he found them in meager but resolute spirits. Hagan himself was absent, but Maria—no longer the polished society matron—sat playing cribbage with a neighbor while Mrs. Hagan sipped punch by the fire. There was a quiet dignity in her resilience, a defiance of expectation that even George could not help but admire.

Meanwhile, another quarrel brewed—this time between George and his hotheaded cousin Will. At the Van den Bosch residence, where both paid court to the heiress Lydia, Will’s provocations grew unbearable. Mocking Harry’s military exploits, sneering at George’s restraint, he finally drove the latter to fling a glass of wine in his face. A duel was narrowly averted by the timely intervention of the law, leaving both men bound over to keep the peace—much to the relief of their friends.

Thus love, in its many forms, stirred tumult: binding an actress to a lady, pitting cousin against cousin, and proving, as ever, that the heart follows no rule but its own.

The disagreement between George and his cousin Will had driven George to avoid Bloomsbury Square, for Will’s presence there had grown intolerable. Bound by their pledge to keep the peace, Will had taken to taunting George with such relentless spite that it was all George could do not to throttle him on the spot.

Lydia, ever artless, had developed a peculiar habit of praising Theo in George’s presence. Only weeks ago, she had been consumed with jealousy over his affections for another; now, she took every opportunity to declare how perfectly suited he and Theo were.

“How fortunate that dreadful duel was prevented!” she remarked one afternoon, her voice dripping with sweetness. “Those constables arrived just in time, did they not? And poor Mr. Esmond—such a temper! But I’m sure Miss Theo must be grateful. She would have been quite heartbroken if anything had happened to you.”

Such remarks, delivered with wide-eyed innocence before company, did little to endear her to George. He found himself excusing himself from dinners, inventing engagements—anything to avoid the spectacle of Will’s gloating or Lydia’s barbed compliments.

When the truth of the duel’s near-occurrence reached Dean Street, the Lambert household was in an uproar.

“You meant to kill a man, and you sat in our parlor saying nothing of it?” Theo exclaimed, aghast.

“He insulted my brother and me,” George defended. “Would you have had me swallow the lie that we were cowards?”

General Lambert listened in grim silence, his disapproval palpable.

“You’ve always said dueling was wicked, Papa,” Theo pressed.

“To refuse a challenge takes courage,” the General admitted reluctantly.

“A courage I’m not sure I possess,” muttered Jack Lambert, his expression dark. “If any man called me a coward, I’d forget every sermon I’ve ever preached.”

“There! You see?” George said triumphantly.

Jack met his gaze. “I cannot condemn you, Mr. Warrington.”

“*Mr. Warrington*?” George echoed, stung.

“Would you have him break God’s law?” Theo demanded of her brother.

“I am a soldier’s son,” Jack replied tersely. “And no murder was done.”

Hetty, pale at this turn in the conversation, was swiftly ushered out by Theo. George, sensing some unspoken accusation, rose abruptly.

“Do you mean to say,” he demanded, “that you believe I shrank from this fight?”

The General’s stern face softened. “I told Jack it was a lie.”

“A lie? What lie?” George’s temper flared.

Jack flushed. “Miss Lydia implied you had… avoided the duel by less honorable means.”

George’s blood ran hot. “That little viper!”

“Do not speak of her so!” Jack snapped, his own anger rising. “She is innocence itself! If she was mistaken, it was out of relief that bloodshed was averted.”

“Mistaken?” George laughed bitterly. “She slandered me deliberately. And you believed her?”

Jack looked wretched. “I—I may have been swayed by jealousy. I confess, I feared she favored you.”

The admission hung between them. George, stunned, realized the truth: Jack was in love with Lydia.

The General, re-entering, found them at an impasse. “Made your peace, lads?”

“Not quite,” George muttered.

Jack, red-faced, excused himself shortly after dinner. George, too, left early—ostensibly on business, but in truth, he meant to confront Lydia.

At Bloomsbury Square, however, he found Jack already there, pacing before the door. The servant informed them that the household had departed for Hampshire—to Castlewood, no less.

“Gone?” Jack’s voice cracked. “With *him*?”

George’s jaw tightened. Will had clearly wasted no time.

Aunt Lambert arrived in her sedan chair, only to learn her errand was pointless. Jack, in a black fury, stormed off.

George, left seething, spent the evening in a fruitless search for Will. The next morning, he penned two letters—one to Will, calling him a liar to his face, and another to Van den Bosch, warning him against slander.

Days later, a reply arrived—not from Will, but from Castlewood himself.

*Dear Cousin,* the letter began, with infuriating civility. The Earl announced his impending marriage to Lydia, dismissed Will’s temper as a passing fit, and extended hollow invitations for reconciliation.

George read it with disgust. Castlewood, nearing sixty, had secured himself a wealthy young bride, and Will had been neatly sidelined. The London papers soon confirmed it: the Earl of Castlewood had wed Miss Van den Bosch of Virginia, her fortune trumpeted for all to see.

The game, it seemed, had been played—and George had lost a round. But the match was far from over.

As I sit by the hearth, gazing across the fire at the dear companion who has shared my joys and sorrows these many years, I often find myself wondering—with the fond repetition of middle age—whether any two young fools were ever so reckless as we when we married in the year of the old King’s death. My son Miles, who has taken many a wild leap in pursuit of a fox, confesses that he looks back on those perilous gaps and rivers with a shudder, amazed at his own audacity. Yet none rides harder than he does now. He has broken bones and given his mother many a fright, just as his father—lost in verse or quarter-session speeches—has been flung more than once from his drowsy mare. But Miles’s daring has been his delight, as my marriage has been my blessing, and I thank Heaven for both.

Not that I would set myself up as an example. I do not advise young lovers to wed on a pittance—our vestry would frown upon such imprudence. Yet my wife, bless her, remains an incurable matchmaker. When some rustic pair appears before me pleading for parish relief, we counsel them to save their half-crowns, add a trifle of our own, and summon the vicar.

My dear wife’s thoughts are ever in harmony with mine; I need only wear a certain expression, and her answer is already mirrored in her eyes. If I remark, *"My love, does not the moon tonight resemble a wheel of cream cheese?"* she will reply, *"Indeed, papa, your similes are as droll as ever."* Should I observe, *"Mr. Pitt’s speech was fine, but he is no match for his father,"* she agrees at once: *"No one could equal Lord Chatham."* Then one of our girls will cry, *"But I’ve heard you call Lord Chatham a charlatan!"*—to which my wife sighs, *"How like her Aunt Hetty!"*

As for Miles, he cares little for politics or poetry. The sporting columns of the *Norwich Chronicle* are his sole literature. He has never read my verses—much less my tragedies—and when I mentioned *Pocahontas*, the rascal thought her a Virginian river. My Latin epics lie unread beneath my spectacles, though they are left in plain sight. I have even sketched unflattering caricatures of him in these pages, yet he neither notices nor minds—save once, when he replaced my drawing of him asleep with a crude sketch of myself tumbling from my mare, labeled *"The Squire on Horseback, or Fish out of Water."* The rogue roared with laughter, and the girls giggled as I turned the page. My wife swears she never endured such terror as when I opened the book—but I can bear a jest, even at my own expense.

Yet I love Miles none the less for his lack of wit or beauty. His mother sees in him the likeness of Apollo—or the Prince of Wales, born in the same year. She keeps a print of Mr. Coates’s portrait of His Royal Highness in her dressing-room, insisting upon the resemblance. *[Note, in a feminine hand: "My son is no spendthrift, nor a breaker of hearts, as some gentlemen are; but that he bore a striking likeness to H.R.H. in infancy is certain, for the Duchess of Aneaster herself remarked upon it in St. James’s Park, where Gumbo and my dear Molly often took him for air. Th. W."]*

That same year also saw the birth of Lord Esmond, Lord Castlewood’s heir—though under far different circumstances. My lord and I were already estranged by then, and no golden pap-boat graced my Miles’s christening. Yet fortune has since smiled upon us—*laus Deo!*—for neither he nor I could have mended our lot by mere wit.

Castlewood House now wears a face quite altered from the venerable one I knew in my youth—its scars of time smoothed, its ancient cracks repaired. Yet I prefer to remember it as it was when young George Warrington rode down at his lordship’s invitation to witness his marriage to Miss Lydia Van den Bosch, *"an American lady of noble Dutch lineage,"* as the county papers declared. Then, the towers stood as Colonel Esmond—my grandfather—had known them. The woods, though thinned, still rustled with the same rooks, perhaps, that had cawed in his day. His portrait hung in the hall—a man who had chosen love over title—and as I wandered those corridors, I fancied myself one of Mr. Walpole’s cavaliers, rapier at my side, half-expecting a Jacobite conspirator to step from the shadows.

Strange, sad, sweet fancies filled me then. I dreamed of old tragedies, intrigues, serenades beneath moonlit windows—of Roundheads battering the gates, or Hal’s beefeaters drilling on the lawn. I was courting a certain lady then—ah, madam, your eyes needed no spectacles in those days!—and I filled reams with romantic letters, which she devoured eagerly. Now, she complains I send but three lines when I am away. *"’Tis to spare your eyes, my dear!"*

For the wedding, I commissioned a fine new suit and presented myself at Castlewood with Monsieur Gumbo in attendance—my faithful Sady having been sent home to Virginia, his lungs too frail for English winters. The house overflowed with guests: sulky Lady Fanny as bridesmaid, neighboring virgins in tow, and the Baroness Beatrix, widowed bishop’s wife, bringing a reverend brother-in-law to tie the knot. For a time, the old halls rang with merriment long unheard.

The talk, of course, was of the bride’s fortune—seventy thousand pounds, by the papers, though rumor doubled it. The country folk cared little for town gossip about jilted suitors; they welcomed the reopening of Castlewood’s kitchens, the stables filled, the spits turning once more. Her ladyship assumed her place with dignity, her wit as sharp as her accounts.

Her grandfather, that quiet, keen-eyed Dutchman, set to work at once, mending rents, trimming waste, scrutinizing every flagon of beer, every yard of lace. The servants grumbled, but were paid; the horses thrived on less grain; the gardens flourished. From his turret chamber, the old man ruled invisibly, his thrift steady as time.

Yet not all rejoiced in this new order. The dowager Countess and Lady Fanny soon found themselves outmatched by the young American’s will and wit. They retreated to their jointure house in Kensington—unmourned by my lord, who seldom grieved for absent faces.

And what of cousin Warrington? Did I expect Lord Castlewood to sorrow at my departure, my misfortunes, my death? I knew better. A man of skeptical temper, I observed my neighbors with grim amusement, liking this one despite his hollow core. What are courage and heart to the world? A man may lack both, so long as he is agreeable, keeps a decent figure, and pays his way.

I often studied the portrait of Colonel Esmond—my grandfather—who might once have held these very titles. *"He must have been touched here,"* my lord once remarked, tapping his temple. There are acts some men deem madness—renouncing wealth, title, ambition—that others cannot fathom.

*"I do you justice, cousin,"* I told him once, *"you would surrender no advantage for any man."*

*"Selfish? But am I worse than the world?"* he replied with a shrug, taking snuff.

Once, in a scarlet coat, he fled a cow with schoolboy agility, then laughed at his own cowardice. *"I daresay you respect me no more than I do myself, George,"* he said, and launched into a wry discourse on human frailty—why Heaven had not made us all brave, tall, and rich.

And I? Though I loved to play the king among my company, I could not help but like him—this polished, witty, high-born kinsman, so utterly my inferior. His look seemed to say, *"Do not beat me—but if you must, I will not resist."* And what man, save a bully, strikes a creature so defenseless?

My dear children, we know from our beloved fairy tales how at every feast—be it christening or wedding—some discontented soul nurses a grudge and swears vengeance. So it was no surprise when our fiery cousin Will erupted in curses upon hearing of his brother’s betrothal to the colonial heiress. At first, he stormed from the house, vowing never to return. But Will’s oaths were as fleeting as his tempers, and within days, the prodigal slunk back to the family table. The aroma of roasted meats and spiced wine proved too tempting; he could not forsake Castlewood’s bounty, even in his wrath.

He drank deeply, toasting the bride with venom beneath his breath. As the chapel bells chimed and my lord pledged to love and cherish her, Will muttered responses fit for a tavern brawl. Yet he was not alone in his disdain. Mr. Warrington heard no end of grievances against the new Countess—how she had enchanted and discarded suitors like a siren luring ships to wreck. The City merchants, the Dissenting preacher, even poor Jack Lambert, who had hoped to save her soul, all spat her name like a curse. The lawyer, once so eager to tally her estates, now scoffed, "No such place as Virginia! A scheme, a damned colonial trick!"

As for Will, he nursed his grievance like a wound. "That little Cerberus!" he snarled. "A she-devil with a dowry!"
