Warrington, ever amused, replied, "Then you ought to thank the gods for sparing you such a wife. If she poisons Castlewood, better him than you."

Will clenched his fists. "Aye, but the fortune—*my* fortune—stolen by that grinning Judas!"

Warrington arched a brow. "And how, pray, did you risk your life for it?"

Here Will grew red as a beet, confessing how Lydia herself had provoked their near-duel—how she’d mocked Warrington as a coward, taunted Will for enduring his cousin’s jibes. "Damme, I near skewered you in Tottenham Fields!"

Warrington laughed. "Lucky for me the constables came. You looked ready to spit me like a capon."

Now, the new Countess ruled Castlewood with a scepter of iron. She mastered chapel protocol, her footmen bearing her prayer book as solemnly as a bishop’s procession. She lectured the parson on divinity, the bailiff on crops, and the village midwives on childbirth—though she’d yet to bear a child herself. She even decreed the fate of old Lockwood, the ancient porter, until Warrington intervened.

"Respect his years, my lady," he chided.

She scoffed. "Why? Old folks ain’t handsome, nor amusing. Look at Aunt Bernstein—once a beauty, they say. Pfah! When *I* grow shriveled, I hope someone puts me out of my misery!"

Aunt Bernstein arrived for Christmas, sharp-tongued and imperious. She had orchestrated this marriage, and she expected gratitude. "The girl has fire," she mused. "A bit savage, but we’ll polish her."

Yet Lydia was no puppet. She dismissed the dowager’s airs, commanded the servants like a general, and declared old portraits "musty relics." When Bernstein praised her, the Countess retorted, "I ain’t here to be *formed*, madam. I’m Lady Castlewood now."

And so Christmas passed—amid feasts, quarrels, and the unspoken question: *Who truly rules here?* The old order or the new?

Our kin at Castlewood extended their hospitality until the new year’s turn, and after a fortnight’s absence—an eternity to a lovestruck young fool—I returned to my sweetheart’s side. Madame de Bernstein seemed almost relieved to depart from her ancestral home. The farther we traveled, the more freely she spoke, though she never divulged what passed in that fiery confrontation with her niece. The old woman no longer spoke of molding *cette petite*, and when she mentioned the young Countess at all, it was with a nervous laugh, devoid of malice.

“My nephew Eugene,” she mused, “is fated to be henpecked all his days—that much is clear. A little order in that household will do wonders. That sharp little American fellow will make a decent steward. As for the girl’s mother—well, they say she was a convict shipped from England. No need to shout it from the rooftops. And really, does birth matter so much for such people? She’s clever enough, and she’ll learn English soon enough. The name she’s claimed will open every door.”

She dismissed old Van den Bosch’s dubious past with a wave. “A smuggler, a slave-trader—what does it matter? The Countess of Castlewood can afford to be anyone’s daughter. Now that my nephew has presented her, we must stand by her.”

I had hoped her affection might extend to my own choice—dear Hetty Lambert—but here the Baroness proved immovable. When I spoke of Hagan and his wife, her face darkened. “Do not speak that name,” she snapped, before proceeding to talk of nothing else for hours. The tales she spun of Lady Maria’s escapades shall not stain these pages, for they are unfit for young eyes. Yet let it be said: if she sinned, she was not alone, and if she repented, others might take heed.

Hagan, after leaving the stage, led a blameless life, his eloquence finding a nobler calling in the pulpit. His wife embraced fervent piety, though I suspected her spiritual fervor owed more to strong drink than divine revelation. Still, I remembered her kindness in darker days, when colder hearts turned away.

My aunt’s favor was as fickle as the wind. After my play’s success, I basked briefly in society’s glow, admitted to the wits’ circle, deemed passably genteel by the macaronis. I might have risen among them, had my purse been deeper—or had I not preferred a pair of quiet, loving eyes to all the painted beauties of Ranelagh.

“You might have married anyone,” lamented my pious Lady Warrington, her gaze ever flitting between heaven and the ledger. My uncle, ever the pragmatist, sighed. “A hundred thousand pounds, thrown away for a penniless country girl!”

“But I gave my word,” I protested.

“Words are wind, boy! Duty to family—to the Fifth Commandment!—demands prudence.” He spoke of his own ambitions: his son Miley, paired with Nabob Barwell’s daughter at dancing school. “I told the Nabob myself—’twould be a fine match!”

Yet I held fast to my choice, and the old man turned away in disgust. The Baroness, too, scoffed at my rustic attachments. My star waned; Harry’s rose. And why not? I bore no jealousy.

From the war’s front, Harry’s letters came—brief, plain-spoken. At first, the Baroness paid them little heed. But then came news of Minden, where Wolfe’s old regiment had fought gloriously. And then—Quebec.

Ah, Quebec! That bold, mad gamble! Wolfe, fever-wracked, scaling the heights under cover of night, drawing Montcalm into open battle against all odds. Had the ascent been detected an hour sooner, had the French held their lines—what then? Yet Fortune smiled. The audacity of a gambler, the genius of a general—who can say which won the day?

When word reached England, despair turned to delirium. The nation exulted. Friends embraced in the streets; coffeehouses buzzed. Chatham, the architect of victory, was hailed as a demigod. And Harry—my dear Harry, who had shared in that immortal moment—was a hero by association. Even Uncle Warrington wrote in jubilation. Aunt Beatrix held court, basking in reflected glory. I, by mere resemblance, was fêted. Parson Sampson preached a sermon so rousing his flock nearly cheered in the pews.

“Grief?” scoffed General Lambert when his wife mourned Wolfe’s burial. “If our boys could die so, would you deny them? Would you not climb those cliffs yourself to see it done?”

Little Charley, wide-eyed, agreed. “I’d not mind it—and we got a holiday from school besides.”

As for Harry, his promotion was assured. And the Baroness, ever practical, announced she would buy him a company.

Thus ends the tale—not with sorrow, but with a nation’s pride, a brother’s triumph, and the inexorable turn of fate’s wheel.

Had your father, dear children, possessed even a grain of common sense, this chapter—nay, your very existence—might never have come to pass. But no, he must fling prudence to the wind, and thus you were born to plague him in a thousand ways: clattering through the halls when he craved quiet, rousing him from his after-dinner doze, misplacing his spectacles, pilfering his newspaper, and draining his purse with tailors’ bills, tutors’ fees, and mantua-makers’ extravagances. And when one of you fell ill—ah, then came the true torment! For though he might have slept soundly, your mother would not permit it, fretting and pacing as if the world depended on her vigilance. And so, between the lot of you, his peace was forever shattered.

Now that you have outgrown childhood’s afflictions—petticoats, chickenpox, scarlet fever, and the like—what fresh torment arises? Your mother, ever the schemer, now speaks of preparing the south rooms as a nursery for future grandchildren. She insists that Miles must marry early, as we did, to save him from the perils of Brookes’s and Goosetree’s, as though a young man’s wild oats were not his natural due. Marry! My dear, we had no business marrying at all! By every law of prudence, I should have withdrawn from my engagement to Miss Theo and sought a wealthy bride instead. Your Uncle John was a parson, too meek to call me to account; Charley but a schoolboy; and your grandfather too aged to challenge me with pistols. Ours was a match of pure folly, and our kin were right to disapprove. What are relations for, if not to disapprove?

Mark this, George: when Hester weds, you must quarrel with her choice if it displeases you. And Hester, when George takes holy orders, you must turn up your nose at his bride. As for you, my little Theo—no, you shall never leave me. You must stay to play Haydn and crack my walnuts.

*(Here, in a girlish scrawl upon the margin, is written: “I never intend to go.—THEODOSIA.” And beneath it: “Nor I.—HESTER.” Yet they did, both of them—Theodosia to Joseph Clinton, son of the Reverend Joseph Blake, and Hester to Captain F. Handyman, R.N., as the family Bible records.)*

In those days, your parents dwelt in a fool’s paradise, blind to the world’s meddling. Rinaldo was no more enchanted by Armida’s garden than I was by Theo’s presence. What magic did she wield, that neither glory nor gaming nor the company of wits could lure me from her side? Our hours together were endless, our talk more delightful than the finest wit in London. I scarce noticed when the King of Prussia lost his capital—what were Leipzig or Berlin to us? That house in Dean Street was an Eden, and I have known idleness since, but never such joy.

Yet once, and briefly, that joy was eclipsed. A shadow fell, so dark that I contemplated highway robbery, enlisting under Prussia’s banner, or sailing to India on some desperate venture—anything to escape the agony of separation. For we *were* parted, and the pain of it was such that your mother still whispers of it in dread. Had we remained so, what would have become of us? The thought turns the world to ash.

The blow came from an unexpected quarter. My English kin, deeming themselves wise, had written to Madam Esmond in Virginia, decrying my engagement as folly. Aunt Bernstein led the charge, lamenting Theo’s lack of fortune and beauty. Lady Warrington, ever pious, draped her disapproval in scripture. Even the new Countess of Castlewood, lofty in her rank, deemed it her duty to intervene. Their letters crossed the sea, and one bitter April morning, I found not Theo’s welcome smile, but the Lambert’s grief-stricken faces.

Madam Esmond’s letter lay before me, its words courteous but cold. She implied that the Lamberts had ensnared me, that they had schemed first for Henry, then for me. She urged them, for their daughter’s sake, to end the engagement. The General, pale as death, said, “She will insist on it herself. She is too dutiful to defy her parents.”

And she did. I saw her once more—only once. She came to me, pale but steady, and said, “Good-bye, dear George.” Then she was gone.

The days that followed were torment. I kept my word and stayed away, but by night I haunted their street, watching her window. I bribed her brothers with cakes and coins, dined with them, clung to any thread of connection. The doctor who attended her became my sole confidant, for he had known sorrow himself and pitied me. He told me she was ill—not gravely, but listless, fading. “She bears it with angelic patience,” he said.

Then came a morning when, waiting in his parlor, I saw a veiled figure. A voice said, “Don’t you know me, George?” And there was Hetty—dear, brave Hetty—who had stolen away to bring me comfort.

But that is another tale.

The first shock of their meeting past, Hetty Lambert wasted no time in laying bare her grievances.

"So, you’ve finally come to inquire after Theo?" she began, her voice sharp with accusation. "And now you’re sorry your neglect has made her ill? Six weeks she’s been unwell, and not a word from you! Charming attention, Mr. George, truly!"

"Neglect?" Warrington gasped, stung.

"Oh, don’t pretend! A year at her side, day after day, and then—poof!—gone without so much as a farewell! Is that what you call devotion?"

"I gave your father my word—"

"Your *word*!" Hetty scoffed, tossing her head. "A fine thing, a man’s word! Enough to break a girl’s heart and then vanish on a whim. If I were a man, I’d know better than to play such cruel games. But then, I suppose gentlemen make promises just for the pleasure of breaking them."

Warrington, exasperated, flung out his hands. "Good God, Hetty! Was it I who withdrew? Was it not your father who forbade me the house, who bound me by my honor never to see her again?"

"Honor!" she cried, her eyes flashing. "You keep your oath to him and break faith with her! A pretty notion of honor indeed! If I were a man—" Here her voice faltered, the fire dimming to anguish. "Oh, George, I scarcely know what I say. I am half-mad with grief. You mustn’t leave her. They don’t see it at home—but I know her best. She will *die* if you forsake her. Have pity, I beg you!"

From fury to tears she swung, her small frame trembling. Was she right? Was Theo’s life truly hanging by so frail a thread? Love, Warrington knew, could wound as deeply as any blade—yet surely hearts mended in time? Had not Hetty herself borne such a blow and survived? But no—this was different. Their love was no common passion, and the thought of Theo fading away unmanned him utterly.

The physician, when consulted, would hear none of it. "I’ll have no part in matchmaking," he declared, eyes twinkling despite his stern tone. "If two patients of mine chance to meet and converse, so be it—but meddle in affairs of the heart? Not I! Unless you wish the General to call me out at Montagu House?" And with that, he took his leave, leaving Warrington to his torment.

Yet fate, ever capricious, soon intervened. Days later, as Warrington wandered the Tottenham Road, a familiar landau rolled into view—and there, beside the driver, sat young Charley Lambert, waving madly. Within, propped against cushions, pale as mist but with eyes alight, was Theo.

"The doctor prescribed air," Hetty said primly. "Hampstead should do her good."

"I have been ill," Theo murmured, her voice a fragile thread. "But I am better now."

At that moment, a hymn swelled from the chapel nearby—a soaring, triumphant psalm. Warrington seized her hand, and in her gaze, he found the same unspoken truth that had always bound them. No past, no parting, existed in that look.

Charley, grinning down from his perch, was swiftly dispatched with Hetty to hear the sermon ("I’ve a great curiosity about Mr. Whitfield!" Hetty announced, dragging her brother away), leaving Warrington alone with Theo.

What passed between them in that landau? The old Squire, recounting the tale years later, teased his listeners mercilessly.

"Shall I divulge your mother’s secrets?" he asked, eyes dancing as his children clamored.

"Tell us!" cried the girls, while Mademoiselle Eleonore hid a smile behind her hand.

"Very well," said the Squire, unfolding his manuscript with mock solemnity. "There we were—a young scoundrel, breaking every promise, and a lady too good for him..."

But the heart of the matter was this: Warrington, honor be damned, leapt into that carriage. And Theo, though she chided him gently, did not let go of his hand.

Captain Miles Warrington, you have the honor of winning the favor of a lady—or perhaps many ladies. The Duchess of Devonshire, Mrs. Crew, Mrs. Fitzherbert, even the Queen of Prussia—what matter who? But when a gentleman secures a lady’s regard, does he rush to the mess to boast of it?

"Not such a fool, Squire!" retorts the Captain, admiring his reflection.

"And you, Miss Theo—did you recount every word exchanged with young Mr. Blake in the shrubbery this morning?"

"Joe Blake? Really!" scoffs Theo, flushing.

"And you, mademoiselle? That perfumed letter delivered under Sir Thomas’s seal—have you disclosed its contents? Ah, see how she colors! Crimson as the curtain! No, my friends, we all keep our secrets." (Here the Squire executes his finest French bow.) "The shrubbery held only nuts, my dear. And as for my own tale—what passed in a carriage on the Hampstead Road, the 25th of May, 1760—may the Chevalier Ruspini pluck out every tooth in my head before I reveal it!"

"Tell us, papa!" implores my wife.

"Or summon Jobson from the stables—he drove us that day! I insist!"

"What is this mystery?" asks mademoiselle, her accent charming.

My wife leans close. "What did I say? *Yes*—that was all."

And so it is she who confesses, not I. That single word was the sum of our exchange as the carriage sped toward Hampstead and back again. Theo had not consented to defy her parents—no. But she would marry no other, though we lived as long as Methuselah. Not the Prince of Wales himself could sway her. Her heart had been given with her father’s blessing; it was no longer hers to withdraw. Surely, in time, his kindness would relent. And if I kept my vow—whether now, in twenty years, or beyond this life—she would never break hers.

Hetty’s face glowed when she saw Theo’s serene expression. No physician’s tonic had ever wrought such a change. The sisters returned home, their act of defiance complete. I surrendered my place beside my dear invalid to Hetty, while Charley resumed his post on the box. Later, he admitted the sermon had been dull—too orthodox for his taste.

Hetty was not the only one to notice Theo’s transformation. Their mother embraced them both warmly, praising the virtues of country air. "Another ride tomorrow—and the next day! Lord Wrotham’s horses are at our disposal!"

Did she suspect our meeting? Perhaps. Women have ways of aiding such affairs—little schemes, whispered confidences, averted glances while lovers steal their moments. Or perhaps, like Miss Prim, they snarl and suspect the worst. But Aunt Lambert was no Miss Prim. She was kindness itself, and if she guessed, she gave no sign.

At supper, the General rejoiced at Theo’s renewed appetite. "If a coach-and-six restores her, she shall have it!" he declared.

Yet Theo, ever honest, could not long deceive him. When he marveled again at her improvement, she laid a hand on his. "Papa, it is because I have seen him."

The room stilled.

"Not wrongfully," she pressed. "But I could not hide it from you."

The General paled. "God above—"

"It was my doing!" Hetty cried, rushing to Theo’s side.

Theo clung to her sister. "We are bound, papa. No priest or promise can undo it."

"Women!" the General thundered. "Do you play with my honor?"

Aunt Lambert wept. "Martin—do not speak so!"

But Theo had fainted, her frail form collapsing against Hetty.

---

The next morning, General Lambert stormed into my lodgings.

"Your accomplices did not confess," he said coldly. "But Theo herself told me."

"Accomplices?" I countered. "Your children love you too well to betray justice. Must your pride dictate their hearts?"

"Do not twist my words, George. You swore not to seek her."

"I did not. She came to me."

"You hide behind a girl’s petticoat?"

"Very well—no more evasion. I broke the spirit of my vow, though not the letter. I revoke it now. No promise made in anguish should bind forever. Would you have us part, marry others, while our hearts lie elsewhere? Call that honor? I call it cruelty."

"Dishonor—"

"No!" I rose, fierce. "Were you my own father, I would not endure that word. Theo is mine before God. If she calls, I will answer. If she comes, this house is hers. You gave her to me—you cannot take her back. Must she wither to soothe your pride? Marry her to a prince tomorrow, and it is still betrayal. She is my wife, or she is dead. Choose."

His face darkened. "Then we part as enemies. My family will know you no more."

---

That afternoon, I met Jack Lambert at the Museum. Desperate for solace, I poured out my grief. To my surprise, he sided with me.

"Father questioned me at dinner," Jack said. "I told him: once consent is given, it cannot be revoked. Marriage is sacred, whether before a magistrate or Heaven itself."

He recounted the scene—Theo pale but resolute, Hetty’s whispered joke about sewing shirts, their father’s reluctant surrender.

"*You should have heard Jack’s sermon!*" Hetty had teased.

Theo, too weak for debates, had only wept upon the General’s shoulder.

"*I cited Pausanias,*" Jack said, grinning. "*When Ulysses took Penelope, her father begged her return. She covered her face—as Theo hid in our father’s coat!*"

Laughter had followed, though their mother vowed *she* would have left any suitor on the spot.

"*But I am no woman of spirit,*" Theo had murmured.

---

From despair to hope—how swiftly the tide turned! Jack’s words flooded me with joy. That night, I drank too freely, rhapsodizing beneath Theo’s window (or so Jack claimed). The next morning, though my head throbbed, my heart was light.

I learned later that the General, wounded by my words, had sought solace in prayer. There, kneeling, he saw the truth: Theo’s loyalty was no rebellion, but love. His own pride had blinded him. And so, before Jack had even pleaded, the battle was won.

For a father’s heart, when tender and true, cannot long resist the plea of happiness—especially when it echoes his own youthful ardor.

Years later, among the old family papers at home, I discovered a packet labeled in my mother’s precise hand: *"From London, April, 1760. My son’s dreadful letter."* When it fell into my possession, I burned it. Some wounds are not meant to be preserved for posterity. Nor did I keep her reply—a missive of cold dignity, each word measured, every line a reproach wrapped in scripture.

At the time, my heart was too raw for temperance. Maddened by the forced separation from my Theo, I had written to Virginia in a fury, declaring my mother the architect of my misery. I accused her of destroying not just my happiness, but that of an innocent girl whose health and spirit had been broken by her interference. I told her plainly that the bond between us was severed, that I could no longer be her dutiful son.

She answered as a woman certain of heaven’s approval, invoking divine judgment as her shield. How often do we call upon God to justify our own will? In our quarrels, our ambitions, our stubborn refusals—do we not demand the skies themselves ratify our choices? My mother did so with the zeal of a prophetess, while I, in my rebellion, clung to my own righteousness.

Yet love, once rooted, is not so easily torn up. Theo and I had tried to part, but the separation was unbearable. Her father, kind despite his disapproval, saw the truth: we were Pyramus and Thisbe, whispering through the wall. The more he sought to divide us, the tighter our hands clasped through the cracks.

And so, in defiance of all authority, we found our way back to one another. I took to riding beside her carriage during her convalescent drives. Hetty, ever the vigilant chaperone, developed a sudden fascination with the scenery outside the window. Letters flew between us, carried by the indefatigable Gumbo and Theo’s maid, Molly. By autumn, the roses had returned to Theo’s cheeks—and hope to my heart.

Meanwhile, the world beyond our little drama turned. King George II died; a new reign began. The change meant little to us then, though it would alter our fortunes in ways we could not foresee. For now, we were content in our stolen moments, weaving dreams of a shared future—a cottage, a humble living, a life built despite the storms of disapproval.

Then came the thunderclap: General Lambert was appointed Governor of Jamaica. A frigate waited to carry him and his family across the sea. The news struck like a blade. Theo, pale as death, trembled at the thought of parting. Even Hetty, usually so composed, wept. Only Aunt Lambert, practical as ever, murmured something about *"orders for the ladies’ outfits"*—and a certain dressmaker who remembered a young Miss Esmond of Virginia.

That evening, I walked home in a daze. Gumbo, usually so cheerful, was inconsolable. His sweetheart Molly would be sailing away too. We were a pair of broken-hearted fools, he and I.

But love, once kindled, is not so easily quenched. If walls divide us, we will whisper through them still. If oceans part us, we will find a way across. For Theo and I, the story was not yet over.

The weight of our guilt from the morning’s transgression pressed heavily upon us as we witnessed the pain it inflicted upon the man we revered above all others. The blow dealt to his gentle heart was cruel, and those of us who had conspired in it could scarcely bear the sight. His beloved child had betrayed his trust—his entire family had united in deception against him. Dear, forgiving soul! I believe now, my love, that none of us could have repeated such an offense, knowing the anguish it caused. Kindness was as natural to him as breathing, and to wound him was an act of barbarity, like striking a defenseless child.

Once the deed was done, we who had wronged him groveled in remorse, seeking his pardon. The scenes of reconciliation, the shared prayers, the final parting—all were etched in sorrow. When his ship sailed away, Theo and I stood upon the shore, hand in hand, silent and ashamed. Until his departure, she had remained dutifully at his side, occupying her old place in the household, while I lingered at the edges, unwelcome. The atmosphere at home was strained: Aunt Lambert, usually so affectionate, turned sharp-tongued; little Hetty grew restless, murmuring, *“I wish we were gone.”*

At last, they departed, and Theo was mine alone. We retreated to Winchester and Hampton, though our wedding trip was shadowed by melancholy. It felt as though we had buried a father rather than celebrated a union. I instructed Sampson to announce our marriage in the papers (an act which later made my wife blush in the poor man’s presence), and we settled into modest lodgings in Bloomsbury, where our quiet married life began.

I wrote to my mother in Virginia, informing her of my marriage in the barest terms, emphasizing my intention to study law and seek employment either at home or abroad. The reply came not from her, but through Mrs. Mountain, who coolly relayed that Madam Esmond preferred all further correspondence to pass through her. My family’s outrage was almost comical—my uncle’s butler in Hill Street greeted me with a stare of theatrical horror, and when we encountered Lady Warrington in her chair, she nearly choked on her own indignation.

*“I—I wonder you dare look me in the face!”* she spluttered.
*“Nay, madam, why deny me so precious a privilege?”* I replied, sweeping her the grandest of bows before she snapped her chair shut and ordered her bearers onward.

Even little cousin Miles, exercising in Hyde Park, defied his parents’ orders long enough to shout, *“I’m not to speak to you, but sending my love to Harry isn’t speaking, is it? And you’re very pretty!”* before his scandalized groom hauled him away.

*“You see how my relations receive me,”* I remarked to Theo.
*“As if I married you for their sake!”* she answered, her eyes alight with such devotion that all else seemed trivial.

And so we were happy—blissfully so. The winter passed in cozy contentment: evenings by the fire, nights at the theater (where we eagerly anticipated the debut of my *Pocahontas*), and occasional visits from friends. Even the formidable Madame de Bernstein, after initial disdain, thawed toward Theo, though she made it clear we were no longer *du monde*. *“You may come amuse me, my dears,”* she declared, *“but you must use the back stairs.”*

The Dowager Countess of Castlewood extended a tepid welcome, inviting us to her Kensington assemblies, but the cost of cards and coaches soon outweighed the pleasure, and we withdrew without regret. Theo, radiant even in her mother’s modest jewels, cared nothing for the glittering dowagers who ignored her. Only one insolent fop—some friend of Will’s—dared send her a billet, an impertinence I swiftly discouraged with a threat.

Our most faithful visitor was Maria, now Mrs. Hagan, who often dined with us, sighing over her *“Polidore”* with theatrical rapture. Even the formidable Dr. Johnson occasionally lumbered in, though his manners—snorting over his tea, contradicting everyone—tried Theo’s patience. His opinion of my tragedy was blunt: *“I’d rather hear your wife’s prattle than your decasyllables.”*

Yet upon that tragedy rested all my hopes. My funds dwindling, I staked everything on *Pocahontas*—only to face disaster when Garrick, once obsequious, now sneered at my lack of noble patrons. Even Lord Bute, whom Sampson had rashly promised, refused to attend, scandalized that an actor (Hagan) married to a nobleman’s daughter should appear on stage.

The blow fell on the eve of rehearsal. A letter arrived, crushing my last hope of royal favor. *“His lordship cannot condescend to witness a play tainted by such associations.”*

That evening, Theo had prepared a tender supper, unaware of the ruin looming over us. I forced a smile, blaming fatigue for my pallor—how could I tell her that our future now hung by the thinnest of threads?

The English public, less familiar with the tale of Pocahontas than we Virginians—who still cherish the memory of that gentle and noble-hearted princess—found themselves introduced to her legend through a modest ballad penned by Mr. Warrington. This little verse, printed in the magazines shortly before the debut of his tragedy, was, in the eyes of Parson Sampson and myself, a stroke of cunning strategy.

"Ground-bait, my dear sir!" exclaimed the enthusiastic clergyman. "Mark my words—the fish will rise in shoals on the great night!"

Indeed, whispers circulated through the coffee-houses that the lines had been composed by none other than Mr. Mason, Mr. Cowper of the Temple, or even the illustrious Mr. Gray. I suspect our good Sampson himself planted these rumors, for had Shakespeare been named the author, he would have proclaimed *Pocahontas* the poet’s crowning glory.

My own acquaintance with the valiant Captain Smith began in childhood, seated at my grandfather’s knee in Virginia, poring over the chronicles of the hero’s exploits. His travels, his sufferings, his narrow escapes—both in the wilds of America and the battlefields of Europe—filled my young mind with wonder. Even now, as I take up that well-worn volume here in England, the memories return fresh as yesterday. My grandfather would sketch scenes of Smith’s duels—whether facing Turkish champions on the Danube or standing defiant before Powhatan’s warriors.

Ah, what a spectacle was that legendary combat with the three Turkish champions! The final foe, Bonny Molgro, loomed large in my imagination—his monstrous turban, his gleaming scimitar, his ferocious whiskers! After dispatching the first two, Smith and Molgro clashed with battle-axes, each blow staggering the other, until the Christian, disarmed and wounded, drew his falchion and struck the fatal blow. For his valor, Duke Segismundus granted him a shield bearing three Turks’ heads and a pension of three hundred ducats.

In my tragedy, I took liberties with time and place, transplanting Smith’s heroics to the banks of the Potomac and James River. The ballad that served as our "ground-bait" ran thus:

The tragedy itself need not be recounted here—my children may peruse it at their leisure. Suffice it to say, it wove together the threads of history and romance: an Indian king, a devoted princess, a treacherous Englishman, a noble savage torn by love, and a scheming medicine-man bent on Smith’s destruction. The war-cries, the dances, the arrival of the English fleet—all were crafted to stir patriotic fervor, particularly in light of Britain’s recent triumphs in Canada.

Yet ill omens gathered like storm clouds. Petty jealousies, managerial timidity, and a cabal in high places conspired against us. The house, Sampson swore, teemed with planted detractors. Even so, Hagan’s performance as Smith was masterful—so much so, he later confessed, that a certain eminent actor might well have envied him.

Alas, history’s fidelity proved our undoing. The audience tittered at Pocahontas’s authentic garb (though I ask, should Caractacus have been painted blue?). Then, in the play’s most poignant moment—when Pocahontas flung herself before Smith to save him—a wag in the pit cried, "Bedad! Here’s the Belle Savage kissing the Saracen’s Head!" Laughter erupted, and the curtain fell to hisses.

I bore the defeat with composure. What captain rages against the tempest that sinks his ship? Yet at home, my Theo’s pale face struck deeper than any critic’s barb. Our little supper—meant to celebrate—was instead a quiet communion of shared sorrow. Even the verses she later slipped into my study, though not the loftiest poetry, moved me more than all the applause in the world:

Her blushing smile as I read them was victory enough.

As an older man now, looking back upon the impulsive leap into matrimony that I persuaded my dear Theo to take with me when we were scarcely more than children, I ought—by all respectable logic—to sit here in my comfortable chair, shaking my head in grave disapproval. As a father, mindful of the necessity of mutton chops and the urgency of settling the baker’s bill, I should wag a solemn finger at reckless youth and offer myself as a cautionary tale.

Yet truth compels me to confess that our marriage, so loudly lamented by prudent relatives, defied every gloomy prophecy. It has known trials, certainly—days of hardship borne with a patience that astonished our comfortably horrified kin; moments of grief softened, in time, by Heaven’s mercy; and, above all, blessings so profound I scarcely dare whisper them to the page, save in gratitude to the One who bestowed them.

To marry without a fortune is, by the code of society, a sin—a folly punishable by debt, drudgery, and the slow erosion of hope. And yet, do not thousands make that leap yearly, trusting only in Providence and their own hands? If young hearts waited for fully furnished cottages, stocked larders, and gilded security, the world itself would cease its turning. The gentlefolk linger at the brink, afraid to wet their silken slippers; the poor plunge in barefoot and battle the current.

Who would willingly consign a daughter to poverty? Who would counsel a son to drag a beloved girl from comfort into the grinding shadow of *res angusta domi*—the narrow means of home? I look at my wife and ask forgiveness for leading her into such straits. Yet I think, too, of the love that never faltered, of the strength God granted her to endure, and of the unexpected graces hidden in hardship—the kindness of friends, the sweetness of small joys, the dignity of labor. The prosperous, peering into our modest world, often marveled at our contentment. What seemed privation to them was, to us, life simply lived.

My father-in-law, the good General Lambert, had wed his Molly when he was but a penniless subaltern. They had known lean years and fat, and perhaps (though my Theo will never admit it) had even dispensed with parental permission in their youth. At any rate, they bore no grudge against matrimony and viewed our own venture with a soldier’s calm. When I confessed my finances to the General, I may have painted them with undue optimism; believing me master of a modest sum, he sailed for Jamaica with an easy mind. Theo’s dowry consisted of a few trinkets, some lace from her aunt, and twenty guineas in a purse stitched by her mother and sisters.

As for my own resources—I had counted among them a debt my mother steadfastly refused to acknowledge. The sums I had spent on the Virginia estate, she argued, were but an advance on my inheritance. Her spare coin was for Harry, my younger brother, who had entered His Majesty’s service with empty pockets and a belief (carefully nurtured by Madam Esmond) that he was the rightful heir. When news of my marriage reached her, her letters turned glacial. Harry, visiting home after the war in Canada, described her lavish entertainments in his honor—and the bitter clashes over his friendship with Colonel Washington, whom she detested.

Meanwhile, hints in Harry’s letters suggested another reason for his prolonged stay in Virginia: a certain young lady who had eclipsed even our dear Hester in his affections. When he finally confessed it—veiled in rhapsodies about beauty and virtue—I handed the letter to Theo with a heavy heart.

“I have feared this,” I said. “Poor Hetty!”

“Ah, poor Hetty,” murmured Theo, studying the carpet.

“It could never have been,” I sighed.

“No,” she agreed softly. “They would not have suited.”

Puzzled by her composure, I pressed her—until, with a blush, she confessed an old schoolgirl fancy for a mercer’s apprentice, now a bandy-legged fellow with red hair. The discovery amused rather than wounded me. How little we truly know even those we love best!

Our own means dwindled steadily. My tragedy, though praised by critics and even Mr. Kemble, earned me nothing but a kind offer from Lord Wrotham to publish it by subscription—a charity I refused. We retreated from Bloomsbury to humbler lodgings in Lambeth, still attended by loyal Gumbo and Molly. Relatives clucked at our "extravagance," though none offered aid.

Lady Castlewood, now resplendent in wealth, visited us with condescending cheer. “Poverty’s no shame, cousin,” she declared, surveying our cramped quarters. “Why, in Albany, I scrubbed floors and milked the cow!” She invited us to dine—on days when no dukes or bishops would be present. “Fine company don’t suit you now,” she explained kindly. Her patronage extended to advising Hagan, the actor who had married her sister, to take orders and seek a colonial chaplaincy—a plan later realized, though not through her purse.

My uncle’s family, meanwhile, maintained a pious distance. They spoke of me as a Grub Street wretch, my tragedy as a moral failure, and my wife’s plight as divine justice. Their charity, like their prayers, was for public display.

In desperation, I turned to Dr. Johnson, who received me with gruff kindness. He urged perseverance, suggested literary work, and—when I confessed my dwindling funds—cheered me with the thought that youth and a few hundred pounds were fortune enough. “Why, sir,” he said, “Goldsmith would buy a coach-and-six on less!”

Yet the booksellers, once flattered by my visits in borrowed finery, grew cold when payment was mentioned. One, an ex-footman, dismissed me with a sneer—until I retorted in kind. Later, in better days, I made my peace with him.

And so the year wore on, with hope as thin as our purse. But Heaven, as ever, writes its own endings.

The birth of our little Miles came but a few days after the arrival of another prince into this world—one greeted with cannon fire and grand illuminations, with crowds pressing to glimpse his gilded cradle under the watch of noble attendants. No such fanfare heralded the arrival of our son. No courtiers bowed, no loyal subjects paid homage—only dear Gumbo and faithful Molly, whose devotion to the heir of our humble fortunes was as true as any king could wish.

Why, you may ask, was he not christened George, like his father and that other illustrious babe? The name Miles belonged to a wayward scamp of our family, borne by generations of Warringtons before him. Yet it was my poor little cousin Miles, that solitary kind soul among my kin, who had shown me affection when others withheld it. And so Theo and I agreed—our boy would carry his name.

We dutifully announced his birth in the *Daily Advertiser*, noting the humble address in Church Street, Lambeth, where he first drew breath. Aunt Bernstein, in her reply, chided me for advertising our modest circumstances to the world. "My dear," she wrote, "must you inform all of London that you are buried in such a place?" Still, she sent her kisses to Theo and a gift for the child—a silk coverlet trimmed with lace fit for a prince. Practical it was not, for the lace alone might have fed us for weeks, but Theo and Molly admired it greatly, and so our boy’s cradle was as fine as any lord’s.

Dr. Heberden, ever kind, visited often to see that Theo fared well, and he recommended a capable surgeon nearby. Yet my wife needed little tending beyond what our good landlady and Molly could provide. Then came the gilded spectacle of Lady Castlewood’s carriage rolling up to our gate, bearing a pot of jelly—likely some remnant of her ladyship’s latest banquet. She spoke grandly of the royal celebrations while depositing her offering with gracious condescension. Mr. Johnson, who happened to call that day, observed the scene with keen interest. His bows were deep, if inelegant, as she addressed me as "cousin" and transferred the jelly from silver to crockery with regal benevolence.

"Ah," said the Doctor afterward, savoring a spoonful, "the great are fortunate indeed. Their tables host not only the finest delicacies but the brightest wits. This jelly, sir, may have graced the lips of celebrated beauties, shared the company of tarts and creams most exquisite." Yet Theo, after a taste, set it aside, and Molly declared it had turned.

Our boy had, at least, an earl’s daughter for a godmother—poor Lady Maria, whose own hopes of motherhood had been lost. She doted on the child with a tenderness near heartbreaking to witness. Captain Miles may now strut in the splendid regimentals of the Prince’s Hussars, but let it be remembered that his first swaddling clothes were those meant for a player’s child.

Sampson christened him in the same Southwark chapel where Theo and I had wed. Never were the Prayer Book’s words read more movingly—though the parson’s voice faltered at the end, and there was not a dry eye among us. "Mr. Garrick himself," Hagan declared, "could not have spoken those lines with greater feeling. No child was ever welcomed with purer love."

Now, how our Miles came by his name—that remains to be told. Days before the christening, when we had yet resolved to call him George, the clatter of small hooves sounded at our gate. There stood young Miles, our cousin, breathless from his ride. "Cousin Harry gave me this pony," he panted, "and I like you because you’re like him—and because they say unkind things of you at home, and it isn’t fair." He thrust forth a coral whistle and a gold moidore. "This was my godmamma’s gift, but your baby may have it. And if you’re poor, here’s my moidore—I’m not allowed to spend it anyway."

We led him upstairs—his little boots clomping proudly—to where Theo lay. She kissed him, and his coin never left her purse.

When my mother, in due time, learned of the name Miles—one unknown among the Esmonds—she expressed royal displeasure. I did not explain then. But years later, when I told her the story, a tear traced the lines of her cheek. And I heard afterward that she questioned Gumbo often about the boy who had given his name to our Miles—our *Miles Gloriosus* of Pall Mall, Valenciennes, and Brighton.

In the days of our youth, when Harry and I vexed our tutor with boyish mischief, who could have imagined that I, Esmond Warrington of Virginia, would one day find myself a humble guide to a young charge of my own? My mother, upon our reconciliation, could scarcely bear to speak of those years—referring to them only as "that sorrowful time, my dearest," or "those dark days when we were estranged." Though my pupil, a generous soul, sent barrels of his famed brew to Jamestown—the very liquor that built his fortune—Madam Esmond would only acknowledge him as "your English acquaintance" or "the gentleman from Lambeth," never by name, nor would she touch a drop of his ale. We brew our own at Warrington Manor, yet good Mr. Foker never fails to ship us two casks of his finest each year. His son now moves among the ton, wed to an earl’s daughter; the father remains a kind and honest man, and it was my fortune to know him, for his friendship brought me some of the most welcome gold I ever held.

Yet it was not the coin itself that mattered most, but the purpose his trust gave me. The post of governor to his son lifted me from idleness and despair. Mr. Foker’s brewery then stood near Pedlar’s Acre in Lambeth, and the surgeon who attended my wife in her lying-in also cared for the brewer’s household. A Bavarian by birth, originally named Voelker, the man had taken a liking to me—partly, I suspect, from sharing many a pipe of Virginia tobacco in my garden. Through him, Mr. Foker came to my door, and discovering I knew a smattering of German and could sing *"Prinz Eugen, der edle Ritter"* (a tune my grandfather brought home from Marlborough’s wars), the brewer warmed to me at once. His lady placed her carriage at Theo’s disposal; their little daughter doted on our infant (though the Captain, now grown into as homely a fellow as ever wore a queue, was then a cherub); and their son, young Master Foker, bullied at Westminster for his father’s trade, was given into my care—with a salary sufficient to ease our burdens.

Mr. Foker, shrewd in business, took an earnest interest in my affairs. When I laid bare my struggles—my noble kin’s paltry aid, my Lady Castlewood’s stale jellies, Lady Warrington’s slights—he laughed. But the tale of little Miles’s moidore moved him to tears. "Dey shall have all de beer dey can drink," he vowed of Sampson and Hagan. He sent his wife to call upon Lady Maria, treating her with deference, and when Hagan departed for Dublin, the brewer’s purse furnished his needs.

Upon learning the full extent of my circumstances, Mr. Foker praised me as if I were a saint. My mother had saved for Harry, and both owed me debts unpaid. Yet Harry spent freely, believing the money his by right, while Madam Esmond could never grasp the hardship she dealt me. In late ’62, Harry sent funds for his promotion, begging me to draw on his agents if need arose. He did not know how dire my straits were, nor how my small means had vanished. To take his coin would delay his rise, so I refrained—though sorely tempted—nor would I burden General Lambert, knowing his modest fortune. Such simple forbearance, Mr. Foker hailed as noble virtue.

What did the good man do but write privately to Harry, extolling me and Theo as paragons? He even called upon Madame de Bernstein—who never spoke of his visit, though her manner softened markedly afterward. When I later asked how he gained entry to the Baroness, he laughed. "De Baroness? I knew de Baron when he was a valet in Munich, and I a brewer’s boy!" Some family histories, perhaps, are best left unexplored.

Thus, a season that might have been bleak was brightened by kindness. My young charge proved docile, and guiding him beat haunting booksellers’ doors or courting fickle patrons. Many an evening Theo and I passed in cheerful company with our benefactors. Mrs. Foker and my wife sang sweetly together; I even read my *Pocahontas* to them, earning praise for my delivery.

After young Miles Warrington’s escapade, I saw no more of him, nor heard much of my paternal kin. Sir Miles clung to court like a limpet, and I laughed when Mr. Foker told me on ’Change that they meant to make my uncle "a Beer." "A Beer?" I echoed. "Can’t you hear me? A *Lort!*" he snapped. Sir Miles bent to every minister’s whim. Had the favorite been Scotch, he’d have mimicked the accent. Once, spotting him and his lady leaving court after Mrs. Claypool’s presentation, I stood with my boy upon my shoulder. They stared past me from their gilded coach, footmen blank as statues. Had I worn a fairy’s cloak of invisibility, they could not have ignored me more.

We seldom availed ourselves of Lady Castlewood’s odd invitations to tea *tête-à-tête*. Old Van den Bosch, sharp as he was in trade, made dull company, save for his daughter, who prattled of bulls, bears, and farm-stock as keenly as any broker. My Lord Castlewood avoided home, where his wife ruled and his father-in-law held the purse-strings. "Pa and my lord don’t pull together," the Countess told Theo. "My lord’s always wanting money, and Pa keeps the key. Right, too! Give him the run of it, and our noble family’d starve. We pay cooks, tailors, all—except gaming debts; those we won’t stomach. And we see he has a guinea in his pocket, so he cuts a figure. What debts he had before we came—mercy knows! We do our best to make him respectable, but it’s uphill work."

Theo gasped. "Surely you don’t fear—"

"Fear what? Oh, nothing! But I’d no more trust him with the plate than the cat with the cream. A coronet’s fine, but I’ve paid dear for mine!"

And so had my lord, selling his freedom for a cleared estate and full stables. He was shackled to that little wife and her father. No wonder he fled his own house. To lead a bear, as I did, was no gladsome trade—yet better than Castlewood’s degradation. At least I earned my keep, and no man could accuse me of fawning.

But my true solace was my wife, whose grace in poverty disarmed even Fortune’s harshness. However scant our means, all who knew her felt her a lady born. Landladies adored her; tradesmen jumped to serve her as if she were a duchess. Mrs. Foker once quipped as they rode out, "You look the mistress, and I the maid!"

The Fokers often lent us their chariot. Once, we ventured to the *Protestant Hero* for syllabub, and the hostess insisted on calling Theo "my lady" all afternoon. We visited Dr. Johnson, too, taking tea with him and Goldsmith. But most often, we called upon Aunt Bernstein, who developed a surprising fondness for Theo—so much so that she grudged the child and me for keeping her away. Her notes poured in like love-letters; her servants haunted our kitchen. If Theo delayed a visit, the Baroness scolded me fiercely. Once, when the baby ailed (though it proved a trifle), she came three days running, declaring us cruel to feign his sickness.

The Countess of Castlewood showed no such deference. "What’s all this scraping to that old woman?" she’d cry. "Fine lady? Fiddle! Take off her wig and paint, and what’s left? A dried-up crone!" And truth be told, the Baroness *was* a wreck—rouged, wigged, and clinging to life. "Why is beauty so brief?" she lamented once. "Mademoiselle de l’Enclos kept hers to a great age. I’m but seventy-six—yet look at me!"

Theo gently reminded her that all lives are fleeting.

"Ha! Adam lived a thousand years, and Eve stayed fair. What have we done to be cut so short?"

"Would you wish to live forever, without love?" Theo asked. "If my George were taken, I’d pray to follow."

The Baroness shuddered. "Who loves me in Heaven? I am alone—that’s why I cling here. You’re kind, child, bless you! My servants bear my temper, and I still have my cards—thank God for that!"

Yet in the Countess’s presence, her fire dimmed. The young lady would bustle in, bawling as if to the deaf: "Ezzy, tell grandaunt you’re a good boy!"

"If he is, he don’t take after his father," the Countess retorted. She chose to think the Baroness heard nothing, though the old lady trembled in her company.

Sampson, ever faithful, noted the Baroness’s decline. "She will not prepare for the next world," he mourned. "I have tried to speak of salvation, but she will have none of it. ‘Parson,’ says she, ‘keep sermons for Sundays, and deal the cards.’"

Yet to Theo, she confessed: "His theology! As if I, a Bishop’s widow, needed *his* instruction!"

Thus passed our days—threadbare, yet rich in small kindnesses. Once, Sampson left six guineas in the baby’s hand—a gift more precious than a kingdom. Such lights pierce the darkest times, and love, indeed, is best of all.

The shadow of want no longer darkened my door. The passing of my young kinsman altered my fortunes irrevocably, placing me next in line to a considerable estate. My uncle and his lady, now advanced in years, were unlikely to produce further heirs. "Mark my words," Sampson muttered darkly, "that woman would slit your throat with a psalm on her lips if it served her." Yet in truth, Lady Warrington bore me no such malice. Grief had chastened her proud spirit. The clergymen who flocked to her mourning chambers preached fiery sermons on divine chastisement. "Your heart clung too fiercely to earthly vanities," they thundered. "You sought a coronet for your son—what is gilt and velvet compared to the crown of righteousness?"

A year after the tragedy, she summoned me to London. I found her swathed in black crepe, her Bible open upon the table. She lectured me at length on my moral failings—my scandalous associations with playhouses and poets, my neglect of sacred duties. When I took my leave, she pressed upon me a bundle of sermons for my wife and a hymnal by Miss Dora, who later married a celebrated preacher. The dowager forgave me, but she could never endure the sight of our living child.

My uncle, however, doted on the boy. He would sit for hours watching him play, bringing toys and sweetmeats, begging to be called "Godpapa." In his eyes, I saw my own grief reflected—the raw wound of a lost child that never truly heals. My wife understood this sorrow before I did; her compassion bridged the rift between us. When we buried our second babe, my uncle sent his carriage to follow the small coffin. Afterwards, he took little Miles for an airing, the child chattering obliviously in his new black clothes. How cruelly his innocent prattle pierced us! Even now, decades later, I catch my wife gazing into the fire, communing with that tiny soul in paradise.

We returned to Bloomsbury, where my growing bear of a son—now too sturdy for leading-strings—was packed off to the Charterhouse under Charley's watchful eye. The boy thrived there, boasting that his own sons would follow him to that venerable institution.

Freed from nursemaid duties, I turned to loftier pursuits. My uncle, ever the obsequious courtier, secured me a post licensing hackney-coaches—a sinecure I filled with dignity until political winds shifted. I took my degree at the Temple, donned the barrister's wig, and even accompanied my benefactor Foker to Paris, where the gallant Florac introduced me to Versailles' glittering circles. Our ladies, meanwhile, amused themselves at Wimbledon with my "Parisian letters," forwarded through the obliging Mr. Hume of the Embassy.

Yet even as I enjoyed this tranquil existence, storm clouds gathered across the Atlantic. When Grenville proposed his stamp duties, I warned my wife how fiercely our colonial kin would resent taxation. But none could foresee the hurricane of rebellion that followed. Was it the paltry tax that sparked the fire? No—it was the smoldering resentment of years, the slights endured, the ancient grudges nursed. Had I remained in Virginia, I might have joined the revolt myself, if only to spite my domineering mother.

Then came Hal's fiery letters from Virginia, brimming with patriotic fervor. My dear brother was ever a weathervane, turning to every persuasive wind. "He rebels against Madam Esmond's yoke," I observed. "Or perhaps some local enchantress guides him," Theo suggested slyly. Hal's missives overflowed with devotion—to his new bride, to me, even as he unknowingly profited from our mother's harsh economy. Only later did he learn how she had beggared me to enrich him, a revelation that sparked furious quarrels.

Among his papers, I found the letter announcing his betrothal—a document my sentimental wife preserved, though it pained her. "To think whom he might have married!" she sighed, thinking of poor rejected Hetty. But Hal's heart had been captured by Fanny Mountain, once a shy girl, now blossomed into beauty. Their courtship unfolded like a comedy—clandestine sighs, intercepted letters, and the inevitable explosion when Madam Esmond discovered them.

"An ungrateful, artful, false little wretch!" she stormed when she found Fanny in tears. "What would Mr. Lintot say to your flirtations?" (The apothecary, it seemed, had been her preferred suitor.) Hal, ever the hothead, swore he'd brain any man who insulted Fanny's honor—including his own mother. The scene escalated until Madam, clutching her tortoiseshell stick like a scepter, lamented her divine punishment: "I favored Jacob over Esau, and now my pride is broken!"

Thus did Harry Warrington, once the pampered younger son, bow to the common lot—learning humility through loss, finding love despite opposition, and stumbling toward his destiny, one imperfect step at a time.

When my mother learned of my acceptance of a modest position at home, her displeasure was thinly veiled. She had, perhaps, withdrawn her support in hopes of driving me back to Virginia—back to dependence beneath her roof. Yet we never spoke openly of the breach between us. She severed my allowances; I did not protest but found means to live without them. Only through Harry’s private letters did I ever hear that she might have regretted her severity. In later years, when we met again, her manner softened—gentleness toward my wife, a quiet deference—yet no words of apology ever passed her lips, nor did I demand them.

Pride is a curious thing. Was hers greater than mine? I could have spoken, could have bridged the silence with a word, yet I chose to let the wound fester. When I err, I confess it—not for virtue’s sake, but to soothe my own vanity. To beg forgiveness is to humble the self, and I would sooner swallow bitterness than admit fault. There are souls who forgive as easily as they breathe; I merely learned to endure.

Thus, we remained estranged, exchanging only the barest civilities—announcements of births, formalities—until the political storms of the colonies reached even my quiet life in London. Though I had sided with the Crown in the Stamp Act disputes, a rash letter I penned, criticizing the Crown’s heavy hand, cost me my post. My uncle, ever a creature of convention, regarded me with horrified pity, as though I were a wayward sinner beyond redemption. Yet Theo and our child remained dear to him, and he could not wholly sever ties, though he shook his head over my ruin.

Fate, however, is a fickle tide. The death of the Duke of Cumberland—an event of no consequence to me—proved the turning point. My uncle, ever eager to attend royal obsequies, caught a fatal chill at the funeral. Before I could grasp the news, a lawyer stood at my door, addressing me as *Sir George Warrington*.

The title came with lands, tenants, and obligations. We buried my uncle beside his little son in the family vault, where generations of Warringtons lay. The dowager and her daughter retreated to Bath, leaving me master of an estate I had never expected to inherit.

Prosperity, I soon learned, is not the same as contentment. Tenants bowed, the great coach rolled, yet I missed the simplicity of our Lambeth cottage. In London, we revisited old haunts—Theo embracing former landladies, I smiling at memories of leaner, livelier days. Friends from those times—Hagan, Sampson—came to us, though Dr. Johnson, alas, found our quiet house dull.

And I? I grew restless. The hunt, the endless talk of foxes and horses, the dreary sermons—it all wearied me. My wife, ever patient, masked her worry, but I saw it. Even in Eden, Adam must have sighed, *Is this all?*

Here, three pages of my journal are lost—torn out, perhaps in some late-night fit of melancholy. No matter. What remains is the truth: no man confesses everything, not even to the woman who shares his life. Theo knew my moods, my silences, yet she never pressed. She forgave my sons their boyish sins, shielded them from my temper, and played the diplomat when my pride clashed with the world’s expectations.

Money freed me from want but not from unease. The county deemed me aloof; my refusal to bribe or bully my way into Parliament marked me as a man of "dangerous opinions." Meanwhile, Harry, across the ocean, thrived—his letters full of cheer, his plantation flourishing, his loyalty to the colonial cause unshaken. *He* should have been the squire, not I. He would have charmed the neighborhood, ridden to hounds, toasted with the gentry. I merely endured it.

And my mother? Her letters, once icy, now carried grudging news of Harry—his reliance on his wife and mother-in-law, his frugality (negroes without shoes at a governor’s ball!), her thinly veiled scorn for his choices. Yet she wrote, and Theo, ever the peacemaker, wove threads of reconciliation where I would have left the fabric torn.

So passed the years—crowned with comfort, yet shadowed by a quiet disquiet. For what is a harbor, if not also a cage?

Our mother’s plea would have softened far sterner hearts than ours, and we spoke eagerly of a swift return to Virginia, of booking the finest cabins aboard the *Young Rachel*. But fate intervened—our youngest fell ill, making the voyage perilous, and Theo would not leave the child’s side. So the *Young Rachel* sailed without us that year. The next, my own health faltered, gout seizing me with its iron grip—an affliction that, once passed, left my mind sharper, my spirits lighter. Then came darker tidings from Jamaica, where our dear General, returning from his post, brought news that struck deeper than any worldly loss.

Theo’s mother—the tenderest of friends, save one—had succumbed to fever abroad. Her last regret was that she would not live to see our family flourish.

*“She sees us, though we see her not,”* the General murmured, his voice thick with grief. *“And she thanks you, George, for your kindness to her children.”*

His own life, we feared, would not long outlast hers. She had been his sun; without her, the world was twilight. It pained us to watch him, so diminished, sitting quietly among us. Theo, in her gestures, her very voice, often recalled her mother to him, and little Hetty became his solace. She had refused advantageous matches in Jamaica, the General told us, and later, in England, even the heir to Lord Wrotham’s title knelt before her. But Hetty would not leave her father.

*“As long as he needs me,”* she said, *“I ask for no husband.”*

We jested that the General might divide his time between our Manor and Oakhurst, but Hetty shook her head—Oakhurst held too many ghosts now. She would wed no man for title or wealth. The General, when pressed, confessed that some early disappointment had sealed her resolve, though she never spoke of it. We respected her silence.

My brother Lambert, now a college clergyman near Winchester, had taken a wife—a woman whose presence we endured more than enjoyed. She was free with her opinions, especially regarding Hetty’s unmarried state.

*“A sister is best kept from a husband’s sight,”* she declared one evening, fixing Theo with a knowing look. *“I’ve warned my own sister the same.”*

The General’s brows rose. *“Am I to understand, madam, that you have cautioned Lady Warrington against her own sister?”*

The woman flushed, indignant. *“I only speak from duty! A man is a man, saint or not!”*

*“And your own sister’s husband,”* the General mused, *“did he find *you* a temptation?”*

She gasped, outraged, and fled in tears.

*“Your mother was spared this creature,”* the General sighed later.

*“Mother would have improved her,”* Theo replied, kissing his cheek.

But blood does not always endear, nor neighbors amuse. Jack Lambert, sulking after his wife’s outburst, preached a dull sermon the next day, while little Miles, clad again in mourning, clung to his grandfather’s hand.

Would the General stay and oversee our estate while we journeyed to Virginia? Hetty would command the household, the butler’s keys surrendered to him—though Gumbo, now wed to Molly and father to a brood of tawny children, was to accompany us.

We traveled to London, then Bristol, where our agent fawned over our children—*“Such beauties never graced a ship!”*—and we embarked without mishap. Landing at Richmond, the past rushed back. A coach and a retinue of negroes awaited us; among them, a horseman in our livery sprang down to embrace us—Harry, dearer than ever. He rode with us to our mother’s door, where she stood on the steps, regal as ever. Theo knelt for her blessing.

Harry left us there, unwilling to intrude. *“She tolerates me,”* he admitted, *“but Fanny and she are best apart. Come to the tavern when you’re free.”*

Alone, we faced our mother. Her house, though modest, might have been a palace for the grace of her welcome. Old servants grinned; new ones peered shyly. Feasts were piled before us, and that evening, the gentry of Richmond came to pay respects. Harry appeared, but gloom hung over him. His mother addressed him as a queen might a wayward prince—cool, unyielding.

Later, in private, she found us. *“The children sleep like angels,”* she murmured, caressing imaginary curls. She spoke of the Governor’s levee, of the necessity of attending—her first appearance since Harry’s marriage. She had engaged the finest house in Richmond, her servants decked in livery, her best plate and wines brought at great cost. She even wore her wedding gown from George II’s time.

Theo, ever the peacemaker, persuaded her to receive Fanny, Hal’s wife—a reconciliation more bitter than sweet.

*“You remember these, my dear?”* Mother said, indicating silver sconces. *“Fanny often cleaned them at Castlewood.”*

Fanny’s eyes flashed, but Mother, magnanimous in victory, saw nothing.

Colonel Lee, arriving, bowed extravagantly. *“Madam, since Count Borulawski, I’ve seen no curtsey so fine!”*

*“A favorite of the Polish king,”* he replied, before turning to Harry. *“This is the captain who stood by Wolfe at Quebec! I would have shared his fate, had I been near.”*

Harry, in his old uniform, accepted the praise stiffly.

Our meetings had been many, our bond unbroken. Unlike in pursuits—he for the field, I for the page—we shared a love surpassing even the closest kinship. At Castlewood, without wives or women, we passed days in perfect harmony.

*“Brother,”* Hal confessed once, *“there’s no one like you.”*

And so, hearts full, we prepared to face the storm gathering over Virginia—loyalties divided, but our affection unwavering.

Here is the rewritten chapter, maintaining the original tone and style while refining some of the phrasing for clarity and flow:

---

Why is it that fate so often places me at odds with the world? Propose a law to hand this realm over to the Pretender of Rome or the Grand Turk, and I would surely oppose it—yet find myself outvoted all the same. In Virginia, I was no less at odds with my neighbors. The Patriots branded me a Tory, while the Tories eyed me with suspicion as a dangerous radical. The times were out of joint indeed. *O cursed spite!*

Before a year had passed, I longed for the quiet banks of the Waveney. Yet how could I abandon my mother to the brewing storm? Nor would she leave, nor would any man of spirit. Over his Excellency’s claret, we loyal gentlemen vowed to stand firm—*statue signum, signifer!*—rallying to the cause with brimming glasses and bold words. The Governor, in those moments, spoke like the bravest of Cavaliers.

Of all Virginians, none held to her principles as staunchly as Madam Esmond. When the gentry resolved to host a ball for Lady Dunmore, news arrived of the Boston Port Bill. Swiftly, the House of Burgesses denounced the act and proclaimed a day of fasting and prayer to avert civil war. Yet the ball proceeded—only for sackcloth to follow days later.

"A ball!" cried my mother. "A ball thrown by rebels who will soon insult His Majesty? I would sooner die!" She refused the invitation, only to be hailed as a patriot by cheering crowds the next day. Mistaken for a sympathizer, she thrust her head from her chair and cried, *"God save the King!"*—a cry the people echoed, as all did in those days.

On the night of the ball, Harry, as Burgess and host, wore his old red coat of Wolfe’s (soon to be traded for another hue) and escorted Madam Fanny. My wife and I, as English visitors, attended as well. I danced with the lively Mrs. Washington—a charming partner, though my mother scowled at the praise.

Three days later, when fast-day services were held, Madam Esmond dined in open defiance, lighting her windows while the town stood dark. She bade an aide-de-camp sing *"God save the King,"* then called for *"Britons, strike home!"*—a step too far. A stone crashed through the window, scattering the punch-bowl. Undaunted, she stood framed by starlight, arms outstretched, singing on until the crowd’s jeers were quelled by a familiar voice—*"You mustn’t insult a lady."*

That night, every pane in our house was shattered. *"Britons, strike home!"* had roused more than loyalty.

The colony drilled militia, cast bullets, and defied royal edicts—all while swearing fealty to the Crown. When I warned of England’s might, they scoffed: *"We mean no separation! We are loyal Britons!"* Yet the powder-keg was lit.

Had England fought with honor, not Hessian hirelings and Indian savagery, might the war have ended differently? Long Island should have crushed Washington; Camden should have secured the South. But fate decreed otherwise. The Republic rose, not by luck, but by the unyielding resolve of its leader—Washington, steadfast through hunger, treason, and defeat, magnanimous in victory, and noblest in surrender.

In our own home, division festered. Harry, once my brother in arms, grew rigid in his loyalties, while I—though no radical—saw hypocrisy in denying liberty to those we ruled. When Dunmore promised freedom to slaves who joined the King’s cause, Harry raged, while I asked, *"If we fight for liberty, why not they?"*

Gumbo, freed for his loyalty, now vexed the household with his airs. My mother, ever strict, ordered him whipped—sparking rebellion. Molly, his wife, turned on her mistress, and I took his side. The quarrel passed, but Gumbo had to go.

Theo, heavy with child, sent our little ones to England—her sacrifice made without tears. The ship returned bearing Hagan and Maria, who humbly renounced her title. Maria’s deference won my mother’s heart, and even Hagan’s readings of my *Pocahontas* met approval.

Yet Fanny, sharp-tongued, tormented Maria with sly jabs about age and rank. I pitied Maria’s quiet endurance, while Fanny, once our meek ward, now repaid old wounds with fresh ones.

*"Kindness?"* she sneered when I reproached her. *"No slave was ever treated as I was."*

And I thought of the silent girl who once served us so sweetly—now a viper in my brother’s bed.

---

This version preserves the original’s richness while smoothing some archaic phrasing and tightening the narrative flow. Let me know if you'd like any further refinements!

In the days when America still bent the knee to British rule, our colonies served as a convenient dumping ground for the mother country’s wayward sons—younger brothers who had squandered their inheritances, scoundrels fleeing debtors’ prison, and black sheep too scandalous to remain at home. It was a tradition as old as colonization itself: export the troublesome and let the wilderness reform them—or bury them.

I confess I played my own small part in this traffic when I secured passage for the Reverend Hagan, husband to my cousin Maria, shipping him off to Virginia when no English pulpit would have him. He proved an honest man, a loyal subject, and a credit to my patronage—though had he sought a living in my own parish, I doubt I would have been so generous. There is a hypocrisy in charity at a distance, a quiet relief in sending one’s mistakes across the ocean. Maria, for all her past follies, had shown me kindness when I needed it most, yet I—and others of my name—still looked down upon her. Shame on us for that pride.

But Maria was not the only family outcast dispatched to the colonies. My Lord Castlewood’s younger brother, the Honorable William Esmond, had worn out his welcome in England through debts, duels, and deceit. New York became his reluctant refuge, and there he thrived—not in virtue, but in fresh mischief. No sooner had rebellion begun to simmer than William emerged as one of the loudest Loyalists in the province, raising a company of volunteers and swearing to lay down his life for King and Country. A fine sentiment—were it not from a man who had spent a lifetime evading both honor and creditors.

Meanwhile, in Virginia, the political air grew thick with discontent. The Assembly, dissolved by our prickly Governor, now convened in taverns, plotting rebellion over claret and tobacco smoke. I split my days between Richmond and the Governor’s table, where we still toasted the King even as the ground shifted beneath us. The gentry, though furious at Parliament’s heavy hand, remained civil—for now.

Then came the day I rode out to visit Hal at his new plantation. Finding him absent, I followed the groom’s direction toward Willis’s Ordinary—and stumbled upon a scene that stripped all pretense away. There stood my brother, drilling a motley band of farmers armed with hunting rifles and scythes. At his command, they presented arms—awkwardly, but with a grim determination.

*"Captain Cade, this is my brother,"* Hal announced, his face flushing.

*"Welcome, if he stands with Virginia,"* replied Cade, a hard-eyed man with a militia-man’s grip.

*"I stand with my family,"* I said carefully. The men cheered, but my stomach tightened. This was no idle drill. Rebellion had moved from pamphlets to powder.

Hal dismissed the men, refusing their offer of a drink, and we rode home in silence.

*"So the cat’s out of the bag,"* I said at last.

*"And a damned fierce cat it is,"* he replied. *"Don’t tell Mother."*

I tried reason: *"You mean to fight the finest army in the world with farmers?"*

*"We’ll likely get whipped,"* Hal admitted, grinning. *"But we’re young. A thrashing might do us good."* He nudged his old hound with his boot—a gesture so careless it chilled me.

There was no swaying him. To Hal, this was no longer about taxes or treaties; it was about home. *"You argue as an Englishman,"* he said. *"I fight as a Virginian."*

That night, over dinner, Fanny arched a brow when I mentioned Hal’s *"sport."* My wife assumed he’d been hunting. I let her think it. Some truths, once spoken, cannot be taken back—and the storm ahead would break soon enough.

Upon concluding my visit to my brother, and seeing my wife and child safely returned to Richmond, I rode to Williamsburg to confer with the Governor on the rising unrest spreading through the colonies. Delegates had been chosen to attend the Continental Congress in Philadelphia, while Massachusetts simmered with rebellion. Though Maryland and Pennsylvania still professed loyalty, and the Carolinas and Georgia held firm for the Crown, Virginia’s ancient allegiance was now in question.

The Governor’s circle brimmed with fervent Loyalists, eager to crush what they called treason. My brother Harry’s name was often spat in contempt—blamed for his radical leanings, influenced, they claimed, by his wife and his friend at Mount Vernon. Yet I knew Harry, like many, sought reconciliation before revolt. He was no traitor, only a man pushed to desperation.

We drank the King’s health with fervor, swore to uphold his rights, and dreamed of a royal prince ruling America. Some even suggested offering Harry a command to lure him back to loyalty. I wrote to him, though with little hope, repeating arguments we had worn thin in past debates. Our mother, ever zealous, assailed him with Scripture and threats, only to receive a cool, well-reasoned rebuttal—likely penned with the help of his Whig-leaning clergyman.

When I rode to Fanny’s Mount, I found only my sister-in-law. Harry had gone west, she said, to inspect horses—a flimsy excuse. We both knew the truth. War was coming.

“If fate deals kindly,” I told her, “our mother’s estate shall pass to Harry, for I’ve land enough in England.” Her eyes brightened at that, though she feigned fairness. “Give my love to Theo,” she said, and with that, I left.

The Governor and I agreed the time had come to arm. I was named Colonel of the Westmoreland Defenders—a grand title for a phantom regiment. Hagan, ever loyal, became our chaplain; my mother stitched our colors; Dempster, my old tutor, scoured the Carolinas for recruits. But men were scarce. Lexington had ignited the colonies. Farmers now stood against British regulars, and Breed’s Hill became a rallying cry.

Then came the Governor’s betrayal. He fled by night to a warship, abandoning his post, leaving us to face the chaos. Worse, he armed runaway slaves and incited the tribes—acts of cowardice that turned even loyalists against him. When I confronted him aboard his ship, he raged, called me a rebel, and threatened arrest. I demanded a court-martial; the officers present knew the truth of his shame.

Somehow, my mother’s estate remained untouched, though rebel troops often quartered there. Fanny, returned from the northern campaign, softened toward her, and whispers suggested her influence kept the Whigs at bay. Perhaps. Or perhaps even rebels saw no gain in harassing an old woman while her son fought for their cause.

As for me, my command dissolved like mist. Some called me a traitor for speaking plainly; others, a schemer clinging to land. Let them talk. I knew my conscience. And if history misjudges me—well, I am in good company.

What use was a colonel without a regiment? The Governor and Council, who had so grandly bestowed my commission, had since fled to their ships, engaging in occasional raids along the coast. Lord Dunmore’s alliance with armed freedmen had terrified more than it emboldened; worse still, rumors swirled that he had invited Southern tribes to descend upon us with tomahawk and fire. "And these are our allies?" I said to my mother, exchanging grim glances. The memory of that savage on Braddock’s Field—his knife at my throat—flashed before me.

We fortified Castlewood as best we could, though our true fear lay not in rebel forces but in the specter of frontier violence. My mother, ever steadfast, faltered only once—when shadows moved beyond our gates at night. Convinced they were Indians, she sank to her knees, whispering, "Lord have mercy! The savages—the savages!"

Dunmore’s black recruits scattered to plunder or the sea, but the promised tribal warriors never came. Yet the dread lingered. I watched my mother’s silver hair, my wife’s dark curls, our child’s golden locks, and shuddered at what war might bring. And it was we who had called such horrors upon ourselves! Providence, though inscrutable, seemed to mete justice: the mercenaries we hired faltered in battle, emboldening the rebels, while the murder of a single woman by drunken Indians turned more hearts against the Crown than any lost engagement.

Once the panic faded, my mother’s resolve returned—and with it, insistence that I leave. "What harm can come to an old woman?" she argued. "If rebels come, I’ll hide behind Fanny’s skirts. But you—you must take your family north, to safety." Her words were part sense, part love; she would not abandon Virginia, the land of her father’s grave, though she had already bidden farewell to the new generation.

Before we could depart for Boston, news arrived: the British had quit the city entirely. For months, Washington’s forces lay vulnerable at Cambridge—yet Howe hesitated until Dorchester Heights loomed over the harbor, forcing a humiliating retreat. Victory at Bunker’s Hill was undeniable, yet timidity plagued our commanders—Long Island, Valley Forge—each a missed chance to crush a faltering foe.

Loyalists now treaded carefully. Hagan, our steadfast chaplain, found himself barred from his own pulpit by rebel sentinels. Dragged before Patrick Henry, he sputtered defiance—only to be dismissed with a jest about Shakespearean kings. The new regime flattered Virginians with high offices, peeling away moderates who might have stood firm. Yet in secret, loyal hearts still whispered, passing messages through servants, biding time.

When word came of a southern expedition, I saw my chance. Under guise of visits, my wife, child, and I slipped southward, joining ex-Governor Martin’s forces in Wilmington—only to meet Admiral Parker’s fleet retreating from Charleston.
