Clinton, grim-faced at my "regiment" of women and infants, relented when Captain Horner of the *Sphinx* recognized my wife as Lambert’s daughter. So we became refugees, like Dunmore, folding our colors quietly.

The assault on Sullivan’s Fort was a disaster. Clinton led bravely, waist-deep in futile charges, while Lee’s defenders rained fire. A well-aimed shot shattered a nearby boat, and I—foolishly—cried, "Bravo!" Clinton’s glare was icy. "Pleased, are you?" "Pleased that my countrymen fight well," I retorted. We retreated, battered.

Yet in defeat, Clinton’s courage shone. Whether at Breed’s Hill or here, he led from the front. As we limped north, news came: the Declaration had been signed. Virginia proclaimed all men free—Gumbo included—and denounced hereditary rule. I laughed bitterly. How would Madam Esmond receive such heresy?

By August, we faced Washington at New York. The battle was inevitable—and cruel, for Harry’s regiment stood among the rebels. The Continentals, trapped after their rout, should have been destroyed. Yet Howe let them slip away in darkness, unopposed. A German picket ignored warnings; by dawn, the enemy had vanished.

My outrage was no secret. "Amateur!" Howe scoffed when confronted. Yet many murmured as I did. His amiability spared him blame, but history would not be so kind. The empire slipped through his fingers—while men like Washington, enduring hunger and treachery, forged a nation from the wreck.

War, it seems to me—though I defer to those who have worn the uniform—was forever altered by the bloody lesson of Breed’s Hill. The slaughter our ranks endured behind those rebel barricades left even the boldest of our generals wary. From that day forward, a row of blankets might as well have been a fortress wall for the hesitation it inspired. At White Plains, when Washington’s ragged army again lay within our grasp, they halted before a breastwork of cornstalks, of all things! And then came Valley Forge—a starving, shivering host, left untouched while Howe’s army waltzed and dined in Philadelphia. The man was feted like a Roman conqueror, garlanded by ladies who might as well have been weaving his shroud. History, I suppose, will judge whether his mercy was wisdom or folly.

Yet how strange the turns of fate! Eighteen years before, we had sent Wolfe—a man who burned for victory like a furnace—to wrest Canada from France. He prowled the heights of Quebec like a wolf indeed, sleepless, relentless, until he seized his moment and died winning it. And now? Now we had Howe, fiddling while Burgoyne’s army bled out in the wilderness.

Ah, Burgoyne! His grand march from Quebec began like a parade: proclamations, trumpets, promises of swift triumph. Germans, Indians, artillery—enough to awe the colonies into submission, or so he thought. But the land did not rise for him. The Hudson did not open. And when the hour came for Clinton to strike north from New York, the winds, the tides, the very stars conspired to delay him. Always too late! That might be the epitaph of our war. Reinforcements too late, orders too late, rescue far too late for the thousands surrendered at Saratoga.

I confess, watching it unfold stirred something in me—some old fire my comfortable life in England had banked to embers. My brother Hal, content on his Virginia plantation with his dogs and his crops and his slow, sunlit days, would have scoffed at my restlessness. But then, Hal’s shoe fit him. Mine, though lined with every luxury, pinched.

Yet fate has a way of shaking a man awake. At Fort Clinton, as we stormed those rocky heights, a musket butt nearly dashed my brains out—until a blade flashed, and Joe Blake saved my life. (A debt repaid with a parish living, though Joe still grumbles that he’d rather have a commission.) That night, as the rebel ships burned on the river, lighting the sky like funeral pyres, I felt the strange exhilaration of war. Foolish, perhaps. But the heart beats louder when death whispers in your ear.

Then came the letter. A scrap of paper, delivered under flag of truce, that froze my blood: *Colonel H. Warrington, of the Virginia line, hopes that Sir George Warrington escaped unhurt...* My brother. My own flesh, there across the lines. That night, under a single blanket in a drafty hut, we spoke of home, of Mother—still a staunch Tory, though Hal swore she was safe—and of his Fanny, whom he praised as if she were Venus and Minerva combined. And I told him of my decision: to hang up my sword, to go home. His embrace was answer enough.

We parted at dawn. I carried with me the memory of his face, dimming as the boat pulled away. Would I ever see him again? In this world of strife and parting, nothing was certain.

Clinton’s victory, brilliant as it was, meant nothing in the end. Saratoga broke Burgoyne, and with him, our hopes. By the time the news reached our fleet, we were already bound for England—my wife weeping with relief, her prayers answered. Let others mourn the lost cause. I had my own to cherish.

*Satis pugnae.* Enough of battle. Let it be so.

The joy of homecoming needs little description—the mother’s embrace, the warmth of reunion, the silent gratitude whispered on her knees. Our house stood ready, wide enough to shelter us all, yet Aunt Hetty could not bear to remain. To relinquish her temporary motherhood over the elder children, whom she had nurtured for near three years, was a pain too sharp to endure. She withdrew to Bury St. Edmunds with her father, where she might spoil her eldest nephew and niece in peace.

A year later, fortune favored her. Mr. B., the Jamaica planter, passed, leaving her half his estate—a posthumous tribute to affections once spurned. How much of that inheritance has since vanished into Miles’s pockets? Commissions, uniforms, tavern debts—youth’s follies are no mystery to me. Yet Hetty declares herself content: *“I vowed never to wed unless I found a man as good as my father. I never did, Sir George. No, not half as good—and you may smoke that in your pipe.”*

When the General departed—serene, full of years—it was Theo who wept most. *“I grieve because I fear I loved him too little,”* she murmured. Hetty, ever composed, speaks of him still as though he lingers near, recounting his jests, his kindness, her voice brightening like a girl’s.

Years ago, I fancied Hal might yet hold some fragment of her heart. When he visited us, widowed and sorrowful, I hinted at her girlhood passion, half-hoping he might replace his lost Fanny. But Hal, drowned in grief, swore no woman could equal her. Our house echoed with old tunes, old memories softening every heart. He spoke endlessly of his dear Aunt Lambert—praise her husband drank like wine, for her memory never left him.

Hal, clad in the blue and yellow of an American officer—a fashion Monsieur de Lafayette had brought to favor—strutted through Versailles, presented to Their Most Christian Majesties. A French marquise nearly ensnared him, but he fled back to England, to Bury, where patient ears endured his lamentations for his “angel” wife. We, who knew the paragon, were civil but less fervent, fumbling to answer our children’s questions about Uncle Hal’s saint.

Over claret, the Generals, Parson Blake, Miles, and I refought the Revolution. *“By Jupiter,”* Blake would cry, *“you’re the Tory, Sir George the Whig! He faults our leaders; you defend them. And when I prayed for the King last Sunday, you echoed me loud enough!”*

*“With all my heart,”* Hal would retort. *“I can’t forget I wore his coat.”*

*“Ah, if Wolfe had lived!”* Lambert sighed.

*“You should hear the General speak of him,”* Hal said, rising.

*“Which General?”* I teased.

*“Mine,”* he said, glass aloft. *“His Excellency, George Washington!”*

I drank willingly; the parson did not.

Hal’s devotion to his Chief was boundless, and on one such evening, he confessed his own disgrace. The trouble began with Lafayette—that young French marquis, leapt to major-general by Congress, while seasoned officers chafed. Hal, ever blunt, mocked the appointment. *“A boy of twenty, who scarce speaks English!”* he fumed.

The General, pressed by Congress and beset by winter at Valley Forge—his men starving, outnumbered—had no patience for quarrels. Yet Hal’s words stung. *“I thought Harry Warrington would stand by me,”* Washington said coldly.

Their reconciliation came in private. *“You speak as if I favor the Marquis from whim,”* Washington said, weary. *“But if his rank buys France’s alliance, must we not bear the slight?”* His voice dropped. *“You call this humiliation? What is mine, then—to smile at traitors, to plead with Congress while my men freeze?”*

Hal, chastened, saw then the man’s true stature—greater than Wolfe, greater than ambition. *“To endure,”* I reflected later, *“to stand unbroken by intrigue, to forgo pride for the cause—that is greatness.”*

*“You might have worn the blue and buff yourself,”* Blake grumbled.

*“Scarlet suited me better,”* I replied.

*“And General Washington,”* Theo sniffed, *“never wrote tragedies.”*

Harry laughed. *“True. Hagan read one aloud—the General slept soundly.”*

*“He never liked you,”* Theo retorted. *“Which makes your praise the nobler.”*

And so, under vine and fig-tree, we rested—our wars past, our hearts, at last, at peace.
